, one has

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau,p_{\frac{t}{2}}(z,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|=\Big|\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}{\sf g}_{zx}(\tau/\sigma)\big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{z}^{\sf g}\sigma,p_{\frac{t}{2}}(z,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}d(z,x)^{|\tau|-|\sigma|}\,t^{\frac{|\sigma|}{4}}

from the bound (
2.24
) in the definition of a model. Using the bound (
2.19
) on the moments of the heat kernel, we then have

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau,\partial_{x}^{n}p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}t^{\frac{|\sigma|}{4}}\int\big|\partial_{x}^{n}p_{\frac{t}{2}}(x,z)\big|\,d(z,x)^{|\tau|-|\sigma|}dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}\,t^{\frac{|\sigma|}{4}}t^{\frac{|\tau|-|\sigma|-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}\lesssim t^{\frac{|\tau|-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}.

\rhd

We close this paragraph by the remark about the situation where all the
{\sf\Pi}\tau
are some continuous functions. Then it follows from the bound on
\big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau,p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle
, and the fact that
p_{t}(x,\cdot)
is converging to a Dirac mass at
x
, that the function
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau
satisfies
({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)=0
, for all
\tau\in T
such that
|\tau|>0
. This will be the case of the smooth (possibly renormalized)
models from Section
6
.

§
Modelled distributions and their reconstruction

Think of a
T
-valued function
\boldsymbol{f}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the data needed to associate with each spacetime point
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
the local description
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
of a possibly globally defined distribution close to
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
near each
x
. There is no reason that such a globally defined object exists if one does not impose relations between the different components of
\boldsymbol{f}
. This is what the next definition does. For a real number
\gamma\geq\beta_{0}
set

T_{<\gamma}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigoplus_{\beta<\gamma}T_{\beta},\qquad T_{<\gamma}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha<\gamma}T_{\alpha}^{+}.

Recall from (
2.14
) the meaning of the notation
\|\tau\|_{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\in A
and
\tau\in T
.

Definition 3
.

Let
{\sf g}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow G^{+}
be a function satisfying (
2.23
). Fix a regularity exponent
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. One defines the space
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
of
distributions modelled on the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
, with transition
{\sf g}
, as the space of functions
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow T_{<\gamma}
such that

\begin{split}&[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\max_{\beta<\gamma}\,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}<\infty,\\
&\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\max_{\beta<\gamma}\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}}{d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}}<\infty.\end{split}

Set
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}
. We also define the pseudo-distance between two modelled distributions
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
and
\boldsymbol{f}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g^{\prime})
defined for two distinct models models
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
and
\sf M^{\prime}=(g^{\prime},\Pi^{\prime})
, by setting

d\big(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{f}^{\prime}\big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\underset{x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\sup}\,\underset{\beta<\gamma}{\max}\,\left\{\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(x)-\boldsymbol{f}^{\prime}(x)\big\|_{\beta}+\frac{\Big\|\Big\{\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big\}-\Big\{\boldsymbol{f}^{\prime}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}^{\prime}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}^{\prime}(x)\Big\}\Big\|_{\beta}}{d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}}\right\}.

(As in the definition of models, we choose a global bound to define modelled distributions – the original definition in
[
53
, Section 2]
relies on the local bounds. Since we consider the equations on the domain
(x_{0},x^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
, there is no difference between global and local bounds on the spatial variable
x^{\prime}
. In Section
4
below, we will consider a weighted norm with respect to the time variable
x_{0}
.)

For a basis element
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}\subset T
, and an arbitrary element
h
in
T
, denote by
h_{\sigma}
its component on
\sigma
in the basis
\mathcal{B}
. For a modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}}f_{\sigma}(\cdot)\,\sigma
in
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
, and
\sigma_{0}\in\mathcal{B}
, we have

(2.29)

\begin{split}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\sigma_{0}}&=f_{\sigma_{0}}(y)-\sum_{\tau\geq\sigma_{0}}{\sf g}_{yx}(\tau/\sigma_{0})\,f_{\tau}(x)\\
&=f_{\sigma_{0}}(y)-f_{\sigma_{0}}(x)-\sum_{\tau>\sigma_{0}}{\sf g}_{yx}(\tau/\sigma_{0})\,f_{\tau}(x).\end{split}

Examples

\bullet
The archetype of a modelled distribution is given by the lift

(2.30)

\boldsymbol{f}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{f^{(n)}(x)}{n!}X^{n},

in the polynomial regularity structure of a
\gamma
-Hölder real valued
function
f
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a positive regularity exponent
\gamma
. The identities (
2.29
) become in that case the Taylor expansions

(2.31)

f^{(n)}(y)-f^{(n)}(x)-\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{1}{\ell!}f^{(n+\ell)}(x)(y-x)^{\ell}=O\Big(d(y,x)^{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\Big)

satisfied by each
f^{(n)}
. Note here that the function
f
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\gamma
-Hölder iff there exists a family
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}},|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma
of functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
f_{0}=f
and the condition (
2.31
) with
f_{n}
in the role of
f^{(n)}
. The “if” part holds because one can get
f_{n}=f^{(n)}
from (
2.31
) inductively. The “only if” part is the classical and elementary fact for the isotropic case, and can be found in Appendix
A
of
[
53
]
for anisotropic cases. So the notion of modelled distribution with values in the polynomial regularity structure captures exactly the classical notion of regularity.

\bullet
Given a basis element
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
, set

(2.32)

\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=\widehat{{\sf g}_{x}}\tau-\tau=\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma)\sigma.

It follows from identity (
2.11
) in the definition of a concrete regularity structure that
\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
takes values in
T_{<|\tau|}
.
Since
\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\circ\widehat{{\sf g}_{x}}=\widehat{{\sf g}_{y}}
by (
2.18
), it follows that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{{\sf g}_{y}}\tau-\tau)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\widehat{{\sf g}_{x}}\tau-\tau)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{{\sf g}_{y}}\tau-\tau)-(\widehat{{\sf g}_{y}}\tau-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\tau)

\displaystyle=\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\tau-\tau=\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{yx}(\tau/\sigma)\sigma.

The size estimate
\big|{\sf g}_{yx}(\tau/\sigma)\big|\lesssim d(y,x)^{|\tau|-|\sigma|}
required from the
\sf g
-component of a model, then shows that
\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
is a modelled distribution in
\mathcal{D}^{|\tau|}(T_{<|\tau|},{\sf g})
.

\bullet
If
\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}}f_{\sigma}(\cdot)\,\sigma
, is an element of
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
, then, for each
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
, the
T^{+}
-valued function

\boldsymbol{f}/\tau(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\sigma\geq\tau}f_{\sigma}(\cdot)\,\sigma/\tau.

is an element of
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-|\tau|}(T^{+},{\sf g})
, where we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T^{+},{\sf g})
the space of
T^{+}
-valued modelled distributions with transition
{\sf g}
. Recall that
\mathscr{T}^{+}=(T^{+},T^{+})
is also a regularity structure.

The next statement says that the consistency condition encoded in the notion of modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{f}
ensures the existence of a globally defined object close to
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
near each
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and gives condition for uniqueness. Recall
A
stands for the index set in the grading of
T
and set

\beta_{0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\min A.

Theorem 4
.

(Reconstruction theorem)

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a concrete regularity structure and
{\sf M}=(\sf g,\Pi)
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
. Fix a regularity exponent
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
. There exists a linear continuous operator

\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}:\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}\wedge 0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

satisfying the property

(2.33)

\Big|\big\langle\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim\|{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}\|\,\big\|\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\,t^{\frac{\gamma}{4}},

uniformly in
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g}),x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0<t\leq 1
. Such an operator is unique if the exponent
\gamma
is positive.

A distribution
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
satisfying identity (
2.33
) is called a
reconstruction of the modelled distribution

\boldsymbol{f}
. When
\gamma=0
, the existence of a reconstruction is not ensured by (
2.33
) in
general. See Example 5.5 in
[
22
]
. We will see as a particular case of Corollary
6
that the lift (
2.30
) of a
\gamma
-Hölder function
f
in the polynomial regularity structure has indeed
f
as a reconstruction.

Notice from the definition of
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}
, we have the relation

\displaystyle({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\otimes{\sf g}_{x})\Delta

\displaystyle=({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\otimes{\sf g}_{x})(\Delta\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta

\displaystyle=({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\otimes{\sf g}_{x})(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+})\Delta

\displaystyle=({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\bf 1}_{+}^{\prime})\Delta={\sf\Pi},

and thus

{\sf\Pi}\tau={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\widehat{{\sf g}_{x}}\tau)={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau+{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}.

Therefore the constraint
\big|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau,p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim t^{|\tau|/4}
, that needs to be satisfied by a model, is equivalent to the estimate

(2.34)

\Big|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}\tau-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|=\Big|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}\tau-\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma){\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\sigma,p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim t^{|\tau|/4},

which says that
{\sf\Pi}\tau
is a/the reconstruction of the modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
from (
2.32
), depending on whether
|\tau|\leq 0
or
|\tau|>0
. Since, for
|\tau|<0
, the difference
(\ast)
of two reconstructions of
\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
satisfies

\big|\big\langle(\ast),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim t^{|\tau|/4}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, this difference is a
\mathcal{C}^{|\tau|}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
distribution from identity (
2.22
). So the estimate (
2.34
) shows in particular that we could require from scratch that the
\sf\Pi
map of a model
of
\mathscr{T}
takes values in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}\wedge 0}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
rather than
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The case
|\tau|=0
does not cause any problem as we assume that the only element of
T
of null homogeneity is
1
.

We will only work with
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
-spaces with positive regularity exponents
\gamma
in our study of singular stochastic PDEs. We only
give a proof of the reconstruction theorem in that setting, following Otto & Weber’s nice approach
[
75
]
. An extension to the inhomogeneous integral kernels can be found in
[
63
]
. See Friz and Hairer’s lecture notes
[
42
]
for another treatment along these lines. See Hairer’s original work
[
53
]
or the references given in Appendix
D
for a proof of Theorem
4
in the case
\gamma\leq 0
.

Proof –

Existence –
We construct explicitly a reconstruction operator. Note first that since

\begin{split}\Big({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\boldsymbol{f}(y)-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)(\cdot)&=\Big({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)\Big)(\cdot)\\
&=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B}}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\tau}\,\big({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\tau\big)(\cdot)\end{split}

one has

\begin{split}\left|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\boldsymbol{f}(y)-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x),p_{t}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\right|\lesssim\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},|\tau|<\gamma}d(y,x)^{\gamma-|\tau|}t^{\frac{|\tau|}{4}},\end{split}

from the bounds on models and modelled distributions. For
0<s\leq t\leq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, set

\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int p_{t-s}(x,y)\,\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\boldsymbol{f}(y),p_{s}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,dy.

We will obtain the distribution
{\sf R}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
from
\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}
under the form
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\lim_{s\downarrow 0}\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}
, with the limits taken in that order, with
s
sent to
0
first and then
t
sent to
0
. First, from the bounds on modelled
distributions, we have

\textbf{{I}}_{t}^{t}(x)=\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left|\textbf{{I}}_{t}^{t}(x)\right|\leq\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B}}\left|f_{\tau}(x)\right|\left|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau,p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\right|\lesssim t^{\frac{\beta_{0}}{4}},

and moreover, for
0<s^{\prime}<s<t\leq 1
, we have from the semigroup property of the kernel
p
the
x
-uniform estimate

\left|\textbf{{I}}_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(x)-\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}(x)\right|=\left|\int p_{t-s}(x,z)p_{s-s^{\prime}}(z,y)\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\boldsymbol{f}(y)-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{z}\boldsymbol{f}(z),p_{s^{\prime}}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,dzdy\right|

\begin{split}&\leq\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},|\tau|<\gamma}\int p_{t-s}(x,z)p_{s-s^{\prime}}(z,y)\,d(y,z)^{\gamma-|\tau|}(s^{\prime})^{\frac{|\tau|}{4}}\,dzdy\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},|\tau|<\gamma}(s-s^{\prime})^{\frac{\gamma-|\tau|}{4}}(s^{\prime})^{\frac{|\tau|}{4}}.\end{split}

For
s^{\prime}\in[s/2,s)
, this implies

(2.35)

\left|\textbf{{I}}_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(x)-\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}(x)\right|\lesssim s^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}.

For
s^{\prime}\in(0,s/2)
, by taking
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s^{\prime}\in\big[s/2^{n+1},s/2^{n}\big)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\textbf{{I}}_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(x)-\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\left|\textbf{{I}}_{s/2^{m}}^{t}(x)-\textbf{{I}}_{s/2^{m+1}}^{t}(x)\right|+\left|\textbf{{I}}_{s/2^{n}}^{t}(x)-\textbf{{I}}_{s^{\prime}}^{t}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}(s/2^{m})^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}+(s/2^{n})^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}\lesssim s^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}.

Thus the bound (
2.35
) holds uniformly over
0<s^{\prime}<s
. Hence the (locally in
t
) uniform limit

\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\underset{s\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}(x)

exists, since
\gamma
is positive. As the identity
P_{t^{\prime}}\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}=\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t+t^{\prime}}
follows from the semigroup property, we see from (
2.22
) that
\{\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}\}_{0<t\leq 1}
is bounded in the space
\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. (Note that all of the above estimates on
\textbf{{I}}_{s}^{t}
holds over
0<s\leq t\leq 2
, since the bounds on
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau
can be extended to
0<t\leq 2
by a similar argument to Proposition
2
.) Therefore by noting the continuity of
t\mapsto P_{t}\Lambda

\|(P_{t}-\text{\rm Id})\Lambda\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\|\Lambda\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}}

for any
\varepsilon>0
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}
(see e.g., Lemma 2.15 of
[
63
]
), for
0<s<t\leq 1
we have

\|\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}-\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{s}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}}=\|(P_{t-s}-\text{\rm Id})\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{s}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}}\lesssim(t-s)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\|\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{s}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}}\lesssim(t-s)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}.

Hence
\{\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}\}_{0<t\leq 1}
converges in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
t
goes to
0
, for any
\varepsilon>0
. Denote its limit by
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
. Since

\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle=\lim_{s\to 0}\big\langle\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{s},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle=\lim_{s\to 0}\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t+s}(x)=\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}(x),

we have actually
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
from the
x
-uniform bound
|\textbf{{I}}_{0}^{t}(x)|\lesssim t^{\beta_{0}/4}
. Letting
s=t
and sending
s^{\prime}
to
0
in (
2.35
) we can check that
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
satisfies the bound (
2.33
).

Uniqueness –
To prove uniqueness of the reconstruction operator on
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
when the regularity exponent
\gamma
is positive, we start from the identity

\big|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim t^{\frac{\gamma}{4}},

satisfied uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
by any other reconstruction
operator
(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}
. As for any Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
the convolutions
\int\varphi(x)p_{t}(x,z)dx
, converge to
\varphi
in the smooth topology, one has from the symmetry of the kernels
p_{t}
and the fact that
\gamma
is positive

\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\big)^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle=\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\int\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\Big\rangle\,\varphi(x)dx=\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,O\big(t^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}\big)=0.

\rhd

One can use Proposition
2
to improve estimate (
2.33
) under the form

(2.36)

\Big|\big\langle\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\partial_{x}^{n}p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim t^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}},

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
.

We now state two standard properties of the reconstruction operator. The following fact implies that
\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle
depends only on the restriction of
\boldsymbol{f}
to the support of
\varphi
. This fact is used to define the reconstructions of modelled distributions which are given in
(0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
t\in(0,\infty]
, not in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. See Theorem
20
and Section
4.3
.

Corollary 5
.

Pick
\gamma
positive. If
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
is null on an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}=0
on
U
.

Proof –

Since the mapping
x\mapsto{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
is null on
U
, it follows from estimate (
2.33
) that

|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle|\lesssim t^{\gamma/4},

for all
x\in U
. For a smooth function
\varphi
with compact support in
U
, one can use the convergence of
\int\varphi(x)p_{t}(x,z)dx
to
\varphi
as
t>0
goes to
0
in an appropriate
C^{k}
space, to get

\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle=\lim_{t\to 0}\int\varphi(x)\,\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\,dx=0.

\rhd

The following fact is an immediate consequence of uniqueness in the reconstruction theorem; it is used in Section
4
and implies in particular that the the lift (
2.30
) of a
\gamma
-Hölder function
f
in the polynomial regularity structure has indeed
f
as a reconstruction.

Corollary 6
.

Pick
\gamma
positive and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
.
If the model
({\sf g},{\sf\Pi})
takes values in the space of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the mapping
x\mapsto\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x)
is itself a continuous function and

(2.37)

\big(\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)=\Big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)(x).

Remark

–

One may wonder for which class of
T
-valued functions the reconstruction theorem holds. Caravenna and Zambotti proved in
[
22
]
a new notion of
germs
. A family of distributions
(f_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is measurable in
x
is called a germ. For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the germ
(f_{x})_{x}
is called
\gamma
-
coherent
if there exists
\beta_{0}\leq\gamma\wedge 0
such that

\big|\big\langle f_{y}-f_{x},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim t^{\frac{\beta_{0}}{4}}\big(d(y,x)+t^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{\gamma-\beta_{0}}.

(Here we present a simplified definition rather than the original definition in
[
22
]
.) Caravenna and Zambotti
[
22
]
stated a more general reconstruction theorem at the level of coherent germs (Theorem 5.1 therein), and also stated that the coherence is actually necessary for the existence of the reconstruction (Theorem 6.1 therein). For any models
\sf M=(\Pi,g)
and modelled distributions
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
, the
x
-dependent distributions
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
define a coherent germ.

§
Function-like comodules

Throughout we will work with regularity structures satisfying the following assumption saying that
T
and
T^{+}
contain the polynomial regularity
structure.

Assumption (A1) –

The concrete regularity structure
\big((T^{+},\Delta^{+}),(T,\Delta)\big)
contains the polynomial regularity structure in the following sense. One has
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}^{(+)}=\big\{X^{n}_{(+)}\,;\,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}=\alpha\big\}
, for a symbol
X_{(+)}\in T^{(+)}
and any integer
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\Delta^{(+)}X_{(+)}^{n}=\sum_{\ell\leq n}\binom{n}{\ell}X_{(+)}^{\ell}\otimes X_{+}^{n-\ell}.

The notation
X_{+}^{n}
allows to distinguish the elements in
\mathcal{B}^{+}
and
\mathcal{B}
. Note that we always have
X_{+}
in the right hand side of the above tensor product while we have
X
or
X_{+}
in the left hand side depending on whether we work on
T
or
T^{+}
. Set

\mathcal{B}_{X}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big\{X_{+}^{n}\,;\,n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\big\},\qquad\mathcal{B}_{X}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big\{X^{n}\,;\,n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\big\},

and write

{\bf 1}_{+}=X_{+}^{0},\qquad{\bf 1}=X^{0}.

By assumption, for any
integer

\alpha
, the basis
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}^{(+)}
consists
exactly
of the elements
X^{n}_{(+)}
with
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}=\alpha
. Therefore these polynomials are the
only elements of
T^{(+)}
with integer homogeneities. In particular,
T_{0}^{(+)}
is a one-dimensional vector space.

Note the use of
X_{+}
in the formula for
\Delta X^{n}
. The space

T_{X}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{span}(\mathcal{B}_{X}^{+})

with
\Delta^{+}
is isomorphic to a polynomial regularity structure, while
the space

T_{X}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{span}(\mathcal{B}_{X})

with
\Delta
is a right comodule over
T_{X}^{+}
. One defines a
canonical model
over the polynomial regularity structure

\mathscr{T}_{X}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big((T_{X}^{+},\Delta^{+}),(T_{X},\Delta)\big)

setting for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

{\sf g}_{x}(X_{+}^{n})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=x^{n},\qquad({\sf\Pi}X^{n})(y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=y^{n}.

We see that
{\sf g}_{yx}(X_{+}^{n})=(y-x)^{n}
and
({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}X^{n})(y)=(y-x)^{n}
, so
({\sf g},{\sf\Pi})
is indeed a model over
\mathscr{T}_{X}
.

Assumption (A2) –

Under Assumption
(A
1
)
, we only consider models
({\sf\Pi},{\sf g})
whose restriction to
\mathscr{T}_{X}
is the canonical model.

We only work from now on with regularity structures satisfying Assumptions
(A
1
)
and
(A
2
)
. It is useful, to deal with sub-regularity structures of a given regularity structure, to introduce the following notion. A linear subspace
V
of
T
is called a
subcomodule
if

\Delta V\subset V\otimes T^{+},

that is, defining
V_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=V\cap T_{\alpha}
, the pair
\big((T^{+},\Delta^{+}),(V,\Delta)\big)
is a regularity structure. A subcomodule
V
is said to be
function-like
, if
V
satisfies Assumptions
(A
1
)
and
(A
2
)
and if
V_{\beta}=0
whenever
\beta<0
. Given a subcomodule
V
, set

\alpha_{0}(V)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\min\Big\{\alpha\in A\,;\,V_{\alpha}\neq 0,\,\alpha\notin\mathbb{N}\Big\}

if there is
\alpha\notin\mathbb{N}
such that
V_{\alpha}\neq 0
, and otherwise
\alpha_{0}(V)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\infty
.

Corollary 7
.

Let
V
be a function-like comodule. For a positive regularity exponent
\gamma
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,{\sf g})
, one has
\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha_{0}(V)\wedge\gamma}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

\big(\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)=f_{\bf 1}(x).

Proof –

Set
\beta=\alpha_{0}(V)\wedge\gamma
. To see the regularity of
f_{\bf 1}
, we write

\boldsymbol{f}(x)=\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta}\frac{f_{n}(x)}{n!}X^{n}+\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},\,|\tau|\geq\beta}f_{\tau}(x)\tau,\qquad\boldsymbol{g}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},\,|\tau|\geq\beta}f_{\tau}(x)\tau.

By expanding
(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x))_{X^{n}}=O(d(x,y)^{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}})
for any
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta
, we have

\displaystyle f_{n}(y)-\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{f_{n+\ell}(x)}{\ell!}(y-x)^{\ell}-\ell!\big(\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\,\boldsymbol{g}(x)\big)_{X^{n}}=O\big(d(x,y)^{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\big).

Since
\big(\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\,\boldsymbol{g}(x)\big)_{X^{n}}=O\big(d(x,y)^{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\big)
, we have

f_{n}(y)-\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{f_{n+\ell}(x)}{\ell!}(y-x)^{\ell}=O\big(d(x,y)^{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\big).

This implies that
f_{0}=f_{\bf 1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
and
f_{n}=\partial^{n}f_{\bf 1}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\big\langle f_{\bf 1}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle f_{\bf 1}-\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta}\frac{f_{n}(x)}{n!}(\cdot-x)^{n}+{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\,\boldsymbol{g}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle O\big(d(x,\cdot)^{\beta}\big)+{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\,\boldsymbol{g}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle=O(t^{\frac{\beta}{4}}).

The uniqueness part of the proof of the reconstruction theorem, Theorem
4
, makes it clear that the reconstruction
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
of
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
, with
\gamma>0
, is characterized by the estimate

\Big|\big\langle\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim t^{\gamma^{\prime}},

whatever positive exponent
\gamma^{\prime}
appears in the upper bound. Hence
\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}=f_{\bf 1}
.

\rhd

2
.
4   Products and derivatives

Other regularity structures than the polynomial regularity structure can be used to ‘model’ functions. In good cases, they come equipped with a bilinear operation that plays the role plaid by multiplication in the usual
setting, and allows to define the image of a modelled distribution by a nonlinear map. This is what this section is about.

Let
V,W
be subcomodules of
T
and set

V_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=V\cap T_{\alpha},\qquad W_{\alpha}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=W\cap T_{\alpha}.

0
Definition –

A
product
on
V\times W
is a continuous bilinear map
\star:V\times W\rightarrow T
, such that
V_{\alpha}\star W_{\beta}\subset T_{\alpha+\beta}
, for all
\alpha,\beta\in A
.
The product is said to be
regular
if

\Delta(\tau\star\sigma)=(\Delta\tau)(\Delta\sigma)

for all
\tau\in V
and
\sigma\in W
. In the right hand side, the product
(V\otimes T^{+})\times(W\otimes T^{+})\to T\otimes T^{+}
is canonically defined from
\star
and the product of
T^{+}
setting

(\tau\otimes\mu)(\sigma\otimes\nu)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\tau\star\sigma)\otimes(\mu\nu).

The regularity structures used in the study of singular PDEs have elements that are decorated rooted trees. The product is given as a tree product
in that setting, and such a product is regular in the above sense. The details will be found in Section
9
. A regular product

\star
satisfies

(2.38)

\displaystyle\widehat{g}(\tau\star\sigma)=\widehat{g}(\tau)\star\widehat{g}(\sigma),

for any character
g
on
T^{+}
. For regularity structures containing the polynomial regularity structure one asks the following consistency assumption.

Assumption (A3) –

Under Assumption
(A
1
)
, the product between
T_{X}
and
T
is always defined and satisfies

{\bf 1}\star\tau=\tau\star{\bf 1}=\tau,\text{ for all }\tau\in T,\qquad X^{k}\star X^{\ell}=X^{k+\ell},\text{ for all }k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}.

We remark that Assumption
(A
3
)
is not contained in Hairer’s general definition
[
53
]
(of course always assumed in the specific regularity structure of decorated rooted trees
[
19
,
25
,
16
]
). We make this assumption here because it is used in the proof of ‘Whitney extension theorem’ (Theorem
52
in appendix) by Martin
[
74
]
. Assumptions
(A
1
)
,
(A
2
)
, and
(A
3
)
are jointly called Assumption
(A)
. The proof of the next statement is elementary and left to the
reader. See the proof of Theorem 4.6 in
[
53
]
if needed. For
\alpha\leq 0<\gamma
, denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
the space of modelled distributions of the form

\boldsymbol{f}=\sum_{\alpha\leq|\tau|<\gamma}f_{\tau}\tau

and write

\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}:T\to T_{<\gamma}

for the canonical projection.

Proposition 8
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq 0<\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
, and set
\gamma=(\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{2})\wedge(\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{1})
. Let
\star:V\times W\to T
be a regular product. Given
\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\gamma_{1}}(V,\sf g)
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\gamma_{2}}(W,\sf g)
, one has

\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}(\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\star\boldsymbol{f}_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g).

The mapping
(\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2})\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}(\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\star\boldsymbol{f}_{2})
is continuous.

Let
V
be a
function-like comodule
of
T
equipped with an associative product

\star:V\times V\to V.

Then
\star
is naturally extended to the multilinear map from
V^{n}
to
V
, for any
n\geq 1
. For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,{\sf g})
with
\gamma>0
and a smooth function
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define

F^{\star}(\boldsymbol{f})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{F^{(n)}(f_{\bf 1})}{n!}\,\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\,\star n}\right),\qquad\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\boldsymbol{f}-f_{\bf 1}{\bf 1}.

The sum contains only finitely many terms since the sector
V
is function-like.
Indeed, since
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}=\sum_{\alpha\leq|\tau|<\gamma}f_{\tau}\tau
for an
\alpha>0
, we have
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\,\star n}=\sum_{n\alpha\leq|\tau|<n\gamma}g_{\tau}\tau
for some coefficients
g_{\tau}
. The proof of the next proposition is elementary and left to the reader; see Theorem 4.15 in
[
53
]
for a proof.

Proposition 9
.

Pick a positive regularity exponent
\gamma
. For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,{\sf g})
and a smooth function
F
, one has
F^{\star}(\boldsymbol{f})\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,{\sf g})
. Moreover, the mapping
\boldsymbol{f}\mapsto F^{\star}(\boldsymbol{f})
is locally Lipschitz continuous.

Finally we introduce a linear operator playing the role of the derivative.

0
Definition –

A
derivative
is a continuous linear map
D:T\to T
, such that
DT_{\alpha}\subset T_{\alpha-1}
for all
\alpha\in A
, and

\Delta(D\tau)=(D\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta\tau

for any
\tau\in T
– by an abuse of notation, we mean
T_{\alpha-1}=\{0\}
if
\alpha-1\notin A
.

The assumption on
D
implies

\widehat{g}(D\tau)=D\widehat{g}(\tau)

for any character
g
on
T^{+}
. From this property it is straightforward to show the following statement.

Proposition 10
.

The mapping
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\ni\boldsymbol{f}\mapsto D\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-1}(T,{\sf g})
is continuous.
Moreover, if
{\sf\Pi}\circ D=\mathscr{D}\circ{\sf\Pi}
, holds for a first order differential operator
\mathscr{D}
then

\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(D\boldsymbol{f})=\mathscr{D}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)

for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
with
\gamma>1
.

3  – Regularity structures built from integration operators

We describe in this section a setting where one can lift a given singular PDE into an equation set on a space of modelled distributions. The lift depends on the arbitrary choice of a model on the regularity structure and we will see in Section
4
that the lifted equation has a unique local in time solution
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}(\sf M)
for every model. The solution of the initial singular equation will be defined as the reconstruction of this unique
\boldsymbol{u}
.

The regularity structures used for the study of singular stochastic PDEs have a particular structure that comes from the fixed point formulation of the (system of) PDE(s) under study. We concentrate here on the case where only one second order differential operator is involved, typically
\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}
. (See Section
9
and Appendix
D
for comments on the general case.) We work then with regularity structures equipped with an operator
\mathcal{I}
that plays the role of the convolution operator
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}
, involved in the fixed point formulation of the equation under study. This operator is called an abstract integration map; it is introduced in Section
3.2
. One associates in Section
3.3
to an abstract integration operator
\mathcal{I}
a notion of admissible
\sf\Pi
-maps, which roughly means that
{\sf\Pi}(\mathcal{I}\tau)=(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}({\sf\Pi}\tau)
. For some models
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
with
\sf\Pi
admissible, the map
\mathcal{I}
is (essentially) intertwined to the operator
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}
via
\sf\Pi
. These particular models play a crucial role in Section
3.4
. We construct therein a model-dependent operator
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
that is (essentially) intertwined to
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}
via the reconstruction map
{\sf R}^{\sf M}
, and which also has a regularizing property analogue to a similar property enjoyed by
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}
. The operator
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
will be used in Section
4
to lift the singular PDE into an equation on a space of modelled distributions. The condition that
\sf\Pi
is admissible has some far reaching consequences and it is not obvious in the first place that one can construct a non-trivial model that is admissible. Section
3.5
is dedicated to constructing a large class of admissible models.

The main result of this section is Theorem
17
, which gives the continuity property of the operator
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
.

A remark is in order before we set the scene in Section
3.1
. We will restrict our study to regularity structures for which the minimum homogeneity of its elements satisfies

(3.1)

\beta_{0}=\min A>-2.

This condition ensures that elements of the form
\mathcal{I}(\tau)
that appear in the expansion of solutions to the regularity structure lift of the considered class of singular stochastic PDE are of positive homogeneity. (So if
\mathcal{I}
were increasing the homogeneity of all symbols by
a
we would require
\beta_{0}>-a
.) While the generalized (KPZ) equation (
1.3
) satisfies for instance this assumption, not all singular stochastic PDEs satisfy it. This is for instance the case of the
\Phi^{4}_{2},\Phi^{4}_{3}
or sine-Gordon equations. This kind of equations can nonetheless be studied within the setting of regularity structures by writing their solutions as the sum of an explicit functional of the noise and a remainder term that solves an equation that can be formulated in a regularity structure satisfying condition (
3.1
) – the so-called da Prato-Debussche trick, after similar operation was used in their work
[
30
]
. We consider as an example the case of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
equation

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})u=-u^{3}+\zeta,

set on the
3
-dimensional torus, with
\zeta
a spacetime white noise of Hölder regularity
(-5/2)^{-}
. We decompose a priori the solution
u
into
u=X+v
, where

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})X

\displaystyle=\zeta,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})v

\displaystyle=-v^{3}-3v^{2}X-3vX^{2}-X^{3}.

The polynomial functions
(X^{n})_{1\leq n\leq 3}
can directly be defined as elements of
\mathcal{C}^{(-n/2)^{-}}
by probabilistic means. The above equation for
v
can then be formulated in a regularity structure with three noise symbols for
X,X^{2}
and
X^{3}
, in which
\beta_{0}=(-3/2)^{-}>-2
. The interested reader will find more details on this matter for a general class of singular stochastic PDEs in Section 5 of Bruned, Chandra, Chevyrev and Hairer’s work
[
16
]
.

3
.
1   Operators on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

We will be interested in (systems of) singular stochastic PDEs that involve possibly two types of differential operators. The derivatives
\partial_{i}
in the directions of the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the second order differential operator

{\bf L}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1.

Denote by
{\bf L}^{-1}
the resolution operator associating to a Schwartz function
v\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
the solution
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the equation

{\bf L}u=v.

The strict positiveness of
-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1
ensures uniqueness of a solution
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the preceding equation. (This is the reason why we work with
{\bf L}
rather than with the heat operator.) The operator
{\bf L}^{-1}
can be represented using a variant of the elliptic operators
\mathcal{G}
introduced in Section
2.3
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\big(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1\big)\,\big(-\partial_{x_{0}}^{2}+(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)^{2}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)\,e^{r(\partial_{x_{0}}^{2}-(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)^{2})}dr.

Write

{\bf L}^{-1}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)\,e^{r(\partial_{x_{0}}^{2}-(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)^{2})}\,dr=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{r}\,dr.

We thus use the inhomogeneous operator

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\partial_{x_{0}}^{2}-(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)^{2}

instead of the operator
\mathcal{G}
considered in Section
2.3
. The contents in the previous section holds similarly even if we redefine
p_{t}
as the kernel of
e^{t\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
. Since
q_{t}(x,y)=-(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)p_{t}(x,y)
, by Proposition
2
, the kernel
q_{r}(x,y)
of the operator
K_{r}
satisfies the
x
-uniform bounds

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left|\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau,\partial_{x}^{n}q_{r}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\right|\lesssim r^{\frac{|\tau|-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
,
r\in(0,1]
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, with the
exponent
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2
coming from the derivative operators
\partial_{x}^{n}
and
(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)
applied to
e^{r\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}
.
It is convenient, for technical purposes, we replace
q_{r}
with the function

\widetilde{q}_{r}(x,y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=q_{r}(x,y)-P_{r}(\partial_{x})q_{1}(x,y)

for some
r
-dependent polynomial
P_{r}(\xi)=P_{r}(\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d})
whose coefficients are bounded over
r\in[0,1]
, chosen to satisfy the property that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}y^{n}\widetilde{q}_{r}(x,y)dy=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
for fixed positive number
N
.
For instance, when
N=2
, we choose

\widetilde{q}_{r}(x,y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=q_{r}(x,y)-e^{-(r-1)}q_{1}(x,y).

In general, by noting that Fourier transform of
Q_{r}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=q_{r}(\cdot,0)
is given by
\widehat{Q_{r}}(\xi)=f(\xi)e^{rg(\xi)}
for some polynomials
f
and
g
, and that

Q_{r}(\xi)-P_{r}(\xi)Q_{1}(\xi)=Q_{1}(\xi)\big(e^{(r-1)g(\xi)}-P_{r}(\xi)\big),

we can choose a polynomial
P_{r}(\xi)
such that
\partial_{\xi}^{n}\big(e^{(r-1)g(\xi)}-P_{r}(\xi)\big)|_{\xi=0}
for any
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
.
By using this modified kernel, we decompose
{\bf L}^{-1}
under the form

{\bf L}^{-1}={\bf K}+{\bf K}^{\prime},

with

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\bf K}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{0}^{1}K_{r}\,dr-\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}P_{r}(\partial)K_{1}dr\bigg),\qquad{\bf K}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{1}^{\infty}K_{r}\,dr+\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}P_{r}(\partial)K_{1}dr\bigg).

It is elementary to see that the operators
{\bf K}^{\prime}
maps
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for any regularity exponent
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. This decomposition is similar but different to the one
\bar{K}=K+R
as in
[
53
, Lemma 5.5]
, where
\bar{K}
is the Green function,
K
is a singular kernel with a bounded support, and
R
is a smooth remainder. We concentrate on the operator
{\bf K}
in the remainder of this section.
Denote by

(3.5)

\displaystyle K(x,y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{q}_{r}(x,y)\,dr

its kernel. The compensation of
K
ensures that

\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}y^{n}K(x,y)dy=0

for any
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
with fixed positive number
N
. This property is used in the proof of Theorem
17
in this section, and the proof of Theorem
44
later.

Proposition 11
.

(Schauder estimates for
{\bf L}^{-1}
)
The operators
{\bf L}^{-1}
and
\bf K
are continuous operators from
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma+2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for all non-integer regularity exponents
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof –

It is sufficient to show the estimate for
\widetilde{\mathbf{K}}=\int_{0}^{1}K_{r}\,dr
. Note that
K_{t}=-e^{t\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)
, as
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
and
(\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)
commute. We use the freedom on the choice of the (
(2,1,\dots,1)
-scaling) elliptic operator used to define the Hölder spaces, while giving equivalent norms, to work with the norm associated with the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
rather than the operator
\mathcal{G}
. We emphasize that fact by writing
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(N)}_{s}
for the operators built from
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
in the same way as
\mathcal{Q}^{(N)}_{s}
is built from
\mathcal{G}
. Given a distribution
\Lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
non-integer, we read on the identity

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(N)}_{s}\big(\widetilde{\bf K}(\Lambda)\big)=\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(N)}_{s}\big(K_{r}(\Lambda)\big)dr=-\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{s}{s+r}\Big)^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{(N)}_{s+r}\Big((\partial_{x_{0}}+\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)\Lambda\Big)\,dr,

the estimate

\Big\|Q_{s}^{(N)}\big(\widetilde{\bf K}(\Lambda)\big)\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim s^{\frac{\gamma-2}{4}+1}+O(s^{N}).

The result follows for all
\gamma+2<4N
. The equivalence of the different Hölder norms corresponding to different choices of
N
gives the conclusion.

\rhd

(We refer the reader to Section 14.3 of the second edition of Friz & Hairer’s lecture notes
[
42
]
for a particularly nice proof of the classical Schauder estimates using different tools.) For a regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
for which
\beta_{0}=\min A>-2
, and a model
({\sf\Pi},{\sf g})
on it, Schauder estimates imply in particular that all the distributions
\textbf{K}({\sf\Pi}\tau)(\cdot)
, hence all the distributions
\textbf{K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)
, are actually defined pointwise, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, making sense of
\textbf{K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)
, or even
\partial^{n}\textbf{K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)
, for
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta_{0}+2
. The following lemma allows to take profit from the fact that
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau
behaves near
x
“as” an element of
\mathcal{C}^{|\tau|}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, to give meaning to
\partial^{n}\textbf{K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)
, for all multiindices
n
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2
.

Lemma 12
.

Assume
\beta_{0}>-2
. Given
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, the
integral

(3.6)

\big(\partial^{n}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)\big)(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau,\partial_{x}^{n}K(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau,\partial_{x}^{n}\widetilde{q}_{r}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\,dr

converges for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, provided
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2
.

Proof –

It follows from (
3.2
) that the first term of the right hand side of (
3.6
) is integrable over
r\in(0,1)

if

|\tau|-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}>-2.

\rhd

The
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
-indexed distributions
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
satisfy a similar bound to (
3.2
) for any modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
. We can then define properly
\partial^{n}{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x)
, for all multiindices
n
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2
, as in the preceding lemma.

3
.
2   Regularity structures with some abstract integration operators

Recall Assumption
(A)
essentially says that we consider regularity structures containing the canonical polynomial structure and models that behave naturally on the latter. In this section, we consider a regularity structure that can represent more specific functions/distributions. To explain the motivation of the next assumption, pick a
\gamma
-Hölder function
f
with some
\gamma>0
and let us try to add a new symbol
F
with homogeneity
\gamma
which represents
f
to a polynomial regularity structure
\mathscr{T}_{X}
. The new model space is
T=\text{span}(\mathcal{B}_{X}\cup\{F\})
.
On the other hand,
T^{+}
is required to be rich enough to define the application of the model to
F
as

(3.7)

{\sf\Pi}F=f,\qquad({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}F)(\cdot)=f(\cdot)-\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{n}}{n!}\partial^{n}f(x)=O\big(d(\cdot,x)^{\gamma}\big).

To represent coefficients
\partial^{n}f
, it would be natural to introduce symbols
\{F_{n}\}_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}
in
T^{+}
. Then a good definition of
\Delta
is given by

\Delta F=F\otimes{\bf 1}+\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{X^{n}}{n!}\otimes F_{n}.

Indeed, by applying
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}
on both sides and noting that
{\sf\Pi}=({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\otimes{\sf g}_{x})\Delta
, we have the second equality of (
3.7
) under the choice
{\sf g}_{x}(F_{n})=\partial^{n}f(x)
.
Similarly, we also need to define the application of
\Delta^{+}
to each
F_{n}
by

\Delta^{+}F_{n}=F_{n}\otimes{\bf 1}+\sum_{|m|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{X^{m}}{m!}\otimes F_{n+m}

to define
{\sf g}_{yx}(F_{n})
as a remainder term of the Taylor expansion of
\partial^{n}f
. For this reason, it would be natural to consider the regularity structure that satisfy in addition to Assumption
(A)
the following set of assumptions. Recall we denote by
\{e_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
the canonical basis of
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
.

Assumption (B1) –

(a)

The basis
\mathcal{B}^{+}
of
T^{+}
is a commutative monoid with unit
{\bf 1}_{+}
, freely generated by the symbols

\{X_{+}^{e_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq d}\cup\big\{\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau\big\}_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},\,n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\,|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}>0},

Each element has homogeneity

\big|X_{+}^{e_{i}}\big|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathfrak{s}_{i},\quad\big|\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\tau\big|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}.

The operators
\Delta
and
\Delta^{+}
are related by the
intertwining relations

(3.8)

\Delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)=(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\,|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}\tau,

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
.

(b)

For
n\in\{0,e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
– i.e.,
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1
, there are operators
\mathcal{I}_{n}:T\to T
, with

\big|\mathcal{I}_{n}\tau\big|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}},\quad\tau\in\mathcal{B}.

One has for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}

(3.9)

\Delta(\mathcal{I}_{n}\tau)=(\mathcal{I}_{n}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\,|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}\tau.

For simplicity we write

\mathcal{I}^{(+)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{I}_{0}^{(+)}.

The operator
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is an abstract version of the convolution operator
\partial^{n}{\bf K}
. The restriction
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1
on
n
means that we only consider
{\bf K}
or
\partial_{i}{\bf K}
; this is sufficient for the study of all (systems of) singular stochastic PDEs whose solutions are functions involving second order differential operators satisfying the above classical Schauder estimate. Two remarks are in order.

(a)

The main point of Assumption
(B
1
)
is the introduction of the operator
\mathcal{I}_{n}
. It brings the supplementary operator
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}
for the reasons mentioned before. The first term of the right hand side of the identity (
3.9
) seems different from the definition of
\Delta F
mentioned before, but it specifies the action of the recentering operations on
\tau
. Indeed, by applying
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}
on both sides and noting that
\widehat{{\sf g}_{x}}(\tau)=\tau+\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma)\sigma
, we have

\displaystyle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\mathcal{I}_{n}\tau)={\sf\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{n}\tau-\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma){\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\mathcal{I}_{n}\sigma-\sum_{\ell}{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}\tau)\frac{(\cdot-x)^{n}}{n!}.

(b)

We assume that
T^{+}
is entirely constructed from the
\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}
operators and the polynomials, and has no other elements. This is because these elements are rich enough to build the regularity structure including polynomials, operators
\mathcal{I}_{n}
, and their (possible) products. The regularity structures that are used for the study of singular stochastic PDEs have the structure as above that is described in details in Section
9
.

We notice here some algebraic formulas about
\Delta
and
\Delta^{+}
. We let the readers check that identity (
3.8
) ensures the co associativities

\displaystyle(\Delta^{(+)}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta^{(+)}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{(+)}\tau)=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+})\Delta^{(+)}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{(+)}\tau)

on elements of
T^{(+)}
of the form
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{(+)}\tau
. Next using identity (
3.8
) we check that the antipode
S_{+}
on
T^{+}
satisfies the inductive relation

(3.10)

S_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau\big)=-\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{(-X)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n+\ell}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Delta\tau,

where we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{+}
the multiplication operator in the algebra
T^{+}
. Together with the relation
S_{+}(X_{+})=-X_{+}
, such a formula defines indeed a unique algebra morphism. Recall from Appendix
B
the defining property (
B.1
) of the antipode
S_{+}
on
T^{+}
. As
T^{+}
is a Hopf algebra by Proposition
48
, it suffices to see that

\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\textrm{Id}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Delta^{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\tau\big)=0

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
\tau\in T
. This relation follows from (
3.10
) and (
3.8
) writing

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\textrm{Id}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Delta^{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\tau\big)&=\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,S_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n+\ell}\tau\big)\\
&=\Big\{\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\otimes S_{+}\big)-\sum_{\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\frac{(-X)^{k}}{k!}\,\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n+\ell+k}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Big\}\Delta\tau\\
&=\Big\{\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}\otimes S_{+}\big)-\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{(X-X)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n+\ell}\otimes S_{+}\big)\Big\}\Delta\tau\\
&=0.\end{split}

Remark that the image by the operator
\mathcal{I}
of a modelled distribution is not a modelled distribution. The next two sections are dedicated to
constructing a model-dependent map
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
that maps continuously all
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
into
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(T,\sf g)
when
\gamma
is a positive non-integer real number – the analogue of part of Schauder
estimates, and is intertwined to the convolution operator
\bf K

{\bf K}\circ\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\circ\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}

via the reconstruction operator
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}
associated with
\sf M
. We say that
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
is a
lift of

\bf K
. The construction of this operator requires the introduction of the notion of admissible model.

3
.
3   Admissible models

In this section we consider only the operator
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{0}:T\to T
. The following notion plays a key role in the proof of the existence of a lift of the convolution operator
\bf K
. Recall that
{\bf K}(\zeta)
is well-defined pointwise for any distribution
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\beta>-2
, by Lemma
12
. The following definition is directly from
[
19
, Definition 6.9]
.

0
Definition –

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a regularity structure satisfying Assumptions
(A
3
)
and
(B
1
)
. Assume
\beta_{0}>-2
. A
{\sf\Pi}
-map on
T
is said to be
{\bf K}
-admissible
if it satisfies

(3.11)

{\sf\Pi}(\mathcal{I}\tau)={\bf K}({\sf\Pi}\tau),

and

(3.12)

{\sf\Pi}(X^{n}\star\tau)(x)=x^{n}({\sf\Pi}\tau)(x),

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
. A model
({\sf\Pi},{\sf g})
on
\mathscr{T}
is said to be
{\bf K}
-admissible if its
\sf\Pi
-map is
{\bf K}
-admissible.

The notion of admissible
\sf\Pi
-map gives flesh to the idea that the operator
\mathcal{I}
is the regularity structure counterpart of the convolution
operator
{\bf K}
. The importance of the notion of
\bf K
-admissible model comes from Theorem
17
in the next section. It shows that when working with
\bf K
-admissible models
\sf M
, one can upgrade the intertwining relation (
3.11
) into an intertwining relation between
\bf K
and an operator
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}

on modelled distributions, via the reconstruction map
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}
associated with
\sf M
.

While for a general model as in Definition
1
defining a
\sf g
-map satisfying the constraint (
2.23
) is decorrelated from the task of defining a
\sf\Pi
-map satisfying the constraint (
2.24
) it will turn out that imposing the intertwining relation (
3.11
) on
\sf\Pi
will constrain strongly
\sf g
. Unlike general models, admissible models on a regularity structure satisfying Assumptions
(A)
and
(B
1
)
will turn out to be partly defined by their
\sf\Pi
map; this will be proved in Proposition
15
. The
\sf g
-map of an admissible model on a regularity structure satisfying further a mild Assumption
(B
2
)
will turn out to be
entirely
defined by its
\sf\Pi
map.

We worked so far with models that are not constrained by anything else than their defining properties (
2.23
) and (
2.24
) and it is not clear that one can further impose additional conditions like (
3.11
). We will construct in Section
3.5
a whole class of admissible models with values in the set of smooth functions. This is all we need for the study of singular stochastic PDEs, as the nonsmooth admissible models involved in this setting are limits of smooth admissible models, and limits of admissible models are admissible. As for now, we keep going and see what can
be done with admissible models.

Recall from Lemma
12
the definition of
\partial^{n}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2
, and define the model-dependent polynomial-valued function on
T

\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{X^{n}}{n!}\,\partial^{n}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)\in T_{X},

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition 13
.

For a
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M
on
\mathscr{T}
one has, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\tau\in T
,

{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\big(\mathcal{I}\tau+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\tau\big)={\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)

Proof –

Using identity (
3.9
) and the admissibility of the model, one has indeed

\begin{split}{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}\tau)=\big({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)(\Delta\mathcal{I}\tau)&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}\big({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)(\mathcal{I}\sigma\otimes\tau/\sigma)+\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{(\cdot)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{\ell}\tau)\\
&=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}\sigma){\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\tau/\sigma)+P_{x}(\tau,\cdot)={\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau\big)+P_{x}(\tau,\cdot),\end{split}

so
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}\tau)
and
{\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau\big)
differ by a polynomial
P_{x}(\tau,\cdot)
of degree at most
|\tau|+2
. We identify this polynomial with
-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\big(\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\tau\big)
noting that
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}\tau)
has null derivatives at
x
up to the order the integer part of
|\tau|+2
, from condition (
2.24
).

\rhd

Corollary 14
.

For a
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M
on
\mathscr{T}
one has, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(3.13)

\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)=\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}.

Proof –

Given
\tau\in T
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one has both
\big(\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)-\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big)\tau\in T_{X}
, and

\begin{split}{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\Big(\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\tau-\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\tau\Big)&={\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\big(\mathcal{I}\tau+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\tau\big)-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\Big(\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\tau\Big)\\
&={\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau\big)-{\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{y}^{\sf g}\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\tau\big)=0,\end{split}

from Proposition
13
. As
{\sf\Pi}_{y}^{\sf g}
is injective on
T_{X}
this implies that

\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)-\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}=0.

\rhd

Proposition 15
.

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a regularity structure satisfying Assumptions
(A)
and
(B
1
)
. The
\sf g
-map of a
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf(\Pi,g)
on
\mathscr{T}
satisfies

{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau)={\bf K}({\sf\Pi}\tau)(x),

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof –

We show that there is at most one choice of
{\sf g}(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau)
such that
(\sf\Pi,g)
is an admissible model. Applying
{\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}
to the identity (
3.9
) giving
\Delta(\mathcal{I}_{n}\tau)
, with
n=0
, one gets from the
\bf K
-admissibility of
{\sf\Pi}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\mathcal{I}\tau)&={\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{{\sf g}}\tau)+\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{(\cdot)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell}^{+}\tau)\\
&={\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{{\sf g}}\tau)+\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,{\sf f}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell}^{+}\tau),\end{aligned}

where
{\sf f}
and
{\sf g}
are related by the formulas

\displaystyle{\sf f}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell}^{+}\tau)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|m|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{x^{m}}{m!}\,{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{\ell+m}^{+}\tau),\quad{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)=\sum_{|m|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(-x)^{m}}{m!}{\sf f}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{n+m}^{+}\tau).

As in the proof of Proposition
13
, since the derivatives of
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{{\sf g}}(\mathcal{I}\tau)
up to order
|\tau|+2
vanish at
x
,
we have

\displaystyle{\sf f}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)=-\partial^{n}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{{\sf g}}\tau)(x),

hence

(3.15)

\displaystyle{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)=-\sum_{|m|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(-x)^{m}}{m!}\partial^{n+m}{\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{{\sf g}}\tau\big)(x).

This implies another inductive formula

(3.16)

\displaystyle{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)=\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau;|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\sigma|+2}\,{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma)\,\partial^{k}\mathbf{K}({\sf\Pi}^{{\sf g}}_{x}\sigma)(x),

which is proved by applying
{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}
to the identity (
3.8
) describing
\Delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)
and using (
3.15
). Since
\beta_{0}>-2
, if
k=0
the condition
|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\sigma|+2
can be removed.
Hence we have
{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau)=\mathbf{K}({\sf\Pi}\tau)(x)
, by the comodule identity (
2.10
).

\rhd

Set

\mathcal{J}^{\sf M+}(x)\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{X_{+}^{n}}{n!}\,\partial^{n}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(x)\in T_{X}^{+}\subset T^{+},

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The following statement
is proved exactly as Proposition
13
and Corollary
14
; it will be used in the proof of Theorem
19
.

Proposition 16
.

Given a regularity structure satisfying Assumptions
(A)
and
(B
1
)
and a
\bf K
-admissible model
({\sf\Pi},{\sf g})
on it, one has, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

{\sf g}_{yx}\Big(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M+}(x)\tau\Big)={\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau)(y),

and

(3.17)

\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}^{+}\Big(\mathcal{I}^{+}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M+}(x)\Big)=\Big(\mathcal{I}^{+}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M+}(y)\Big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}.

Recall here that
\widehat{g}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes g)\Delta^{+}:T^{+}\to T^{+}
denotes the action of
g\in G^{+}
on
T^{+}
defined in the same way as
\widehat{g}:T\to T
.

3
.
4   Lifting
{\bf K}
as a continuous map from
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
into
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(T,\sf g)

For a given
{\bf K}
-admissible model
{\sf M}=({\sf\Pi},{\sf g})
we define in this section a continuous map
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
from
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
into
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(T,\sf g)
, for any positive non-integer regularity exponent
\gamma
, intertwined to
{\bf K}
via the reconstruction operator

(3.18)

\displaystyle{\bf K}\circ\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\circ\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}.

To get a grasp on what
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
could be one keeps from the reconstruction theorem, Theorem
4
, the image that for
\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(T,\sf g)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the distribution
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{g}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{g}(x)
behaves near
x
like the function
d(\cdot,x)^{\gamma+2}
. For
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
, since we have

{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\Big(\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)={\bf K}\Big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big),

from Proposition
13
, it then looks natural to add to
\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)
the polynomial expansion

\big(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\big(\partial^{\ell}{\bf K}\big)\Big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)(x)

of
{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)
at point
x
, at order
\gamma+2
, and expect that

{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\Big(\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)+\big(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)\Big)

behaves like
d(\cdot,x)^{\gamma+2}
near
x
. (The remark after Lemma
12
justifies the good definition of the quantities
(\partial^{\ell}{\bf K})\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)
in
\big(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)
, for
|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2
.) This does not guarantee that the
T
-valued map

(3.19)

\big(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)+\big(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

is a modelled distribution, but this turns out to be the case! Note that unlike
\mathcal{I}
or
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)
, the
T_{X}
-valued function
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
is a non-local function of
\boldsymbol{f}
– i.e.
(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)
is not a function of
\boldsymbol{f}(x)
only. Note also that one has
formally

\big(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)=\mathcal{I}\boldsymbol{f}(x)+\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\partial^{\ell}{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x).

This identity gives the intuitive meaning of the polynomial part of
\big(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)
. Decomposition (
3.19
) is needed to make sense of
\big(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)
in a rigorous way. We can prove the following theorem by the same way as
[
53
, Theorem 5.12]
except the use of a decomposition of
K
by integration rather than the dyadic decomposition as in
[
53
]
.

Theorem 17
.

Let the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
satisfy Assumptions
(A-B1)
and
\sf(g,\Pi)
be a
\bf K
-admissible model on it. Let
\gamma
be a positive non-integer regularity exponent
\gamma
, and choose an integer
N
such that
\gamma+2<N
and that the property (
3.3
) holds for any
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
. Then the map
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
sends continuously
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
into
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(T,\sf g)
, and satisfies the intertwining identity (
3.18
).

Before the proof, we recall from Proposition A.1 of
[
53
]
the (anisotropic) integral Taylor formula for the remainder.

Lemma 18
.

There exists a family of Borel probability measures
\{m_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}
on
[0,1]^{d+1}
satisfying the following properties.
For any smooth functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma>0
, one has the identity

f(y)-\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{\partial^{k}f(x)}{k!}(y-x)^{k}=\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\gamma}\frac{(y-x)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int_{[0,1]^{d+1}}\partial^{\ell}f(x_{t})m_{\ell}(dt),

where
\ell
runs over a finite set and
x_{t}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(x_{i}+t_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}))_{i=0}^{d}
.

Proof of Theorem
17
–

We use the interwining relation (
3.13
) to write

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\Big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)\hskip-1.42271pt+\hskip-1.42271pt\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(y)\Big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)\hskip-1.42271pt+\hskip-1.42271pt\Big((\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\Big).

For the
\mathcal{I}
term, from the continuity of
\mathcal{I}
, one has the estimate

\big\|\mathcal{I}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)\big\|_{\beta}\lesssim\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta-2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\,d(y,x)^{\gamma+2-\beta}

for any
\beta\in A
. The
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}
terms take values in the polynomial part
T_{X}
of
T
. Decompose
K(x,y)
into the integral of
\widetilde{q}_{r}
by (
3.5
), and let

\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}_{r}dr,\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}=:\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}_{r}dr,

stand for the corresponding operators. Since
q_{1}
has a smoothing property, we replace
\widetilde{q}_{r}
with
q_{r}
in the following calculations. Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, and write
(\tau)_{X^{n}}
for the component
of
\tau\in T
in the direction of
X^{n}
.
We have for

(\circleddash)_{r}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big(\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}_{r}(y)\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)+\big(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}_{r}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}_{r}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\Big)_{X^{n}}

the two decompositions

\displaystyle(\circleddash)_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in A,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta+2}\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\beta},\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\bigg\{\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{y}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(y),\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\Big\rangle-\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(y-x)^{k}}{k!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\partial_{x}^{n+k}q_{r}(x,\cdot)\Big\rangle\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}(\Asterisk)^{1}_{r}+(\Asterisk)^{2}_{r},

and

\displaystyle(\circleddash)_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in A,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta+2}\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\beta},\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\Big\rangle+\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\boldsymbol{f}(x)-{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\boldsymbol{f}(y),\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\Big\rangle-\sum_{\beta\in A,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\beta+2}\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{y}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\beta},\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}(\bigstar)_{r}^{1}+(\bigstar)_{r}^{2},

where

\displaystyle(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}(z)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,z)-\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(y-x)^{k}}{k!}\,\partial_{x}^{n+k}q_{r}(x,z).

Choose
r_{0}\in(0,1]
such that
r_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\simeq d(y,x)\wedge 1
. We use the
(\Asterisk)
-decomposition to estimate the integral over
0<r<r_{0}
, and the
(\bigstar)
-decomposition to estimate the integral over
r_{0}\leq r\leq 1
.

\bullet
For
r\in(0,r_{0}]
, we have from the bound (
3.2
) the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\big|(\Asterisk)^{1}_{r}\big|dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in A,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta+2}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in A,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\beta+2}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}r_{0}^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}\lesssim d(y,x)^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

Since
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2
, from the bound (
2.36
) in the reconstruction theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\big|(\Asterisk)^{2}_{r}\big|dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}\,dr+\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(y,x)^{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\int_{0}^{r_{0}}r^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}\,dr

\displaystyle\lesssim r_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}+\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(y,x)^{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}r_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}\lesssim d(y,x)^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

\bullet
To deal with the integral over
r\in(r_{0},1]
, we use the
(\bigstar)
-decomposition. Since this integral does not make sense if
r_{0}\geq 1
, we assume
d(y,x)\leq 1
. For
(\bigstar)^{1}_{r}
, we apply Lemma
18
to write

(3.20)

\displaystyle(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}(z)=\sum_{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(y-x)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int_{[0,1]^{d+1}}\partial^{n+\ell}q_{r}(x_{t},z)m_{\ell}(dt),

where
\ell
runs over a finite set
. Note that no index
n
with
\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}=|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}
exists, because
\gamma\notin\mathbb{Z}
. By decomposing

\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x_{t}}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x_{t})+{\sf\Pi}_{x_{t}}^{\sf g}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(x_{t})-\widehat{{\sf g}_{x_{t}x}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big),

and using the bounds (
2.36
) and (
3.2
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\big|(\bigstar)^{1}_{r}\big|\,dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(y,x)^{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\left\{r^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}+\sum_{\beta\in A,\,\beta<\gamma}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}r^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}\right\}dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(y,x)^{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\left\{r_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}+\sum_{\beta\in A,\,\beta<\gamma}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}r_{0}^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim d(y,x)^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

We obtain the same bound for the
(\bigstar)^{2}_{r}
-term by a similar argument. Note that the terms with integer
\beta
can be excluded. Indeed, the only elements of
\mathcal{B}
with integer homogeneity are the polynomials, and
\langle{\sf\Pi}_{y}^{\sf g}X^{k},\partial_{y}^{n}q_{r}(y,\cdot)\rangle=0
for any
|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
by the property (
3.3
). Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{1}\big|(\bigstar)^{2}_{r}\big|\,dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in A,\,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}>\beta+2}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}\int_{r_{0}}^{1}r^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}dr

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in A,\,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}>\beta+2}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}r_{0}^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}{4}}\lesssim d(y,x)^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

To show the intertwining identity (
3.18
), it is sufficient to obtain the estimate

(3.21)
