\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg\langle{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)-\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{n}}{n!}\big(\partial^{n}{\bf K}\big)\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\bigg\rangle\bigg|\lesssim t^{\frac{\gamma+2}{4}}

for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(F,{\sf g})
.
Then the uniqueness of the reconstruction operator gives
{\bf K}(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})
. To prove (
3.21
), we write

\displaystyle{\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle={\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\big(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{f}(x)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle={\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)-{\bf K}\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle={\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)-\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{n}}{n!}\big(\partial^{n}{\bf K}\big)\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x).

We decompose
{\bf K}
into the integral of
K_{r}
over
r\in[0,1]
by (
3.5
) and ignore
P(\partial)K_{1}
as it has a smoothing property. Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}q_{r}(y,\cdot)p_{t}(x,y)dy=q_{r+t}(x,\cdot)
by definition, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg\langle K_{r}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)-\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{n}}{n!}\big(\partial^{n}K_{r}\big)\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\bigg\rangle\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\big|K_{r+t}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x)\big|+\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}t^{\frac{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}\big|\big(\partial^{n}K_{r}\big)\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)(x)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim(r+t)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{4}}+\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2}t^{\frac{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}r^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}.

Since
\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}>-1
, the integral over
r\in[0,t]
gives the upper bound
t^{\frac{\gamma+2}{4}}
. For the integral over
r\in[t,1]
, we use the representation
(\bigstar)^{1}_{r}
wth
n=0
. By the estimate of
(\bigstar)^{1}_{r}
obtained before, we have

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle(\bigstar)^{1}_{r}(\cdot),p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\gamma+2<|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}d(y,x)^{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\left\{r^{\frac{\gamma-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}+\sum_{\beta\in A,\,\beta<\gamma}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}r^{\frac{\beta-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}\right\}p_{t}(x,y)dy

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\gamma+2<|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\left\{t^{\frac{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}r^{\frac{\gamma-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}+\sum_{\beta\in A,\,\beta<\gamma}t^{\frac{\gamma-\beta+|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}}r^{\frac{\beta-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}\right\}.

Then the integral over
r\in[t,1]
gives the upper bound
t^{\frac{\gamma+2}{4}}
.

\rhd

Note that the intertwining relation (
3.18
) between
\bf K
and
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
provides indeed an ‘upgraded’ version of the defining identity (
3.11
) for a
\bf K
-admissible model in
so far as the former reduces to the latter when applied to the modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{f}(x)=\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)=\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}(\tau/\sigma)\sigma
. Indeed, on the one hand we have
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}={\sf\Pi}\tau
. On the other hand
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
has positive regularity and takes its values in a function-like sector when the model takes values in the space of continuous functions, so Corollary
6
applies and identifies the reconstruction of
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
as
{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\big(\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\big)(x)
, equal to
{\sf\Pi}(\mathcal{I}\tau)
, as all the
x
-indexed polynomial terms are null when evaluated at
x
.

3
.
5   Building admissible smooth models

We left aside in Section
3.3
the non-elementary question of existence of non-trivial admissible models to concentrate on their properties. We construct in this section a large class of admissible models for which all
({\sf\Pi}\tau)_{\tau\in T}
and
\big({\sf g}(\sigma)\big)_{\sigma\in T^{+}}
are smooth functions. In applications to singular stochastic PDEs such models can be built from realizations of the noise(s) in the equation.

Recall Assumption
(B1)
in Section
3.2
describes the action of the ‘recentering operator’
\Delta
on elements of
T
of the form
\mathcal{I}_{k}\tau
. We single out for our needs an assumption on
\Delta
that provides a crucial induction structure.

Assumption (B2) –

There exists an increasing sequence
\{\mathcal{B}^{(n)}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
of subsets of
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{(0)}=\{X^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}
,
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
, and

\Delta\tau-\tau\otimes{\bf 1}\in T^{(n-1)}\otimes T^{(n-1)+}

for any
n
and
\tau\in\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
, where
T^{(n-1)}
is the vector space spanned by
\mathcal{B}^{(n-1)}
, and
T^{(n-1)+}
is the subalgebra of
T^{+}
generated by the symbols

\{X_{+}^{e_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq d}\cup\big\{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}\sigma\big\}_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}^{(n-1)},\,k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}.

Here
\mathcal{B}^{(-1)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset
.

Assumptions
(B
1
)
and
(B
2
)
are jointly called Assumption
(B)
. Assumption
(B
2
)
is satisfied by the regularity structures of decorated trees as defined in Section
9
. Indeed, for this case, we can define
\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
as the set of decorated trees
\tau
such that the sum of the number of edges in
\tau
and the total number of
\mathfrak{n}
decorations is
n
. That is,
n
means the ‘complicatedness’ of the tree.
It should be noted that, by the definition (
3.8
), we have

\Delta^{+}\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}\tau\in T^{(n)+}\otimes T^{(n)+}

for any
\tau\in\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
, and thus
\Delta^{+}
is closed within
T^{(n)+}
. Therefore the pair of subspaces

\mathscr{T}^{(n)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(T^{(n)+},T^{(n)}\big),

equipped with the restrictions of the
\Delta^{+}
and
\Delta
maps, is also a regularity structure.

Under Assumptions
(A-B)
, formula (
3.16
) in the proof of Proposition
15
shows that the
\sf g
-map of an admissible model
\sf(\Pi,g)
is uniquely determined by its
\sf\Pi
-map. The following theorem essentially comes from
[
53
, Proposition 3.32]
, which is a generalzation of the ‘Lyons’ extension theorem’.

Theorem 19
.

Let
\mathscr{T}
be a regularity structure satisfying Assumption
(A-B)
and (
3.1
). One can associate to any family
\big([\tau];\tau\in\mathcal{B},|\tau|<0\big)
of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
a unique
\bf K
-admissible model
({\sf g},{\sf\Pi})
on
\mathscr{T}
such that
{\sf\Pi}\tau=[\tau]
, for all
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\tau|<0
.

Proof –

We set the scene for an inductive proof of the statement, taking profit of the induction structure given by Assumption
(B
2
)
.
We will define inductively on
n\in\mathbb{N}
the
\bf K
-admissible models
{\sf M}^{(n)}=({\sf g}^{(n)},{\sf\Pi}^{(n)})
on
\mathscr{T}^{(n)}
over such that

{\sf g}^{(n)}:T^{(n)+}\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}:T^{(n)}\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and

{\sf g}^{(m)}|_{T^{(n)+}}={\sf g}^{(n)},\qquad{\sf\Pi}^{(m)}|_{T^{(n)}}={\sf\Pi}^{(n)}

for any
m>n
. We denote by
\textsf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n)}}
the reconstruction operator associated with the model
{\sf M}^{(n)}
.
Note that the model
{\sf M}^{(0)}
on
\mathscr{T}^{(0)}
is canonically defined by Assumption
(A
2
)
.

\bullet

We now define an extension
{\sf M}^{(n)}
of
{\sf M}^{(n-1)}
on
\mathscr{T}^{(n)}
. It is sufficient to define
{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\tau
and
{\sf g}^{(n)}(\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}\tau)
for
\tau\in\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
.
Recall from the sentences after Theorem
4
that the function

\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\sigma<\tau}{\sf g}_{x}^{(n-1)}(\tau/\sigma)\,\sigma

is an element of
\mathcal{D}^{|\tau|}(T^{(n-1)},{\sf g}^{(n-1)})
and its reconstruction is a candidate of
{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\tau
.
Given that
{\sf g}^{(n-1)}
and
{\sf\Pi}^{(n-1)}
take values in smooth functions, any smooth function is a reconstruction of
\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
for the model
{\sf M}^{(n-1)}
, if
|\tau|<0
. (Recall the reconstruction operator is defined uniquely only when acting on modelled distributions of positive regularity. We are working here with a modelled distribution of negative regularity when
|\tau|<0
.)
Define

{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\tau=\begin{cases}[\tau]&(|\tau|<0),\\
\textsf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}(\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau})&(|\tau|>0).\end{cases}

This is a smooth function in both cases. (Recall that
{\bf 1}
is the only element of
T
of null homogeneity by Assumption
(A
1
)
.) The map
{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}
coincides with
{\sf\Pi}^{(n-1)}
on
T^{(n-1)}
.

\bullet
Define then an extension
{\sf g}^{(n)}
of
{\sf g}^{(n-1)}
to
T^{(n)+}
by requiring that it is multiplicative, and by setting

{\sf g}^{(n)}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\sigma\leq\tau;|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\sigma|+2}\,{\sf g}^{(n-1)}_{x}(\tau/\sigma)\,\partial^{k}\mathbf{K}\Big(\big({\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\big)^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}_{x}\sigma\Big)(x),

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{B}^{(n)}
, in view of (
3.16
).
Note that
(T^{(n-1)+},T^{(n)})
is a regularity structure and
({\sf g}^{(n-1)},{\sf\Pi}^{(n)})
is a model over it.
Closing the induction step amounts to proving that

(3.22)

\big|{\sf g}^{(n)}_{yx}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)\big|\lesssim d(y,x)^{|\tau|+2-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}},

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2
. Look for that purpose at the
T^{(n-1)+}
-valued function

\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(\mathcal{I}^{+}+\mathcal{J}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}(x)\big)\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)+(\mathcal{N}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau})(x),

where
\mathcal{I}^{+}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{+}
is linearly extended by imposing
\mathcal{I}^{+}(X^{k})=0
, the linear map
\mathcal{J}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}:T^{(n-1)}\to T^{+}
is defined by

\mathcal{J}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\sigma\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{X_{+}^{k}}{k!}\partial^{k}\mathbf{K}\Big(\big({\sf\Pi}^{(n-1)}\big)^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}_{x}\sigma\Big)(x),\qquad\sigma\in\mathcal{B}^{(n-1)}

in the same way as
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}
by replacing
{\sf M}
with
{\sf M}^{(n-1)}
and
X^{k}
with
X_{+}^{k}
, and the function
\mathcal{N}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
is defined by

(\mathcal{N}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau})(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}\frac{X_{+}^{k}}{k!}\partial^{k}{\bf K}\Big(\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)-\big({\sf\Pi}^{(n-1)}\big)^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}_{x}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\Big)(x)

in the same way as
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}
by replacing
{\sf M}
with
{\sf M}^{(n-1)}
and
X^{k}
with
X_{+}^{k}
, where
\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
is defined as
{\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\tau
.
Then in the same proof as Theorem
17
, we can prove that
\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}
is an element of
\mathcal{D}^{|\tau|+2}(T^{(n-1)+},{\sf g}^{(n-1)})
and that, denoting by
\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}
the reconstruction operator associated with the model
({\sf g}^{(n-1)},{\sf g}^{(n-1)})
on
(T^{(n-1)+},T^{(n-1)+})
, we have
\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}={\bf K}(\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau})
.
In the proof, Proposition
16
has the role of Corollary
14
.
Since
\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)-\big({\sf\Pi}^{(n-1)}\big)^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}_{x}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)=\big({\sf\Pi}^{(n)}\big)^{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}}_{x}\tau
in the definition of
(\mathcal{N}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau})(x)
, we have

\big(\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}\big)(x)=\mathcal{I}^{+}\big(\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}(x)\big)+\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<|\tau|+2}{\sf g}^{(n)}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)\,\frac{X_{+}^{k}}{k!}.

The
X_{+}^{k}
-component of

\Big(\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}\Big)(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}^{(n-1)}_{yx}}^{+}\Big(\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}\Big)(x)

is then equal to

{\sf g}^{(n)}_{y}(\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}\tau)-\sum_{\eta<\tau}{\sf g}^{(n)}_{x}(\tau/\eta){\sf g}^{(n)}_{yx}(\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}\eta)-\sum_{m}{\sf g}^{(n)}_{x}(\mathcal{I}_{k+m}^{+}\tau)\frac{(y-x)^{m}}{m!}={\sf g}^{(n)}_{yx}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau),

and of size
d(y,x)^{|\tau|+2-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}
, since
\mathcal{K}^{{\sf M}^{(n-1)}+}\boldsymbol{h}^{\tau}\in\mathcal{D}^{|\tau|+2}\big(T^{(n-1)+},{\sf g}^{(n-1)}\big)
. This shows the bound (
3.22
).

\bullet
It remains to show that
{\sf\Pi}
is
\mathbf{K}
-admissible. Given that we assume
\beta_{0}=\min A>-2
, the elements of
T
of the form
\mathcal{I}\tau
have positive homogeneity. So the definition of
{\sf\Pi}
on
\mathcal{I}\tau
comes under the form of the reconstruction of a modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{h}^{\mathcal{I}\tau}
. Since
\boldsymbol{h}^{\mathcal{I}\tau}
is function-like with the
{\bf 1}
-component
{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau)
, by Corollary
7
, it follows that

\displaystyle{\sf\Pi}(\mathcal{I}\tau)(x)=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(\boldsymbol{h}^{\mathcal{I}\tau})(x)={\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}\tau)=\mathbf{K}({\sf\Pi}\tau)(x).

\bullet
The above construction makes it clear that the map
\sf\Pi
is entirely determined from its restriction to the elements of negative homogeneity. The uniqueness part of the statement of the theorem follows then from formula (
3.16
) giving
{\sf g}_{x}(\mathcal{I}^{+}_{k}\tau)
as it shows that the map
\sf g
is entirely determined by the
\sf\Pi
map under the Assumption
(B1-B2)
.

\rhd

4  – Solving singular PDEs within regularity structures

In this section, we formulate singular stochastic PDEs in the sense of modelled distributions. We trade in this section the generality of the above results for the simplicity of an example that contains the main difficulties of the general case. The reader can consult
[
53
]
or
[
16
]
for a description of the general case. We consider the generalized
(KPZ) equation

(4.1)

\begin{split}\big(\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1\big)u&=f(u)\zeta+\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}g_{2}^{ij}(u)(\partial_{x_{i}}u)(\partial_{x_{j}}u)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{1}^{i}(u)(\partial_{x_{i}}u)+g_{0}(u)\\
&=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}f(u)\zeta+g_{2}(u)(\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)^{2}+g_{1}(u)\partial_{x^{\prime}}u+g_{0}(u)\\
&=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}f(u)\zeta+g(u,\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)\end{split}

with a noise
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta_{0}}
. (Remember that the minimum homogeneity in a regularity structure associated with a singular stochastic PDE coincides with the minimum of the regularities of the noises in the equation.) This type of equation appears in a number of problems. If
d=1
and
\zeta
is a space-time white noise then (
4.1
) contains the KPZ equation, which appears in the large scale picture of one-dimensional random interface evolutions. Here
u
is scalar valued but a vector valued case is used in the description of the random motion of a rubber on a manifold
[
55
]
, a random perturbation of the harmonic flow map on loops. If
d=2,3
and
\zeta
is a space white noise, then (
4.1
) contains the generalized PAM

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})u=f(u)\zeta.

The differential equation (
4.1
) with the initial value
u(0,x^{\prime})=u_{0}(x^{\prime})
has an equivalent integral form

u(x)=e^{x_{0}(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)}u_{0}(x^{\prime})+{\bf L}^{-1}\big(f(u)\zeta+g(u,\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)\big)(x).

Under an appropriate setting, the generalized (KPZ) equation (
4.1
) will be lifted to the following equation on modelled distributions
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)

(4.2)

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{h}+\mathcal{P}^{\sf M}\Big(f^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Big),

for some
T_{X}
-valued modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{h}
and an operator
\mathcal{P}^{\sf M}
having the role of
{\bf L}^{-1}
. This section is dedicated to giving the meaning to the equation (
4.2
) and showing that this equation has a unique solution on a small time interval
(0,t_{0})
; this is the content of Theorem
23
, which is the main result of this section. At the end of this section we will be in a position to define the model-dependent solution of the singular equation (
4.1
) as the model-dependent reconstruction
u^{\sf M}={\sf R}^{\sf M}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M})
of the unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M}
of (
4.2
). The function
u^{\sf M}
will then appear as a continuous function of
\sf M
.

The restriction to each band
[0,t_{0}]\times[-R,R]
of spacetime white noise has a norm growing indefinitely as
R
goes to infinity for each fixed
t_{0}>0
. To avoid working with unbounded spacial domains and functional spaces involving spacial weights we will assume that all the objects are
\mathbb{Z}
-periodic in space – they would be
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic in space in a more general setting. The function
\boldsymbol{h}
in (
4.2
) plays the role of the regularity structure lift of the propagator of the initial condition
u_{0}
. The use of time weights to take care of the free propagation
(e^{t(\Delta-1)}u_{0})_{t>0}
of the initial condition in a regularity structures setting is made necessary by the classical sharp estimate

(4.3)

\big\|\partial_{x}^{k}e^{t(\Delta-1)}u_{0}\big\|_{\infty}\lesssim t^{-\frac{(|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}-\alpha)\vee 0}{2}}\,\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Theorem
23
is proved under spacial periodic boundary conditions and in the space of modelled distributions involving temporal weights exploding in
t=0^{+}
. We introduce the former in Section
4.1
and the latter in Section
4.2
. We examine in Section
4.3
the notion of non-anticipative operator, involved in the analysis of equation (
4.2
). We prove in Section
4.4
that (
4.2
) is locally in time well-posed.

4
.
1   Spatially periodic models

We work on the models and modelled distributions that are spacially
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, with
d=1
here – we give the definitions for an arbitrary space dimension
d
. All the results and estimates proved above hold true in the periodic case. For any
x=(x_{0},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, denote by
x+m\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(x_{0},x^{\prime}+m)
.

0
Definition –

A
model

\sf M=(g,\Pi)
is said to be
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic
if
for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\displaystyle{\sf g}_{y+m,x+m}={\sf g}_{yx},\qquad\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x+m}\tau\,,\,\varphi(\cdot+m)\big\rangle=\big\langle{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\tau\,,\,\varphi(\cdot)\big\rangle,

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\tau\in T
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The canonical model
({\sf g},{\sf\Pi})
on the polynomial regularity structure
(T_{X}^{+},T_{X})
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic in the above sense. Note that
{\sf g}_{x}(X_{+}^{n})=x^{n}
and
({\sf\Pi}X^{n})(x)=x^{n}
are
not

\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic functions.
This is the reason why we do not impose periodic conditions on
{\sf g}
and
{\sf\Pi}
.
It is elementary to see that if
\sf M
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic model on
\mathscr{T}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, with

\gamma
positive, then
\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
is also
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, in the sense that

\big\langle\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi(\cdot+m)\big\rangle=\big\langle\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi(\cdot)\big\rangle,

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
– see Proposition 3.38 in
[
53
]
.
All objects in remainder of this section
are implicitly assumed to be
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic.

4
.
2   Modelled distributions with singularity at
x_{0}=0

We use time weights

\omega(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|x_{0}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge 1,\qquad\omega(x,y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\omega(x)\wedge\omega(y)

to treat the boundary condition at
x_{0}=0
.

0
Definition –

Fix two exponents
\eta\leq\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. One defines the space
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
of
modelled distributions with singularity of weight
\eta
at
x_{0}=0
, as the space of functions
\boldsymbol{f}
from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{x_{0}=0\}
into
T_{<\gamma}
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} [\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\max_{\beta<\gamma}\sup_{x\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{f}(x)\|_{\beta}}{\omega(x)^{(\eta-\beta)\wedge 0}}<\infty,\\
\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\max_{\beta<\gamma}\sup_{x,y\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\,d(x,y)\leq\omega(x,y)}\frac{\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}}{\omega(x,y)^{\eta-\gamma}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}}<\infty.\end{aligned}

Set
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
.

One also talks of singular modelled distributions. An example of singular modelled distributions is obtained as follows. Given
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the
T_{X}
-valued function

(4.5)

\displaystyle(P_{\gamma}v)(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}={\bf 1}_{x_{0}>0}\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\partial^{k}\big(e^{x_{0}(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)}v\big)(x)\,\frac{X^{k}}{k!}

belongs to
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
for any
\gamma\geq\eta
. This is
a consequence of the Schauder estimates satisfied by the heat semigroup recalled in (
4.3
) – see e.g. Lemma 7.5 of
[
53
]
.

We recall some embedding theorems. It is easy to see that
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta^{\prime}}}\leq|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
if
\eta^{\prime}\leq\eta
. If
\eta\leq\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma
, we also have

|\!|\!|\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma^{\prime},\eta}}\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

with an implicit constant depending on the model
\sf M
– see e.g. Proposition 3.5 of
[
64
]
. Instead of
[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
, it will be convenient to consider the seminorms

[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\max_{\beta<\gamma}\sup_{x\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{f}(x)\|_{\beta}}{\omega(x)^{\eta-\beta}}

and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
.
In general
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\leq|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}
but the reverse inequality fails. However for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}
such that

\lim_{x_{0}\to 0}\mathcal{Q}_{\beta}\boldsymbol{f}(x)=0

for any
\beta<\eta
, the reverse inequality
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
holds with an implicit constant depending on the model
\sf M
– see e.g. Lemma 6.5 of
[
53
]
.

The reconstruction theorem, Theorem
4
, is extended to singular modelled distributions as follows. See Appendix
C.1
for a detailed proof. An extension to the inhomogeneous integral kernels can be found in
[
64
]
.

Theorem 20
.

Let
{\sf M=(g,\Pi)}
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
such that
-2<\beta_{0}<0
. Assume that
-2<\eta\leq\gamma
, with
\gamma>0
. Then there exists a continuous linear operator

\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}:\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{C}^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that, for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, the bound

(4.6)

\Big|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\partial_{x}^{n}p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\,\omega(x)^{(\eta\wedge\beta_{0})-\gamma}t^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{4}},

holds uniformly over
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g}),x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0<t\leq\omega(x)^{4}
, where the implicit proportional constant depends polynomially on
\|{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}+\|{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}
, and is independent of
\boldsymbol{f}
. Such an operator is unique if the exponent
\gamma
is positive.

The operators discussed in previous sections can be extended to the spaces
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
, as follows. All of the following maps are locally Lipschitz continuous. For the detailed proofs, see
[
53
, Propositions 6.12, 6.13, 6.15, and 6.16]
. Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
the space of modelled distributions
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
of the form

(4.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}=\sum_{\alpha\leq|\tau|<\gamma}f_{\tau}\tau.\vskip 2.84544pt

•

(Proposition
8
’)
Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq 0<\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
, and set
\gamma=(\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{2})\wedge(\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{1})
and
\eta=(\eta_{1}+\alpha_{2})\wedge(\eta_{2}+\alpha_{1})\wedge(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})
.
If a regular product
\star:V\times W\to T
is given, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\gamma_{1},\eta_{1}}(V,{\sf g})\times\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\gamma_{2},\eta_{2}}(W,{\sf g})\ni(\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2})\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}(\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\star\boldsymbol{f}_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g}).

•

(Proposition
9
’)
Let
\gamma>0
and
0\leq\eta\leq\gamma
. If an associative regular product
\star:V\times V\to V
and a smooth function
F
is given, then

\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(V,{\sf g})\ni\boldsymbol{f}\mapsto F^{\star}(\boldsymbol{f})\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(V,{\sf g}).\vskip 2.84544pt

•

(Proposition
10
’)
Let
\gamma>1
. If a derivative
D:T\to T
is given, then

\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})\ni\boldsymbol{f}\mapsto D\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma-1,\eta-1}(T,{\sf g}).\vskip 2.84544pt

•

(Theorem
17
’)
Let
\gamma>0
and
-2<\eta\wedge\beta_{0}
. If
{\sf\Pi}
is
{\bf K}
-admissible,

\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})\ni\boldsymbol{f}\mapsto\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2,\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2}(T,{\sf g}).

A sketch of the proof of Theorem
17
’ is given in Appendix
C.1
. About this statement, note here the gain in the explosion exponent after we applied the operator
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
. We will use this gain in Section
4.4
to gain a small contraction factor in the fixed point formulation of equation (
4.1
) as an equation on a space of modelled distributions.

4
.
3   Non-anticipative operators

A function
f
on
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})^{2}
is said to be
non-anticipative
if
f\big((x_{0},x^{\prime}),(y_{0},y^{\prime})\big)=0
, whenever
x_{0}<y_{0}
. The kernel
P
of the resolution operator
{\bf L}^{-1}
(in the sense that
{\bf L}^{-1}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}P(x-y)f(y)dy
) is of the form

P(x,y)={\bf 1}_{x_{0}>y_{0}}\,p_{x_{0}-y_{0}}(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}),

where
p_{t}
is the kernel of
e^{t(\Delta-1)}
, thus
P
is non-anticipative. The aim of this section is to prove the refined multilevel Schauder estimate (Proposition
22
) associated with the non-anticipative operator
{\bf L}^{-1}
.

We consider the modelled distributions defined on the domain
(0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, for a given positive time
t
. Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
the set of functions
\boldsymbol{f}:(0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to T_{<\gamma}
such that the bounds (
4.4
) hold with the domain of
x,y
restricted to
(0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Denote by

|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}

the associated norms. It is also useful to consider
[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}
and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}
. Since
\omega(x),\omega(y)\leq t^{\frac{1}{2}}
if
x,y\in(0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we have

|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta-\kappa}}^{\prime}\lesssim t^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}

for any
\kappa>0
small enough such that
[\eta-\kappa,\eta)\cap A=\emptyset
. The small factor
t^{\kappa/2}
is used in the fixed point problem in the next section. To apply the reconstruction operator to locally defined modelled distributions, we use the cut-off operator.
The following result is obtained from Proposition
8
’ and Lemma
51
. See Lemma 5.7 of
[
64
]
for the detailed proof.

Proposition 21
.

Let
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
such that
-2<\beta_{0}<0
and let
\gamma>0
and
\eta\leq\gamma\wedge\beta_{0}
.
Fix a smooth non-increasing function
\chi:(0,\infty)\to[0,1]
such that
\chi(t)=1
if
0<t\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\chi(t)=0
if
t\geq 1
.
For each
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we set
\chi_{t}(x)={\bf 1}_{x_{0}>0}\chi(x_{0}/t)
and define

\boldsymbol{\chi}_{t}(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\frac{\partial_{x}^{k}\chi_{t}(x)}{k!}X^{k}.

Then one can define the linear operator
C_{t}:\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t)}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
by

C_{t}\boldsymbol{f}(x)={\bf 1}_{(0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}(x)\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\big(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{t}(x)\star\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big),

and
C_{t}
is uniformly bounded over
t\in(0,1]
and satisfies
(C_{t}\boldsymbol{f})|_{(0,\frac{t}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}=\boldsymbol{f}|_{(0,\frac{t}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}
.

Proposition 22
.

Pick
\gamma>0
,
-2<\eta\leq\beta_{0}<0
, and
0<\rho\leq\gamma+2
. For any
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
and
t\in(0,1]
, there exists a continuous linear map
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}:\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}(T,{\sf g})
such that the following properties hold for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
.

(1)

For any
x\in(0,\frac{t}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, one has
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}(x)-\mathcal{I}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\in T_{X}
.

(2)

For any
\kappa>0
, one has

(4.8)

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2-\kappa}_{(0,t)}}\lesssim t^{\kappa/2}\,|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta}_{(0,t)}},

where the implicit proportional constant is independent of
\boldsymbol{f}
and
t
.

(3)

If
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\tau
happens to be continuous for any
\tau\in T
, then the function
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(C_{t}\boldsymbol{f})
satisfies

(4.9)

\displaystyle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\big(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)(x)=\int_{[0,x_{0}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}P(x,y)\,\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}(y)\,dy.

Proof –

We provide only a sketch here. See Theorem 5.9 of
[
64
]
for the detailed proof.
Recall the decomposition
{\bf L}^{-1}={\bf K}+{\bf K}^{\prime}
.
Noting that
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(C_{t}\boldsymbol{f})\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}
, we denote by
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\sf M}
the lift of the
{\bf K}^{\prime}
operator in the polynomial part of the regularity structure

\big((\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\rho}\partial^{\ell}{\bf K}^{\prime}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)\,\frac{X^{\ell}}{\ell!}\in T_{X}.

Since
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
maps
\mathcal{C}^{\eta}
into
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, we can show that
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}
.
Then we can also show that

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\rho}\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}(C_{t}f)+(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}

by Theorem
17
’.
The property (1) is obvious from the definition. To show the property (3), note that

\displaystyle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}={\bf K}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)+{\bf K}^{\prime}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)={\bf L}^{-1}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big).

Since
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
vanishes on
(-\infty,0)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
by Corollary
5
, we have (
4.9
).
To show the property (2), recall the sufficient condition for the equivalence between two norms
|\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
and
|\!|\!|\cdot|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}^{\prime}
.
Set
\boldsymbol{g}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}
.
By definition,
\boldsymbol{g}
takes values in the function-like sector
V
with
\alpha_{0}(V)=\beta_{0}+2
.
If
\eta\leq-1
, it is sufficient to show that

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\boldsymbol{g}_{\bf 1}(x)=0

for the equivalence between
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}}
and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}}^{\prime}
.
Recall that
\boldsymbol{g}_{\bf 1}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{g}={\bf L}^{-1}\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\big)
.
Since
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}
, we have
\boldsymbol{g}_{\bf 1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta+2}
by Schauder estimate, so it is Hölder continuous. Since
\boldsymbol{g}_{\bf 1}=0
on
(-\infty,0)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
from the non-anticipativity of
K_{\bf L}
, it also vanishes at
x_{0}=0
.
If
-1\leq\eta\leq 0
, we also have
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\boldsymbol{g}_{X^{k}}(x)=0
for any
|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}=1
by a similar argument. By the equivalence between
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}}
and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\rho,\eta+2}}^{\prime}
, we have (
4.8
) as follows.

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\rho,\eta+2-\kappa}(0,T)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\rho,\eta+2-\kappa}(0,T)}^{\prime}\lesssim t^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\rho,\eta+2}(0,T)}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{g}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\rho,\eta+2}(0,T)}\lesssim t^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}(0,T)}.

\rhd

4
.
4   Fixed point solution

Finally we make sense of the equation (
4.2
) and show its local well-posedness.

0
Definition –

A
regularity structure

\mathscr{T}
is said to be
associated with equation (
4.1
)
if it satisfies Assumptions
(A-B)
and contains subcomodules
S,DS,F,N
of
T
satisfying Assumption
(A
1
)
and the following constraints.

•

The symbol
\Xi
and the set
\partial S
are contained in
N
.

•

The sector
S
is function-like and regular products

S\times\cdots\times S\to F,\qquad DS\times DS\to N,\qquad F\times N\to T

are given and satisfy Assumption
(A
3
)
. We denote them all by the same symbol
\star
.

•

Abstract integration operators

\mathcal{I}:T\to S,\qquad\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}:T\to DS,\qquad(1\leq i\leq d)

are given and satisfy Assumption
(B)
.

•

Derivative operators

D_{i}:S\to DS,\qquad(1\leq i\leq d)

are given and satisfy

{\sf\Pi}\circ D_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}\circ{\sf\Pi},\quad\textrm{and}\quad D_{i}X^{k}=k_{i}X^{k-e_{i}}{\bf 1}_{k\geq e_{i}},\quad\text{and}\quad D_{i}\mathcal{I}\tau=\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}\tau.

The element
\Xi
represents the noise
\zeta
. The spaces
S
and
DS
are used to represent the solution
u
and its derivative
\partial_{x^{\prime}}u
, respectively. (The letter
S
is chosen for ‘solution’.) The space
F
are used to represent
f(u)
and
g_{n}(u)
, with
n=0,1,2
. (The
letter
F
is chosen for ‘function’.) The space
N
is used to represent the ‘singular’ elements
\zeta
,
\partial_{x^{\prime}}u
, and
(\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)^{2}
. (The letter
N
is chosen for ‘noise’.) The only role played by the intermediate spaces
DS,F,N
is to clarify on which spaces the product
\star
is defined; they play no other role. We will see in Section
9
how to construct explicitly a regularity structure associated with the generalized (KPZ) equation. The
\star
product is used to define nonlinear images of singular modelled distributions as in Section
2.4
. In this setting, the regularity structure lift of the generalized (KPZ) equation is formulated under the form

(4.10)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{v}&=\boldsymbol{h}+\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}\Big(f^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Big)\\
&=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\Phi_{t}^{\boldsymbol{h},{\sf M}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\end{split}

for appropriate choices of
t\in(0,1]
and
\rho>0
, where
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})
denotes the composition operator (Proposition
9
), and
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\Xi\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star\Xi
, and

\displaystyle g^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma^{\prime}}\big\{g_{2}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D\boldsymbol{v})^{\star 2}+g_{1}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D\boldsymbol{v})+g_{0}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\big\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma^{\prime}}\bigg\{\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}(g_{2}^{ij})^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D_{i}\boldsymbol{v})\star(D_{j}\boldsymbol{v})+\sum_{i=1}^{d}(g_{1}^{i})^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D_{i}\boldsymbol{v})+g_{0}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\bigg\}

for an appropriate
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
– we will choose
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma+\beta_{0}
in the proof of Theorem
23
.

Pick a
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
over
\mathscr{T}
and
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T_{X},\sf g)
. Assume that
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{h},{\sf M}}
sends
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(S,\sf g)
into itself, which turns out to be the
case as proved below under the conditions of Theorem
23
.

0
Definition –

A
solution to equation (
4.2
) on the time interval
(0,t_{0})
is a fixed point of the map
\Phi_{t_{0}}^{\boldsymbol{h},{\sf M}}:\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(S,{\sf g})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(S,{\sf g})
.

Theorem 23
.

Assume that
f
and
g
are smooth functions. Let
\mathscr{T}
be a regularity structure associated with equation (
4.1
) and satisfying Assumptions
(A-B)
, with
\beta_{0}\in(-2,-1)
. Pick
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2]
and
\gamma>-\beta_{0}
. Then for any
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
and any
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T_{X},{\sf g})
, there exists a positive time
t_{0}=t_{0}(\boldsymbol{h},{\sf M})
such that equation (
4.2
) has a unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}
on the time interval
(0,t_{0})
. The time
t_{0}
can be chosen to be a lower semicontinuous function of
\boldsymbol{h}
and
\sf M
.

Proof –

Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
denotes the set of modelled distributions of the form (
4.7
).
Starting from
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(S,{\sf g})
, we show that

f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\Xi+g^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},2\eta-2}(T,{\sf g}).

From the ‘singular/exploding’ version of Proposition
9
given at the end of Section
4.2
, one has
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v}),(g_{2}^{ij})^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v}),(g_{1}^{i})^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v}),g_{0}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\gamma,\eta}(F,{\sf g})
. Since
\Xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\beta_{0}}^{\infty,\infty}(N,{\sf g})
, one has

f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\Xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},\eta+\beta_{0}}(T,{\sf g})\subset\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},2\eta-2}(T,{\sf g})

from the singular version of Proposition
8
given at the end of Section
4.2
. Noting that the smallest homogeneity in the subcomodule
\partial S

is
\beta_{0}+1<0
, which is the homogeneity of
\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}\Xi
, one has
D_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}_{\beta_{0}+1}^{\gamma-1,\eta-1}(\partial S,{\sf g})

and
(D_{i}\boldsymbol{v})\star(D_{j}\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{D}_{2\beta_{0}+2}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},2\eta-2}(N,{\sf g})
. Thus
g^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},2\eta-2}(T,{\sf g})
. From Proposition
22
, one has

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\Phi_{t}^{\boldsymbol{h},\sf M}(v)|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{h}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}+t^{\eta/2}|\!|\!|f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\Xi+g^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v},D\boldsymbol{v})|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma+\beta_{0},2\eta-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{h}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}+t^{\eta/2}C\Big(|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{v}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}}\Big)

for some locally bounded function
C
. Then one can associate with each positive radius
\lambda\gtrsim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{h}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
a time horizon
t(\lambda)
such that
\Phi_{t(\lambda)}^{\boldsymbol{h},\sf M}
sends the ball of
\mathcal{D}_{(0,t(\lambda))}^{\gamma,\eta}(S,{\sf g})
of radius
\lambda
into itself. From the local Lipschitz continuity result, the map
\Phi_{t(\lambda)}^{\boldsymbol{h},\sf M}
is also a contraction on the ball of
\mathcal{D}_{(0,t(\lambda))}^{\gamma,\eta}(S,{\sf g})
of radius
\lambda
. As such, it has a unique fixed point on the ball of radius
\lambda
. An elementary argument gives the uniqueness of a fixed point within
\mathcal{D}_{(0,t(\lambda))}^{\gamma,\eta}(S,{\sf g})
, as in the proof of Theorem 4.7 in
[
52
]
.

\rhd

The proof makes it clear that one can ask
f
and
g
to have finite regularity rather than being smooth. We do not try to optimize the regularity assumptions on
f
and
g
here. Thinking of
\boldsymbol{h}
as the regularity structure lift of the free propagation of an initial condition on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, assuming in
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T_{X},\sf g)
allows us to work with an initial condition of Hölder regularity
\eta
– recall the constraint
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2]
. Note that the map is uniformly contracting on a small enough time interval for
f
and
g
ranging in a bounded set. In order to compare fixed points of
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{h},{\sf M}}
associated with different admissible models over
\mathscr{T}
– hence different maps on different spaces, we use the metric
d_{\gamma}
in Definition
3
with a slight modification to
\mathcal{D}_{(0,t)}^{\gamma,\eta}
norms.
One can then prove the following statement in terms of this metric by making explicit in the reconstruction theorem and the lifting theorem that the operators
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}
and
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}
depend in a locally Lipschitz way on
\sf M
with respect to the pseudo-distance
{\sf d}_{\gamma}
on the space of models over
\mathscr{T}
introduced in (
2.25
). We do not give the details here and refer the reader to the corresponding results in
[
53
]
, Theorem 3.10 and Theorem 5.12 therein.

Proposition 24
.

Given any time
t_{0}^{\prime}<t_{0}(\boldsymbol{h},\sf M)
, the restriction to
(0,t_{0}^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
of
\boldsymbol{u}
defines locally a continuous function of
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T_{X},{\sf g})
and the
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M
.

Together with Theorem
43
in Section
6.3
below and Chandra & Hairer’s convergence result
[
25
]
this continuity result allows to give meaning of the solution to a singular stochastic PDE as a limit in probability of solutions of renormalized equations driven by a mollified noise. This result holds more generally for all the equations that can be treated using regularity structures. Emphasize that this continuity result is fundamental. In a random setting where the noise is random and the models of interest are constructed as measurable functionals of the noise the continuity allows to transport automatically support theorems or large deviation results about random models into corresponding results about the solutions of the regularity structure lifts of the equations under study. See Hairer & Schönbauer’s work
[
59
]
on support theorems, Hairer & Weber’s work
[
61
]
on large deviation results, or Hairer & Mattingly’s work
[
58
]
on the strong Feller property for solutions of singular stochastic PDEs, for a sample.

The last statement of this section makes the link between solving equation (
4.1
) with a smooth noise
\zeta
and the corresponding problem in the regularity structure equipped with the canonical model
{\sf M}^{\zeta}
associated with the smooth noise. The latter is constructed in Section
6.1
and the only thing we presently need to know about it is that its reconstruction map
\textsf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}
is multiplicative with respect to the
\star
-product of modelled distributions, and sends the noise symbol
\Xi
on the smooth function
\zeta
. For positive exponents
\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
and
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2]
, pick
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and denote by
P_{\gamma}v
the lift in the polynomial structure of the heat propagator acting
on
v
, defined by (
4.5
).

Proposition 25
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{(0,t_{0})}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
stand for the solution
in a sufficiently small time interval
(0,t_{0})
of the fixed point problem

(4.11)

\boldsymbol{u}=P_{\gamma}v+\mathcal{P}_{t_{0}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\Big(f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\,\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u}\big)\Big).

Then on the domain
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the function
u\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(C_{t_{0}}\boldsymbol{u})
coincides with the solution to the well-posed equation (
4.1
) with initial condition
v
.

Proof –

As in (
4.9
), the function
u
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle u(x)=Pv(x)+\int_{(0,x_{0})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}P(x,y)\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}C_{t_{0}}\Big(f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u}\big)\Big)(y)\,dy,

with
Pv
the free propagation of the initial condition. We take advantage of the fact that
{\sf M}^{\zeta}
is a smooth model to write

\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(\boldsymbol{w})(x)={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\big(\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)(x)

for any modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{D}^{\alpha,\eta}(T,{\sf g}^{\zeta})
with
\alpha>0
– see identity (
2.37
). Moreover for any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{(0,t_{0})}^{\gamma.\eta}(T,{\sf g}^{\zeta})
, we have
C_{t_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)=\boldsymbol{f}(x)
if
x\in(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Thus, in this domain, we can use the multiplicative character of the map
\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}
and write

\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\big(f(\boldsymbol{u})\big)=f\big(\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u}\big),\quad\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(D\boldsymbol{u})=\partial_{x}\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u}.

as a consequence of Corollary
7
and Proposition
10
, and

\displaystyle\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\Big(f(\boldsymbol{u})\Xi+g\big(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u}\big)\Big)=f\Big(\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\zeta+g\Big(\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{x^{\prime}}\textbf{{R}}^{{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u}\Big).

This finishes the proof.

\rhd

Arrived at that stage we have a model-dependent notion of solution
u^{\sf M}
to
the generalized (KPZ) equation, under the form

u^{\sf M}=\textsf{{R}}^{\sf M}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M}=\Phi^{\boldsymbol{h},\sf M}(\boldsymbol{u}),

indexed by the set of
\bf K
-admissible models
\sf M
on the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
associated with the equation. Theorem
19
gives us a whole family of smooth
\bf K
-admissible models which we can use. However the
\bf K
-admissible models of interest are not smooth as we wish they satisfy the identity
{\sf\Pi}\Xi=\zeta
for a non-smooth noise
\zeta
. The combinatorial structure of the elements of
\mathscr{T}
detailled in Section
9
allows to associate to any regularized version
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
of
\zeta
a
\bf K
-admissible model
{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
such that
{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}\Xi=\zeta_{\varepsilon}
, and
{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
is multiplicative for the
\star
-product on
T
. We will talk of
{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
as the naive interpretation map. However, these models diverge as
\varepsilon>0
goes to
0
. The tools needed to construct some
\varepsilon
-dependent smooth models that have a limit as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
are developed in the next section at the same level of generality as Section
2
and Section
3
. The so-called renormalization operation involved in the construction of these converging
\bf K
-admissible models will be given a dynamical meaning in Section
6
. It is only after Theorem
43
in Section
6
that we will be able to give an answer to the question “
What dynamics does
u^{\sf M}
follow?
” when
{\sf M}
is the renormalized naive model associated with the
\sf\Pi
-map
{{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}}{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
introduced in the next section.

5  – Renormalization structures

We introduce in this section the fundamental notion of renormalization structure, and a notion of compatibility between some regularity and renormalization structures. We emphasized in the previous paragraph that it is generically not possible to define a canonical
\bf K
-admissible model as a limit of the canonical
\bf K
-admissible models associated with regularized noises
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
if the noise(s)
\zeta
is (are) not sufficiently regular. On a technical level, the non-convergence of the models
{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}
is related to the fact that the canonical model is defined by some intricate convolution of kernels that explode on the diagonal. Limit models need to be constructed by probabilistic means as limits in probability of models built from regularized noises,
using a moving window
, as in Meta-Theorem
1
in Section
1
. The implementation of this moving window picture involves the renormalization structures that we introduce in this section. Note that we do not need to know the details of the renormalization operation; the only properties that we need are encoded in the definition of a renormalization structure and the compatibility condition with a regularity structure given below in Definition
26
. An example of renormalization structure will be given in Section
9
, where the renormalization operation will be intimately related to the Taylor expansion procedure.

Renormalization structures are defined in Section
5.1
. If we call the concrete regularity structures from Section
2.2
right regularity structures, then renormalization structures

\mathscr{U}=\big((U,\delta),(U^{-},\delta^{-})\big)

look like left regularity structures, with the difference that elements of the space
U^{-}
have non-positive homogeneities. A fundamental notion of compatibility between renormalization and regularity structures is introduced in Section
5.2
; it accounts for the fact that the renormalization operation induces a renormalization operation on
T^{+}
and ‘commutes’ with the recentering operators
\Delta
and
\Delta^{+}
. This property allows to associate with each model
\sf M
over
\mathscr{T}
and each character
k
on
U^{-}
a new model
{}^{k}{\sf M}
on
\mathscr{T}
. This is the main result of Section
5.2
, Theorem
28
. A large class of characters
k
produces some
\bf K
-admissible models
{}^{k}{\sf M}
if
\sf M
is
\bf K
-admissible.

5
.
1   Definition

A renormalization structure is made up of two ingredients. First, it is a vector space
U
with a basis whose elements are built by induction from elementary elements and multilinear operators giving new elements. The use of the symbol
\tau
for a generic basis vector emphasizes this recursive, tree-like, definition. Each basis vector
\tau
is a placeholder for a function
[\tau]
from
(0,1]
into a Banach space, typically
\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, a Hölder space or an algebra, whose structure as an element of the target space is encoded in the structure of
\tau
. In the cases of interest, the functions
[\tau]
have no limit in
0^{+}
and the basic problem is to remove in a ‘consistent’ way the diverging pieces of these
[\tau]
so as to end up with a collection of functions parametrized by
\varepsilon>0
having a limit where
\varepsilon
goes to
0
. The functions
[\tau]
are then said to have been renormalized. What ‘consistent’ means is part of what follows.

Roughly speaking, the basic operation for renormalizing a placeholder
\tau
consists in removing from
\tau
its different diverging pieces, in all possible sensible ways. This is the second ingredient of a renormalization structure. Tuples of pieces of elements of
U
are not necessarily elements of
U
; we store them in a side space
U^{-}
. Endowing
U^{-}
with an algebra structure allows to store the removed pieces of
\tau
as an element of
U^{-}
under the form of a product. We require nonetheless that any
\tau
amputated from diverging pieces is an element of
U
; this is a restriction on which pieces of any
\tau\in U
can be removed. We thus have a splitting map

\delta:U\rightarrow U^{-}\otimes U,

with
\delta\tau
the sum of all the elements from
U^{-}\otimes U
corresponding to removing from
\tau
all possible diverging allowed pieces, possibly several at a time. The removed pieces may themselves have diverging
subpieces, and it makes sense to assume we have another splitting map

\delta^{-}:U^{-}\rightarrow U^{-}\otimes U^{-},

that extracts them on the left hand side of the tensor product
U^{-}\otimes U^{-}
. That the remaining piece is still in
U^{-}
rather than in another
space is a consistency requirement.

Definition 26
.

A
renormalization structure
is a pair of graded vector spaces

U=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\bigoplus_{\beta\in B}U_{\beta},\qquad U^{-}=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in B^{-}}U_{\alpha}^{-}

such that the following holds.

•

The vector spaces
U_{\alpha}^{-}
and
U_{\beta}
are finite dimensional.

•

The space
U^{-}
is a connected graded bialgebra with unit
{\bf 1}_{-}
, counit
{\bf 1}_{-}^{\prime}
, coproduct

\delta^{-}:U^{-}\to U^{-}\otimes U^{-},

and grading
B^{-}\subset(-\infty,0]
, with
0\in B^{-}
.

•

The index set
B
for
U
is a locally finite subset of
\mathbb{R}
bounded below. The space
U
is a left comodule over
U^{-}
, that is
U
is
equipped with a splitting map
\delta:U\rightarrow U^{-}\otimes U
, which satisfies

(5.1)

\displaystyle(\textrm{\emph{Id}}\otimes\delta)\delta=(\delta^{-}\otimes\textrm{\emph{Id}})\delta,\quad\textrm{and}\quad({\bf 1}_{-}^{\prime}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta=\text{\rm Id}.

Moreover, for any
\beta\in B
, one has

(5.2)

\displaystyle\delta U_{\beta}\subset{\bigoplus_{\alpha\leq 0}}U_{\alpha}^{-}\otimes U_{\beta-\alpha}.

We denote by

\mathscr{U}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big((U,\delta),(U^{-},\delta^{-})\Big)

a renormalization structure.

Similarly to the regularity structure, let
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}^{-}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\beta}
be bases of
U_{\alpha}^{-}
and
U_{\beta}
, respectively, and set

\mathcal{U}^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in B^{-}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}^{-},\qquad\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigcup_{\beta\in B}\mathcal{U}_{\beta}.

Note that, unlike in the definition of a concrete regularity structure satisfying Assumption
(A
1
)
, we do not require that
\mathcal{U}_{0}
is one dimensional in the definition of a renormalization structure. Since all
\alpha\in B^{-}
are non-positive, one has
\beta-\alpha\geq\beta
in (
5.2
). Proposition
48
in Appendix
B
can be applied to the negative grading
B^{-}
of
U^{-}
, and says that
U^{-}
is a Hopf algebra; we denote by
S_{-}
its antipode. Choosing a basis of
U^{-}
provides an associated decomposition of
\delta\tau
of the form

(5.3)

\displaystyle\delta\tau=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\sum_{\varphi\trianglelefteq\tau}\varphi\otimes\tau/^{-}\varphi,

where the
\varphi
are distinct elements of the chosen basis. The notation
\varphi\trianglelefteq\tau
means that
\varphi
is a basis element that appears as one of the left hand side members of the finite sum giving
\delta\tau
. We call
\delta
a
renormalization splitting
and fix throughout a basis of
U^{-}
. The results we prove in the sequel do not depend on that arbitrary choice. Similarly to what we saw in Section
2.2
for the Hopf algebra
(T^{+},\Delta^{+})
, the
\delta^{-}
splitting of the Hopf algebra
(U^{-},\delta^{-})
induces a convolution group law on the set
G^{-}
of characters on
U^{-}

(k_{1}*k_{2})\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(k_{1}\otimes k_{2})\delta^{-}\tau,\qquad(\tau\in U^{-}).

The inverse of a character
k
for the convolution product is explicit and given by
k\circ S_{-}
. Given a character
k
on
U^{-}
, we define a linear map
\widetilde{k}:U\rightarrow U
, setting

\widetilde{k}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(k\otimes\textrm{Id})\delta.

The group
G^{-}
acts on
U
from right. Indeed, as a direct consequence of the comodule property in (
5.1
), one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{1}*k_{2}}

\displaystyle=((k_{1}*k_{2})\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta=(k_{1}\otimes k_{2}\otimes\text{\rm Id})(\delta^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta

\displaystyle=(k_{1}\otimes k_{2}\otimes\text{\rm Id})(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\delta)\delta=(k_{1}\otimes\widetilde{k_{2}})\delta

\displaystyle=\widetilde{k_{2}}\circ\widetilde{k_{1}}

for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in G^{-}
.

5
.
2   Compatible renormalization and regularity structures

We introduce a ‘compatibility’ property between regularity and renormalization structures. We use the notations from Appendix
B
. In particular, given an algebra
A
and two spaces
E,F
, we define a linear map
\mathcal{M}^{(13)}
from the algebraic tensor product
A\otimes E\otimes A\otimes F
to the algebraic tensor product
A\otimes E\otimes F
setting

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\Big(a_{1}\otimes e\otimes a_{2}\otimes f\Big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(a_{1}a_{2})\otimes e\otimes f.

Recall we write
\mathscr{T}=\big((T^{+},\Delta^{+}),(T,\Delta)\big)
for a
regularity structure and
S_{+}
for the antipode map on
T^{+}
.

Definition 27
.

A regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
is said to be
compatible
with a renormalization structure
\mathscr{U}
if the following three compatibility conditions hold true.

(a)

The spaces
T
and
U
coincide as linear spaces and the bases
\mathcal{B}
and

\mathcal{U}
coincide. (Each element
\tau\in\mathcal{B}
is in particular homogeneous
in both
T
and
U
, but it may belong to
\mathcal{B}_{\beta_{1}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\beta_{2}}
with
\beta_{1}\neq\beta_{2}
.) Moreover,

(5.4)

\delta T_{\beta}\subset U^{-}\otimes T_{\beta},\quad\textrm{ for all }\beta\in A.

(b)

There exists an algebra morphism

\delta^{+}:T^{+}\to U^{-}\otimes T^{+}

such that

(5.5)

\big(\textrm{\emph{Id}}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\delta^{+}=\big(\delta^{-}\otimes\textrm{\emph{Id}}\big)\delta^{+},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\big({\bf 1}_{-}^{\prime}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\delta^{+}=\text{\rm Id}

and

(5.6)

\delta^{+}T_{\alpha}^{+}\subset U^{-}\otimes T_{\alpha}^{+},\quad\textrm{ for all
}\alpha\in A^{+}.

(c)

The compatibility conditions

(5.7)

\displaystyle\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{(+)}\big)\delta^{(+)}=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta^{(+)}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\Delta^{(+)}

and

(5.8)

\displaystyle\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\bf 1}_{+}^{\prime}\big)\delta^{+}={\bf 1}_{+}^{\prime}(\cdot){\bf 1}_{-}

hold.

Emphasize the fact that the homogeneity notion in
T
captures the notion of regularity of the associated analytic objects encoded by elements of
T
while the homogeneity notion in
U
captures the diverging behavior of the corresponding regularized objects, as the regularization parameter goes to
0
. It makes sense that the two notions of homogeneities are unrelated. Definition
27
also captures the fact that the renormalization procedure encoded in
\mathscr{U}
induces a renormalization operation on
T^{+}
and commutes with the recentering operators
\Delta
and
\Delta^{+}
. We will see
in Proposition
30
below that the six conditions from Definition
27
hold iff condition (
5.4
) and condition (
5.7
), in its form without the
+
labels, hold, under a reasonable assumption on
\delta^{+}
that holds true for the regularity and renormalization structures associated with (systems of)
singular stochastic PDEs.

Compare conditions (
5.4
) and (
5.2
). Emphasize here as in item
(a)
that the notion of homogeneity is relative to the grading used to define it. An element of
T=U
may thus have different homogeneities, depending on whether it is considered as an element of
T
or
U
. By condition
(a)
, the space
T
is a left
U^{-}
-comodule. The map
\delta^{+}
in
(b)
accounts for the effect in
T^{+}
of the renormalization process. By (
5.5
), the space
T^{+}
is also a left
U^{-}
-comodule. Hence for given a character
k
on
U^{-}
, we can define linear maps
\widetilde{k}:T\to T
and
\widetilde{k}^{+}:T^{+}\to T^{+}
, by

\widetilde{k}=\big(k\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\delta,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{k}^{+}=\big(k\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\delta^{+}.

Properties (
5.4
) and (
5.6
) ensure that homogeneities of elements of
T
and
T^{+}
are stable under these actions.
Condition
(c)
, read with the
+
labels, somehow says that the renormalization operation encoded in
\widetilde{k}
commutes with the Taylor expansion operation on the coefficients of any modelled distribution, encoded in
\Delta^{+}
. Condition
(c)
, read without the
+
labels, says something similar for modelled distributions. Note that the Hopf algebra
T^{+}
is a left
U^{-}
-comodule bialgebra. By Proposition
49
, we have the following compatibility condition on the antipode

(5.9)

\displaystyle\delta^{+}\circ S_{+}=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes S_{+}\big)\circ\delta^{+}.

Recall that given a model
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
on
\mathscr{T}
, the anchored interpretation operator
{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}
associated with
\sf M
is given for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by

{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}=({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1})\Delta.

The next statement and its proof are part of Theorem 6.15 in Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti’s work
[
19
]
on the algebraic renormalization of regularity structures. It tells us that the
\widetilde{k}
and
\widetilde{k}^{+}
maps have jointly a natural and simple action on the space of models on
\mathscr{T}
.

Theorem 28
.

Let a renormalization structure
\mathscr{U}=(U,U^{-})
be compatible with a regularity structure
\mathscr{T}=(T^{+},T)
. Given any character
k

on
U^{-}
, and any model
{\sf M=(g,\Pi)}
on
\mathscr{T}
, define
{}^{k}{\sf M}=({}^{k}{\sf g},{}^{k}{\sf\Pi})
, on
\mathscr{T}
setting

(5.10)

^{k}{\sf M}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big({\sf g}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+},{\sf\Pi}\circ\widetilde{k}\Big).

One has

(5.11)

\big({\sf g}_{y}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)*\big({\sf g}_{x}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)^{-1}={\sf g}_{yx}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+},

and

(5.12)

\Big(\big({\sf\Pi}\circ\widetilde{k}\big)\otimes\big({\sf g}_{x}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)^{-1}\Big)\Delta={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\circ\widetilde{k},

for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, the size conditions (
2.23
) and (
2.24
) hold for
{}^{k}{\sf M}=({}^{k}{\sf g},{}^{k}{\sf\Pi})
, so
{}^{k}{\sf M}
is a model.

Proof –

The proof is short and simple because the notion of compatibility between some regularity and renormalization structures is tailored for that purpose. One has

\begin{split}\big({\sf g}_{y}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)*\big({\sf g}_{x}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)^{-1}&=\big((k\otimes{\sf g}_{y})\delta^{+}\big)\otimes\Big((k\otimes{\sf g}_{x})\delta^{+}\circ S_{+}\Big)\Delta^{+}\\
&\hskip-4.26773pt\overset{\eqref{*EqCompatibilityAntipode}}{=}\big((k\otimes{\sf g}_{y})\delta^{+}\big)\otimes\big((k\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1})\delta^{+}\big)\Delta^{+}\\
&=\big({\sf g}_{y}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)\circ\big(\widetilde{k}^{+}\otimes\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)\Delta^{+}\\
&\hskip-4.26773pt\overset{\eqref{*EqCompatibilityCondition}}{=}\big({\sf g}_{y}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)\circ(k\otimes\Delta^{+})\delta^{+}\\
&={\sf g}_{yx}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}.\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\Big(\big({\sf\Pi}\circ\widetilde{k}\big)\otimes\big({\sf g}_{x}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)^{-1}\Big)\Delta\ \ &\hskip-4.26773pt\overset{\eqref{*EqCompatibilityAntipode}}{=}\Big((k\otimes{\sf\Pi})\delta\otimes(k\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1})\delta^{+}\Big)\Delta\\
&=\big({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)\circ\big(\widetilde{k}\otimes\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)\Delta\\
&\hskip-4.26773pt\overset{\eqref{*EqCompatibilityCondition}}{=}\big({\sf\Pi}\otimes{\sf g}_{x}^{-1}\big)\circ(k\otimes\Delta)\delta\\
&={\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}_{x}\circ\widetilde{k}.\end{split}

The size conditions (
2.23
) and (
2.24
) on
{}^{k}{\sf M}
follow now from formulas (
5.11
) and (
5.12
), and from the fact that the maps
\widetilde{k}
and
\widetilde{k}^{+}
preserve the spaces
T_{\beta}
and
T_{\alpha}^{+}
, respectively, as a consequence of the stability conditions (
5.4
) and (
5.6
).

\rhd

Together with Corollary
6
this statement implies in particular that if the model
\sf M
takes values in the space of
continuous functions
then the reconstruction operator
{}^{k}\textbf{{R}}
associated with the renormalized model is related
to the reconstruction operator
R
associated with the unrenormalized model by the relation

{}^{k}\textbf{{R}}=\textbf{{R}}\circ\widetilde{k}.

This point will be used crucially in Section
6.3
, where we will give a dynamical picture of the renormalization of models.

We consider in the remainder of this section the case of interest for the
study of (systems of) singular stochastic PDE(s) where the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
is built from integration operators and satisfies Assumption
(B)
. Unfortunately, even if a model
\sf M
is
{\bf K}
-admissible,
{}^{k}{\sf M}
is not always
{\bf K}
-admissible for any
k\in G^{-}
. We put forward an assumption under which one builds
{\bf K}
-admissible models using elements
k
of a non-trivial subgroup
G_{\textrm{ad}}^{-}
of
G^{-}
. Assume
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{U}
and let
\mathcal{F}
stand for the family of operators

\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathcal{I}_{p}\}_{|p|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1}\cup\{X^{n}\star\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\setminus\{0\}},

acting on the basis
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{U}
, where
X^{n}\star
denotes the linear operator on
T
defined by
\tau\mapsto X^{n}\star\tau
. Recall that such a multiplication is always given by Assumption
(A
3
)
.

Assumption (C1) –

The regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
is built from integration operators and satisfies Assumption
(B
1
)
and the renormalization structure
\mathscr{U}
is compatible with
\mathscr{T}
. Moreover, the following holds.

•

The algebra
U^{-}
is generated by the basis elements
\mathcal{U}_{<0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigcup_{\alpha<0}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
and the unit
\textbf{{1}}_{-}
.

•

Let
\mathfrak{J}^{-}
be the ideal of
U^{-}
generated by the set
\big(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{U})\big)\cap\mathcal{U}_{<0}
. The linear map
\delta:U\to U^{-}\otimes U
satisfies, for any operator
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{U}
,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\delta(F\tau)-(\text{\rm Id}\otimes F)\delta\tau\in\mathfrak{J}^{-}\otimes U.

•

We define a projection operator
P_{-}:U\to U^{-}
setting
P_{-}\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\tau{\bf 1}_{\tau\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}}
, for any
\tau\in\mathcal{U}
. The linear map
\delta^{-}:U^{-}\to U^{-}\otimes U^{-}
is defined by
\delta^{-}=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes P_{-})\delta
on
\mathcal{U}_{<0}
and its multiplicative extension.

Define the subset
G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
of
G^{-}
by

G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big\{k\in G^{-}\,;\,k\big(F(\tau)\big)=0\ \text{for any}\ F(\tau)\in\big(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{U})\big)\cap\mathcal{U}_{<0}\Big\}.

Proposition 29
.

The set
G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
is a subgroup of
G^{-}
, and for any
k\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
and any
\bf K
-admissible model
\sf M
, one has
{}^{k}{\sf M}
is also
\bf K
-admissible. The group
G^{-}_{\textrm{\emph{ad}}}

is called the
renormalization group
.

The definition of the group
G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
gives the meaning to assumption (
5.13
). Up to irrelevant terms for
k\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
, the renormalization operations in
U^{-}
or
U
of
an ‘integral’ is the integral of its renormalized integrand, and multiplication by a polynomial has no effect on the renormalization process.

Proof –

Note that
k(\mathfrak{J}^{-})=0
for any
k\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
. Let
\tau
be an element of
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
such that
F\tau\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}
.

(a)

Given
k,h\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
since identity (
5.13
) and the third property of Assumption
(C
1
)
ensures that
(k*h)(F\tau)=(k\otimes h)\delta^{-}(F\tau)=0
, for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
k*h\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
. Next we show that
k^{-1}=k\circ S_{-}\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
. Denote by
\mathcal{M}^{-}
the multiplication operator in
U^{-}
and pick
\sigma\in U_{\beta}
with
\beta<0
. Since
\delta\sigma\in{\bf 1}_{-}\otimes\sigma+\sum_{\alpha<0}U_{\alpha}^{-}\otimes U_{\beta-\alpha}
, by applying the operator
\mathcal{M}^{-}(\text{\rm Id}\otimes S_{-}P_{-})
to (
5.13
) we have from Assumption
(B1)
and the fact that
\mathfrak{J}^{-}
is an ideal

S_{-}(F\sigma)\in\sum_{\alpha<0}\mathcal{M}^{-}\Big(U_{\alpha}^{-}\otimes S_{-}\big(P_{-}FU_{\beta-\alpha})\big)\Big)+\mathfrak{J}^{-},

which implies
k^{-1}(FU_{\beta})=k(S_{-}FU_{\beta})=0
, by an induction on
\beta
.

(b)

Let
F=\mathcal{I}_{p}
. By (
5.13
),

{}^{k}{\sf\Pi}(\mathcal{I}_{p}\tau)=(k\otimes{\sf\Pi})\delta\mathcal{I}_{p}\tau=(k\otimes{\sf\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{p})\delta\tau=\partial^{p}{\bf K}(k\otimes{\sf\Pi})\delta\tau=\partial^{p}{\bf K}({}^{k}{\sf\Pi}\tau).

Since we have a similar identity for
F=X^{n}\star
, we obtain that
{}^{k}{\sf M}
is admissible.

\rhd

We end this section by showing that the definition of compatible renormalization and regularity structures takes then a simple form under the following additional mild assumption. It essentially says that multiplications by a polynomial and integrations are not the sources of renormalization problems.

Assumption (C2) –

The algebra morphism
\delta^{+}:T^{+}\to U^{-}\otimes T^{+}
, is determined by the identities

(5.14)

\displaystyle\delta^{+}X_{+}^{\ell}={\bf 1}_{-}\otimes X_{+}^{\ell},\qquad\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{p}^{+}\tau)=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{p}^{+})\delta\tau.

Proposition 30
.

Under Assumption
(C
2
)
, assume
\mathscr{T}
satisfies the property (
5.4
) and the version of identity (
5.7
) without the
+
labels. Then the other conditions in Definition
27
follow automatically.

Proof –

The comodule property (
5.5
) follows from (
5.1
) and the definition (
5.14
).
Indeed,

\displaystyle\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\delta^{+}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\big)\delta\tau=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\delta)\delta\tau

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\big)(\delta^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta\tau=\big(\delta^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\big)\delta\tau

\displaystyle=\big(\delta^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau).

The counit part of (
5.5
) and (
5.8
) are left to readers.
The condition (
5.6
) follows from (
5.4
) and the definition (
5.14
).
The
(+)
-labelled version of (
5.7
) is checked for
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta^{+}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\Delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\left(\big(\delta^{+}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\delta^{+}\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\left(\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+})\delta\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}{\bf 1}_{-}\otimes\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+})\delta\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta^{+})\Delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\varphi\trianglelefteq\tau}\varphi\otimes\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}(\tau/^{-}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta)\delta\tau+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\varphi\trianglelefteq\tau}\varphi\otimes\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}(\tau/^{-}\varphi),

and

\displaystyle\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+}\big)\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+}\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+})\delta\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{\varphi\trianglelefteq\tau}\varphi\otimes\left((\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta(\tau/^{-}\varphi)+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{X_{+}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n+\ell}^{+}(\tau/^{-}\varphi)\right),

hence we have

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta^{+}\otimes\delta^{+}\big)\Delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau)=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+}\big)\,\delta^{+}(\mathcal{I}_{n}^{+}\tau).

It remains to prove (
5.5
) and (
5.7
) for elements of the form
X_{+}^{n}\tau
. This is elementary using the multiplicative property of
S^{+}
and
\Delta^{+}
.

\rhd

6  – Multi-pre-Lie structure and renormalized equations

Let us summarize the successive steps that we have followed after the formalism of regularity structures was set up in Section
2
. We described in Section
3
a particular class of regularity structures, and the class of admissible models on them, that have the property that one can lift the integral operator
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})^{-1}
into an operator on some spaces of modelled distributions that has a Schauder-type continuity property given in Theorem
17
. This result played a crucial role in the local in time well-posedness result proved in Theorem
23
of Section
4
. It gives us a modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M}
that solves a well-defined regularity structure formulation of a fixed point formulation of an ill-defined singular (stochastic) PDE. We define the (model-dependent) solution of this ill-defined equation as the (model-dependent) reconstruction
u^{\sf M}={\sf R}^{\sf M}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M})
of
\boldsymbol{u}^{\sf M}
. To make this definition consistent with the initial objective we would like to use some models
\sf M
for which
{\sf\Pi}(\Xi)=\zeta
. The construction of some admissible model that has this property is made very non-trivial by the fact that
\zeta
has low regularity. As a matter of fact, this cannot be done in a deterministic reasonable way but one can construct some random admissible models that are limits in a probabilistic sense of some smooth models built from the canonical admissible model
{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}
associated with a regularized noise
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
. The construction recipe
({\sf M},k)\mapsto{}^{k}{\sf M}
for these renormalized models was given in Theorem
28
in Section
5
. Denote by
{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}=({\sf g}^{\varepsilon},{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon})
the canonical admissible model associated with a regularized noise
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
. We will see in Section
7
that there is a particular choice of character
k_{\varepsilon}
for which the
{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}(\tau)(x)
are centered for all the
\tau
of negative homogeneity and all state space point
x
. Chandra & Hairer first proved the probabilistic convergence of the renormalized admissible models
{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}
to some limit admissible random model
\overline{\sf M}
. We will not prove this result in the tourist guide and refer the reader to the review
[
11
]
for some information on this matter. Rather we will see in the present section that one can give a somewhat explicit description of the dynamics of
u^{\overline{\sf M}}
based on the following facts.

(a)

The continuity of the map
