{\sf M}\mapsto u^{\sf M}
ensures that the dynamics of
u^{\overline{\sf M}}
is the limit of the dynamics of the
\overline{u}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=u^{{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}}
.

(b)

We will see in the present section that
\overline{u}_{\varepsilon}
is actually the solution of an explicit stochastic PDE driven by the regularized noise
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
, called the
renormalized equation
. This is the main result of this section, stated in Theorem
43
.

We will concentrate in this section on the study of the generalized (KPZ) equation

(6.1)

\begin{split}\big(\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1\big)u&=f(u)\zeta+g_{2}(u)(\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)^{2}+g_{1}(u)(\partial_{x^{\prime}}u)+g_{0}(u)\\
&=f(u)\zeta+g(u,\partial_{x^{\prime}}u),\end{split}

with a given initial condition. It already involves the main difficulties
of the most general situation, with the advantage of leaving aside a number of purely technical and notational matters compared to the most general situation.

1. Picard iteration and decorated trees.
We saw in Section
4
that a there is a unique modelled distribution

\boldsymbol{u}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B}}u_{\tau}\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{(0,t_{0})}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g),

with
\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
and
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2]
solving the lift (
4.10
) in the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
of equation (
6.1
). It satisfies on the domain
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
the fixed point problem

(6.2)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}&\simeq\mathcal{I}\Big(f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\Xi+g_{2}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\star(D\boldsymbol{u})^{\star 2}+g_{1}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\star D\boldsymbol{u}+g_{0}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\Big)\\
&\simeq\frac{f^{(k)}(u)}{k!}\,u_{\tau_{1}}\cdots u_{\tau_{k}}\,\mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}\Xi\big)+\frac{g_{2}^{(k)}(u)}{k!}\,u_{\tau_{1}}\cdots u_{\tau_{k}}u_{\sigma_{1}}u_{\sigma_{2}}\mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}D_{i}\sigma_{1}D_{j}\sigma_{2}\big)\\
&\quad+\frac{g_{1}^{(k)}(u)}{k!}\,u_{\tau_{1}}\cdots u_{\tau_{k}}u_{\sigma_{1}}\,\mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}D_{i}\sigma_{1}\big)+\frac{g_{0}^{(k)}(u)}{k!}\,u_{\tau_{1}}\cdots u_{\tau_{k}}\,\mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}\big),\end{split}

up to some model-dependent non-trivial polynomial components, with
\tau_{k},\sigma_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B}
, and implicit sums over
\mathcal{B}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. We see on this identity that
T
needs at least to be stable by the operations

\big(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\big)\mapsto\mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}\big),\ \mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}\Xi\big),\ \mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}D_{i}\sigma_{1}\big),\ \mathcal{I}\big(\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{k}D_{i}\sigma_{1}D_{j}\sigma_{2}\big);

this naturally endows the elements of
T
with a tree/inductive structure. This fact is common to all the equations that can be treated by the methods of regularity structures. This leads us in Section
6.1
to set the framework of
rooted decorated trees
as a convenient encoding of the elements of
T
.

2. Decorated trees and pre-Lie algebras.
The importance of this algebraic setting comes from the fact that the vector space
V
spanned by the set of all rooted trees with vertex and edge decorations in some given sets happens to be a universal object in a class of algebraic structures called multi-pre-Lie algebras. Morphisms of such multi-pre-Lie algebras defined on
V
are thus determined by their restrictions to a set of generators. We show in Section
6.2
that the modelled distribution solution of the regularity structure lift of equation (
6.1
) involves precisely such a morphism, with values in the space of vector fields; see Proposition
36
.

3. Pre-Lie algebras and renormalization.
The regularity structure associated with equation (
6.1
) is built from
V
, with
T
and
T^{+}
subsets of
V
. Building
\mathscr{T}
within
V
, any renormalization structure
\mathscr{U}
compatible with
\mathscr{T}
and satisfying Assumption
(C)
will also be built within
V
, with
U
and
U^{-}
some subsets of
V
. Theorem
43
below shows that
\overline{u}_{\varepsilon}
is the solution of an explicit equation driven by
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
. This result was first proved in the seminal work
[
16
]
of Bruned, Chandra, Chevyrev and Hairer. The proof builds on the fact that the dual renormalization map
\widetilde{k}^{*}
that one can associate to any
k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
happens to be a multi-pre-Lie morphism under a
compatibility condition
on the multi-pre-Lie structure and the renormalization operator
\delta
, found here under the form of Assumption
(D3)
.

Assumptions
(D1-D3)
to be found in this section are
all met in the case of a general subcritical system of singular stochastic PDEs, and we verify them by hand in Section
9
where we construct the regularity and renormalization structures associated with the generalized (KPZ) equation. We emphasize them here as ‘assumptions’ to stress the mechanics at work in the most general case.

6
.
1   Free
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra generated by
\sf N

We introduce in the first paragraph the space of edge and node decorated trees. Decoration spaces are associated to any given system of singular stochastic PDEs, and the associated space of decorated trees provides the background scene from which one can define the regularity and renormalization structures associated with the system. The multi-pre-Lie structure of the space of decorated trees is introduced in another paragraph and its dual operator described explicitly.

§
Decorated trees

0
Definition –

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
(called a
node type set
) and
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
(called an
edge type set
) be abstract sets.

•

A
rooted tree

\tau
is a finite connected non-planar graph without loops, with a node set
N_{\tau}
and an edge set
E_{\tau}
, and with a distinguished node
\rho_{\tau}
, called the
root
. The root defines a natural order on each edge, from the root to the leaves. In particular, each edge
e\in E_{\tau}
is written as the form
e=(u,v)
, where
u,v\in N_{\tau}
are endpoints of
e
and
u
is closer to the root.
u
is called a parent of
v
, and
v
is called a child of
u
.

We identify two trees
\tau
and
\sigma
if they are graph isomorphic, so we always write a graph by putting ancestors lower and descendants upper. The root is put at the bottom. Here is an example.

\rho_{\tau}

•

A
typed rooted tree
is a rooted tree with type
maps
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm n}:N_{\tau}\to\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm e}:E_{\tau}\to\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
.
Moreover, a
rooted decorated tree
is a typed rooted tree
\tau
with two maps

\mathfrak{n}:N_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1},\qquad\mathfrak{e}:E_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}.

We denote a generic typed rooted tree by Greek letters like
\tau
, and a generic rooted decorated trees with two decorations
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{e}
by
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
or a bold letter
\boldsymbol{\tau}
.

We will consider later rooted trees
\tau
equipped with three decorations
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e}
– see Section
9.1

for the precise definitions. In this section, we hide the
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration in the node type map, so we consider the type sets

\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}=\{\bullet,\circ\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}.

The node type
\bullet
represents the monomial
{\bf 1}=X^{0}
, and
\circ
represents the noise
\Xi
. The third node type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
is a node with the
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration
\alpha
. The set
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
labels the set of differential operators involved in
the system of equations under study. There is a single operator
\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1
in the example of the generalized (KPZ) equation (
6.1
), so the set
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
consists of only one element, associated with the integration operator
\mathcal{I}
in that case. If we consider a system of singular stochastic PDEs involving different operators, different operators
\mathcal{I}
’s would be associated with each of them and the set
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
would collect them all.

An element
X^{n}\in T
is denoted by
\bullet^{n}
, that is a graph with only one node with the type
\bullet
and the
\mathfrak{n}
-decoration
n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. An edge with
\mathfrak{e}
-decoration
p\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
represents the operator
\mathcal{I}_{p}
, with the notations of Section
3.2
, for one of the operators
\mathcal{I}
involved in the equation.

All operations appearing in the equation (
6.2
) are graphically defined as follows. In the following pictures, types and decorations are omitted unless necessary, and the root of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
in the first bullet and of
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
in the third bullet is denoted by a square.

•

The integration
\boldsymbol{\tau}\mapsto\mathcal{I}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
is given by the map connecting the root of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
with a new node, which becomes a root of the tree
\mathcal{I}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
, and giving the
\mathfrak{e}
-decoration
p\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
to the connecting edge.

•

The product of trees
\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{j})
(
j=1,\dots,m
) is given by the
tree product
, that is joining their roots.

Thus we see that the rooted trees obtained by the above operations are sufficient to describe the fixed point problem (
6.2
).
The symbol
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
does not come from the fixed point problem (
6.2
), but its use is made clear in Section
6.3
. As we concentrate in this section on the generalized (KPZ) equation the edge type set
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
will consist of a single element, suggestively denoted by
\mathcal{I}
. There is no difficulty in working with a finite edge type set.

0
Definition –

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the set of
all
rooted decorated trees with type sets

\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}=\{\bullet,\circ\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}=\{\mathcal{I}\}
, and let
V
be the vector space spanned by
\mathcal{V}
.
Moreover, denote by
\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}:V\to\mathbb{R}\big)_{\tau\in\mathcal{V}}
the dual basis of
\mathcal{V}
and let
V^{*}
be the vector space spanned by
\{\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}}
.

Throughout this section we view each element of
\mathcal{V}
as the rooted tree
\tau
with the composite decorations
(\mathfrak{t}_{\rm n},\mathfrak{n}):N_{\tau}\to{\sf N}
and
(\mathfrak{t}_{\rm e},\mathfrak{e}):E_{\tau}\to{\sf E}
, where

{\sf E}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}\simeq\mathbb{N}^{d+1},\quad{\sf N}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}.

The set
{\sf N}
is considered as a subset of
\mathcal{V}
consisting of simple trees

{\sf N}=\{\mathfrak{b}^{n}\}_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n},n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}=\big\{\circ^{\ell},\bullet^{m},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{n,\alpha}\big\}_{\ell,m,n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1},\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}.

Write
{\sf N}^{0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathfrak{b}^{0}\}_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}}\simeq\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
. We introduce a few notations. Note that each
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
has a decomposition of the form

(6.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathcal{I}_{p_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})\star\cdots\star\mathcal{I}_{p_{a}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a})

with
\mathfrak{b}^{n}\in{\sf N}
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}\in\mathcal{V}
. Taking care of the number of automorphisms of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
that leave it fixed, for
\boldsymbol{\tau}
of the form

(6.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\big(\mathcal{I}_{q_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})\big)^{\star m_{j}},

with
(q_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i})\neq(q_{j},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})
for any
i\neq j
, define inductively

S(\boldsymbol{\tau})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=n!\prod_{j=1}^{b}S(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})^{m_{j}}\,m_{j}!.

Then we define the paring
\langle\!\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\!\rangle
between
V
and
V^{*}
by

(6.5)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=S(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau})

for
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}
. We see
V^{*}
as a part of the algebraic dual of
V
. (As
V
is infinite-dimensional,
V^{*}
is not equal to the full algebraic dual of
V
.) The ‘copy’ space
V^{*}
will play an important role in the second half part of this section.

§
Canonical model

Given a
smooth
noise
\zeta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define the canonical operator
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
on the whole of
V
requiring that it is multiplicative with respect to the
\star
product and setting, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}(\circ^{n})(x)=x^{n}\zeta(x),\qquad{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}(\bullet^{n})(x)={\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{n,\alpha})(x)=x^{n},

and

{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}(\mathcal{I}_{p}\boldsymbol{\tau})=\partial^{p}{\bf K}({\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}),

for all
n,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\tau},p
. The regularity structures we will work with have spaces
T
and
T^{+}
that are subsets of
V
. Since all functions
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}
are smooth, the restriction of
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
to

T_{<0}
defines the
canonical model

{\sf M}^{\zeta}=\sf\big(g^{\zeta},{\Pi}^{\zeta}\big)

on the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
, from Theorem
19
. Things are explicit here as the multiplicativity and the
\bf K
-admissibility properties fix the definition of
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
on all decorated trees in
V
. Emphasize the fact that since the map
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
is multiplicative its associated reconstruction map is also
multiplicative.

§
Multi-pre-Lie algebras

We first recall the definition of a multi-pre-Lie algebra, referring the reader to Foissy’s article
[
40
]
for basics on multi-pre-Lie algebras. All we need to know on the subject is the following definition and the result of Proposition
32
below.

0
Definition –

Let
\sf{E}
be a set.
A vector space
W
, equiped with a family
(\rightslice_{\sf{e}})_{\sf{e}\in\sf{E}}
of bilinear maps from
W\times W
into
W
, is called an
\sf{E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra
if one has

(a\rightslice_{\sf{e}}b)\rightslice_{\sf{e}^{\prime}}c-a\rightslice_{\sf{e}}(b\rightslice_{\sf{e}^{\prime}}c)=(b\rightslice_{\sf{e}^{\prime}}a)\rightslice_{\sf{e}}c-b\rightslice_{\sf{e}^{\prime}}(a\rightslice_{\sf{e}}c),

for all
a,b,c\in W
, and
\sf{e},\sf{e}^{\prime}\in\sf{E}
.

The two arguments of a pre-Lie product
a\rightslice_{\sf{e}}b
do not play a symmetric role, and we think here of
a
as acting on
b
via the operator
\rightslice_{\sf{e}}
; we read
a\rightslice_{\sf{e}}b
from left
to right. Here is an example of
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra. Take
\sf E
finite, identified with
\{1,\dots,|\sf E|\}
, and consider the space of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{|\sf E|}
. Then the family of differentiation operators

G\triangleright_{\sf e}H\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=G\partial_{x_{\sf e}}H

defines an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra. If
\sf E
consists of a single
element
\rightslice
, this operator is called a
pre-Lie product
, and a vector space equipped with a pre-Lie product is called a
pre-Lie algebra
. Any pre-Lie algebra is Lie-admissible, in the sense that the map
(a,b)\mapsto a\rightslice b-b\rightslice a
defines a Lie bracket.
The relevance of the multi-pre-Lie structure in the study of singular stochastic PDEs comes from Proposition
36
in the next section, as it identifies the components
u_{\tau}
of solutions
\boldsymbol{u}=\sum u_{\tau}\tau
regularity structures lifts of a singular stochastic PDEs as
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra morphisms.

We define now the multi-pre-Lie structure in the space
V^{*}
. The reason for working on
V^{*}
rather than on
V
will appear clearly in Section
6.2
and Section
6.3
. The spaces
V
and
V^{*}
being infinite dimensional, the symbol
\otimes
denotes below the algebraic tensor product of these spaces with themselves, without any completion.

0
Definition –

Given
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}\simeq\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, a node
v
of a decorated tree
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
, denote by

\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\rightarrow}_{(v)}\boldsymbol{\sigma},

the element of
\mathcal{V}
obtained by grafting
\boldsymbol{\tau}
on the node
v
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, along an edge of
\mathfrak{e}
-decoration
{\sf e}
.
Define also

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{(v)}\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1};m\leq\mathfrak{n}(v)\wedge p_{\sf e}}\binom{\mathfrak{n}(v)}{m}\,\boldsymbol{\tau}\xrightarrow{{\sf e}-m}_{(v)}\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}-m{\bf 1}_{v}}\in V,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{v\in N_{\sigma}}\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{(v)}\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in V,

where
{\bf 1}_{v}
is the indicator function of
v
. Recall that the binomial coefficient of multiindices is defined at the end of Section
1
. Finally, define a linear map

\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}:V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\rightarrow V^{*}

by

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{*},\qquad\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V},

where the map
(\cdot)^{*}:V\to V^{*}
is the linear extension of the map
\mathcal{V}\ni\boldsymbol{\tau}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\in V^{*}
.

[

]
. A proof can be found in Appendix

.

Proposition 31
.

The space
V^{*}
with the operators
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\}_{{\sf e}\in{\sf E}}
is the free
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra generated by
{\sf N}
, in the sense that the universal property
(b)
in Appendix
C.2
holds.

Any morphism from
V^{*}
into an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra is thus determined by its restriction to the generators
{\sf N}
of
V^{*}
. This is the universal property of the free
\sf{E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra with generators
\sf N
. In particular if two
\sf{E}
-multi-pre-Lie morphisms from
V^{*}
into the same
\sf{E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra coincide on the generators of
V^{*}
then they are equal.

The space
T=U
of the regularity and renormalization structures associated with the generalized (KPZ) equation is a subspace of
V
with each space
T_{\beta},T^{+}_{\alpha},U_{\beta^{\prime}},U^{-}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
spanned by finitely many rooted decorated trees. Denote by
\pi_{U}:V\to U
the canonical projection. The next assumption is a piece of properties to be satisfied by the basis
\mathcal{B}
of
T
and
U
. In Section
9.1
,
\mathcal{B}
is defined as the set of all trees
strongly conforming
to the rule. The first one means that the
\mathfrak{n}
-decoration is independent of the rule and the second one means that the rule is local. The last one describes that the projection map
\pi_{U}
behaves consistently with respect to all the grafting products
\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}
.

Assumption (D1) –

The homogeneous basis
\mathcal{B}
of
T
and
U
is a subset of
\mathcal{V}
with the following properties. (Recall that the notions of homogeneity in
T
and
U
are different.)

•

If
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{B}
for any
\mathfrak{m}:N_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
.

•

If
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\mathfrak{b},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}\in\mathcal{B}
.

•

For any
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}
and
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
,

\pi_{U}\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}(\pi_{U}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\big)=\pi_{U}\big((\pi_{U}\boldsymbol{\tau})\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\big)=\pi_{U}\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\big).

Set

U^{*}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}\big\{\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\,;\,\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}\big\}

and denote by
\pi_{U^{*}}:V^{*}\to U^{*}
be the canonical projection. Then we
define the map

\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\,:U^{*}\otimes U^{*}\to U^{*}

setting

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{U^{*}}\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big).

The following statement is proved in Appendix
C.2
.

Proposition 32
.

Under Assumption
(D
1
)
, the space
U^{*}
with the operators
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\}_{{\sf e}\in{\sf E}_{\leq 1}}
is the
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra generated by
{\sf N}\cap\mathcal{B}
.

Finally we define an operator playing the role of ‘antiderivative’.

0
Definition –

For each
i\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}
, define the linear map
\uparrow_{i}:V^{*}\to V^{*}
by

\uparrow_{i}(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}})^{*}=\sum_{v\in N_{\tau}}(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}+e_{i}\mathbf{1}_{v}})^{*}.

The map
\uparrow_{i}
sends
U^{*}
into itself under Assumption
(D
1
)
. We denote by

\downarrow_{i}:V\to V

the dual map of
\uparrow_{i}:V^{*}\to V^{*}
under the pairing (
6.5
), that is,

\langle\!\langle\,\downarrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau},\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle,

for any
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Moreover, we extend the pairing (
6.5
) into a pairing between
V\otimes V
and
V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
setting

\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\,\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle.

Under such pairings, denote by

\updownharpoons_{\sf e}:V\rightarrow V\otimes V,

the dual map of
\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\,:V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\to V^{*}
, that is,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle,

for any
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{V}
and
{\sf e}\in\sf E
. The following explicit formulas for
\downarrow_{i}
and
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
are
helpful to get a graphical image. It is used only in the proof of Theorem

47
giving an explicit construction of the regularity and renormalization structures associated with the generalized (KPZ) equation.

Lemma 33
.

For any
i\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}
and any
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathcal{V}
, one has

\displaystyle\downarrow_{i}\big(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\big)=\sum_{v\in N_{\tau},\,e_{i}\leq\mathfrak{n}(v)}\mathfrak{n}(v)\,\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}-e_{i}\mathbf{1}_{v}}.

Moreover for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathcal{V}
and any
\sf e\in E
one has

(6.7)

\displaystyle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e=(v,w)\in E_{\tau};\mathfrak{e}(e)\leq{\sf e}}\frac{1}{({\sf e}-\mathfrak{e}(e))!}(C_{e}\tau)_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes(P_{e}\tau)_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}+({\sf e}-\mathfrak{e}(e)){\bf 1}_{v}},

where
C_{e}\tau
and
P_{e}\tau
are the two connected components of the graph
\tau\setminus\{e\}
, with
P_{e}\tau
containing the root of
\tau
.
(Again, recall that the factorial of a multiindex is defined at the end of Section
1
.)

Proof –

We show that the equation (
6.6
) holds for the map
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
defined by the second formula (
6.7
). The first formula is proved by a similar argument. Note that, for any elements
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\in\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mathfrak{u}^{m}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\mathcal{I}_{q_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})\in\mathcal{V}
, one has

(6.8)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle={\bf 1}_{\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{u},n=m,a=b}\,n!\sum_{s\in S_{a}}\prod_{i=1}^{a}{\bf 1}_{p_{i}=q_{s(i)}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(i)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle,

where
S_{a}
is the symmetric group of the set
\{1,2,\dots,a\}
. For
\boldsymbol{\eta}
of the form
\boldsymbol{\eta}={\mathfrak{b}}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})
,
we divide the right hand side of (
6.7
) according to the edge
e
is connected to the root or not and have

\displaystyle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}\boldsymbol{\eta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{({\sf e}-p_{i})!}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{n+{\sf e}-p_{i}}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j:j\neq i}\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j})+\sum_{i}\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{2})\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j:j\neq i}\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\updownharpoons_{\sf e}^{1}\boldsymbol{\eta}+\updownharpoons_{\sf e}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta},

where we write
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{2}
following Sweedler’s notation in the first equality. Similarly for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mathfrak{u}^{m}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\mathcal{I}_{q_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})\in\mathcal{V}
one has

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}\binom{m}{\ell}\mathfrak{u}^{m-\ell}\star\mathcal{I}_{{\sf e}-\ell}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\mathcal{I}_{q_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{b}\mathfrak{u}^{m}\star\mathcal{I}_{q_{j}}\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}\big)\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{k;k\neq j}\mathcal{I}_{q_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{1}\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{2}\boldsymbol{\sigma}.

Hence it is sufficient to show that

(6.9)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{1}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}^{1}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{2}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle.

It is not difficult to show (
6.9
) directly from (
6.8
). For (
6.10
), it is sufficient to consider
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{q_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i})
, and for such
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
one has

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{2}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=n!\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{s\in S_{a}}{\bf 1}_{q_{s(i)}=p_{i}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(i)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\prod_{j;j\neq i}{\bf 1}_{q_{s(j)}=p_{j}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(j)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle

and

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=n!\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})}\sum_{s\in S_{a}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{1},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle{\bf 1}_{q_{s(i)}=p_{i}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(i)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\prod_{j;j\neq i}{\bf 1}_{q_{s(j)}=p_{j}}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(j)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle.

Since

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})}\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{1},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle\,\langle\!\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}^{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(i)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\updownharpoons_{\sf e}\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{s(i)}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle,

identity (
6.10
) follows if (
6.6
) holds for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}
, which leads to an induction on the number of edges contained in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. The case
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\in{\sf N}
is an easy exercise.

\rhd

6
.
2   Modelled distributions solutions of singular PDEs

The approximate description (
6.2
) of the fixed point problem (
4.11
) leads to an explicit formula for the coefficients of the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
. Noting that
\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
can be arbitrarily chosen, the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of (
6.2
) is of the form

(6.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{u_{k}}{k!}X^{k}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B},\,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma-2}u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau}).

Inserting such an expansion into (
6.2
), we see that all coefficients
u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}
are cylindrical functions of

{\sf u}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}.

Here we say that a function of
\sf u
is cylindrical if it depends only on a finite number of entries among
(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}
. For any smooth cylindrical function
F
, we denote by
\partial_{k}f\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{k}}F
the derivative with respect to
u_{k}
. Moreover, we define the derivative operators
(D_{i})_{i=0}^{d}
by setting

D_{i}F\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}u_{k+e_{i}}\partial_{k}F,

and
D^{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\prod_{i=0}^{d}D_{i}^{n_{i}}
for
n=(n_{i})_{i=0}^{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
.

0
Definition –

Set
{\sf u}_{0}=u_{0}
and
{\sf u}_{1}=(u_{e_{i}})_{i=1}^{d}
. Define the linear map
F
from
V^{*}
to the space of cylindrical functions of
\sf u
as follows. For the primitive trees in
{\sf N}^{0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathfrak{b}^{0}\}_{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}}\simeq\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
, set

(6.12)

\begin{split}F(\circ^{*})({\sf u})&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=f({\sf u}_{0}),\\
F(\bullet^{*})({\sf u})&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=g({\sf u}_{0},{\sf u}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=g_{2}({\sf u}_{0})({\sf u}_{1})^{2}+g_{1}({\sf u}_{0}){\sf u}_{1}+g_{0}({\sf u}_{0})\\
&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}g_{2}^{ij}(u_{0})u_{e_{i}}u_{e_{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{1}^{i}(u_{0})u_{e_{i}}+g_{0}(u_{0}),\\
F\big(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha})^{*}\big)({\sf u})&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=0.\end{split}

For a generic tree

\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}),

define inductively

(6.13)

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})({\sf u})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{*})({\sf u})\right\}\left\{D^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{p_{i}}\right\}F\big(\mathfrak{b}^{*}\big)({\sf u}).

Here are some examples. Recall that
\bullet
represents
{\bf 1}=X^{0}
, and
\circ
represents the noise
\Xi
.

\displaystyle F\big((\Xi\star\mathcal{I}(\Xi))^{*}\big)({\sf u})

\displaystyle=f(u_{0})f^{\prime}(u_{0}),

\displaystyle F\big((\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}(\Xi)\star\mathcal{I}_{e_{j}}(\Xi))^{*}\big)({\sf u})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}g_{2}^{ij}(u_{0})f(u_{0})^{2}&(i\neq j),\\
2g_{2}^{ii}(u_{0})f(u_{0})^{2}&(i=j).\end{cases}

We recall some useful formulas for the derivative operators.

Lemma 34
.

For any smooth cylindrical function
F
of
\sf u
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}
, one has

(6.14)

\displaystyle\frac{D^{n}F}{n!}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m:\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\times(\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\})\to\mathbb{N}\\
\sum_{q}(\sum_{k}m(k,q))q=n\end{subarray}}\bigg\{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\\
q\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m(k,q)!}\Big(\frac{u_{k+q}}{q!}\Big)^{m(k,q)}\bigg\}\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\\
q\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\partial_{k}^{m(k,q)}\bigg)F

(Faà di Bruno formula from Lemma A.1 of
[
16
]
).
Here
0!=1
,
u_{k}^{0}=1
, and
\partial_{k}^{0}=\text{\rm Id}
by convention, so the sum and the multiplications in the right hand side are over only finitely many parameters.
Moreover, for any
k,n\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}
, one has

(6.15)

\partial_{k}D^{n}F=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\binom{n}{\ell}D^{n-\ell}\partial_{k-\ell}F,

where
\binom{n}{\ell}=0
if
\ell>n
and
\partial_{k-\ell}=0
if
\ell>k
by convention, so the sums are over
\ell\leq n\wedge k
.

Proof –

The proof of (
6.14
) is an induction on
n
. In the case
n=e_{i}
, the right hand side of (
6.14
) coincides with
D_{i}F
by definition.
Next assume that (
6.14
) holds for
n
and consider
n+e_{i}
.
For simplicity, we denote by
M_{n}
the set of all maps
m:\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\times(\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\})\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{q}(\sum_{k}m(k,q))q=n
, and write
A(m)=\prod_{k,q}\frac{1}{m(k,q)!}\big(\frac{u_{k+q}}{q!}\big)^{m(k,q)}
and
B(m)=\big(\prod_{k,q}\partial_{k}^{m(k,q)}\big)F
.
By Leibniz rule, we can divide
D_{i}\frac{D^{n}F}{n!}
into two terms according to that
D_{i}
is applied to
A(m)
or
B(m)
.
By definition of
D_{i}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}u_{\ell+e_{i}}\partial_{\ell}
, the latter part is reorganized as

(6.16)

\sum_{m\in M_{n}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\big(m(\ell,e_{i})+1\big)A(\widetilde{m}_{\ell})B(\widetilde{m}_{\ell}),

where
\widetilde{m}_{\ell}\in M_{n+e_{i}}
is defined by
\widetilde{m}_{\ell}(k,q)=m(k,q)+{\bf 1}_{(k,q)=(\ell,e_{i})}
. On the other hand, since
D_{i}u_{k}^{m}=mu_{k}^{m-1}u_{k+e_{i}}
, the former part is also reorganized as

(6.17)

\sum_{m\in M_{n}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d},\,p\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\};m(\ell,p)\geq 1}(p_{i}+1)\big(m(\ell,p+e_{i})+1\big)A(\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p})B(\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p}),

where
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p}\in M_{n+e_{i}}
is defined by
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p}(k,q)=m(k,q)-{\bf 1}_{(k,q)=(\ell,p)}+{\bf 1}_{(k,q)=(\ell,p+e_{i})}
.
The term (
6.16
) can be absorbed into the sum (
6.17
) where the condition on
p
is replaced by ‘
p\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}
’, by setting
\widetilde{m}_{k,0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\widetilde{m}_{k}
and
m(\ell,0)=1
for any
\ell
. Conversely, for any
\mu\in M_{n+e_{i}}
, if
\mu(\ell,p)\geq 1
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}
and
p\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\}
, then there exists a unique
m\in M_{n}
such that
\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p-e_{i}}=\mu
. Therefore the sum (
6.17
) is reorganized as

\displaystyle D_{i}\frac{D^{n}F}{n!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in M_{n}}\sum_{\ell,p\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d};m(\ell,p)\geq 1}(p_{i}+1)\big(m(\ell,p+e_{i})+1\big)A(\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p})B(\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in M_{n+e_{i}}}\bigg(\sum_{m\in M_{n},\,\ell,p\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d};\widetilde{m}_{\ell,p}=\mu}(p_{i}+1)\big(m(\ell,p+e_{i})+1\big)\bigg)A(\mu)B(\mu).

It turns out that the quantity inside the large parentheses is equal to

\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d},q\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\}}q_{i}\mu(\ell,q)=n_{i}+1

because of the condition that
\mu\in M_{n+e_{i}}
. Thus we have
\frac{D_{i}}{n_{i}+1}\frac{D^{n}F}{n!}=\sum_{\mu\in M_{n+e_{i}}}A(\mu)B(\mu)
. This yields that (
6.14
) holds for any
n
.

The proof of (
6.15
) is also an induction on
n
. The case
n=e_{i}
follows from Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}D_{i}F

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\partial_{k}(u_{\ell+e_{i}}\partial_{\ell}F)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}(\partial_{k}u_{\ell+e_{i}})\partial_{\ell}F+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}u_{\ell+e_{i}}\partial_{k}\partial_{\ell}F

\displaystyle={\bf 1}_{k\geq e_{i}}\partial_{k-e_{i}}F+D_{i}\partial_{k}F.

Assuming that (
6.15
) holds for
n
, we have for
n+e_{i}

\displaystyle\partial_{k}D^{n+e_{i}}F

\displaystyle=\partial_{k}D^{n}(D_{i}F)=\sum_{\ell}{\textstyle\binom{n}{\ell}}D^{n-\ell}\partial_{k-\ell}D_{i}F=\sum_{\ell}{\textstyle\binom{n}{\ell}}D^{n-\ell}({\bf 1}_{k-\ell\geq e_{i}}\partial_{k-\ell-e_{i}}F+D_{i}\partial_{k-\ell}F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}{\textstyle\binom{n}{m-e_{i}}}D^{n+e_{i}-m}\partial_{k-m}F+\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}{\textstyle\binom{n}{m}}D^{n+e_{i}-m}\partial_{k-m}F.

Since
\binom{n}{m-e_{i}}+\binom{n}{m}=\binom{n+e_{i}}{m}
, it turns out that (
6.15
) also holds for
n+e_{i}
.

\rhd

Using the Faà di Bruno formula (
6.14
), we can give in the following lemma a representation of the nonlinear terms of (
4.11
).
Given a modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
of the form (
6.11
), set

\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma+\beta_{0}}\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}}\big(F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\big)^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u})\star\mathfrak{b}\right)=f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\star\Xi+g^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u}).

Lemma 35
.

Consider a generic tree of the form
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\big(\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})\big)^{\star m_{j}}
such that
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}
and
(p_{i},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i})\neq(p_{j},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})
for any
i\neq j
.
The
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-component of
\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u}(x))
is given by

(6.18)

\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{b}\frac{u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})}(x)^{m_{j}}}{m_{j}!}\right\}\left\{\frac{D^{n}}{n!}\prod_{j=1}^{b}\partial_{p_{j}}^{m_{j}}\right\}F\big(\mathfrak{b}^{*}\big)\big((u_{k}(x))_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1}\big).

Consequently, if
\boldsymbol{u}
solves the fixed point problem (
4.11
) then
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma-2
and any
x\in(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, one has

(6.19)

u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}(x)=\frac{1}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\big((u_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\big),

where the right hand side depends only on
u_{k}(x)
with
|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma
.

Proof –

We consider here

\boldsymbol{\tau}=\circ^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{b}\big(\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})\big)^{\star m_{j}};

the other cases are proved by similar arguments. The element
\boldsymbol{\tau}
appears in the term
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\star\Xi
. Inserting the expansion (
6.11
) into
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\star\Xi
, its
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-component is given by

\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}\,;\,\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{412879}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{321130}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{229379}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\tau}}\frac{f^{(a)}(u_{0})}{a!}\,u_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}}\dots u_{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}},

where
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}
are elements of
\mathcal{B}
of the forms
X^{k}
with
k\neq 0
or
\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})
, and
u_{X^{k}}=\frac{u_{k}}{k!}
by definition.
Rearranging
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}
so that duplicate elements are grouped together, the above quantity is reorganized as

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1},\dots,q_{r}\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\},\,i\neq j\Rightarrow q_{i}\neq q_{j},\\
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\,n_{1}q_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}q_{r}=n\end{subarray}}\frac{f^{(n_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{b})}(u_{0})}{n_{1}!\cdots n_{r}!m_{1}!\cdots m_{b}!}\Big(\frac{u_{q_{1}}}{q_{1}!}\Big)^{n_{1}}\cdots\Big(\frac{u_{q_{r}}}{q_{r}!}\Big)^{n_{r}}u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1})}^{m_{1}}\cdots u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{b})}^{m_{b}}

Applying the formula (
6.14
) to the sum over
q_{1},\dots,q_{r}
and
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}
, the above quantity is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!\,m_{1}!\cdots m_{b}!}\,u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1})}^{m_{1}}\cdots u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{b})}^{m_{b}}D^{n}\partial_{0}^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{b}}f(u_{0}).

This is a particular case of (
6.18
) with
\mathfrak{b}=\circ
and
p_{j}=0
.

If
\boldsymbol{u}
solves the fixed point problem (
4.11
), then

\boldsymbol{u}(x)=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{u_{k}(x)}{k!}X^{k}+\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\mathcal{I}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u}(x))\big)

for any
x\in(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Therefore the quantity (
6.18
) should be equal to
u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}(x)
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma-2
. Assuming
u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})}=F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}^{*})/S(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})
inductively, we have

u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}=\frac{1}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}).

This concludes the proof.

\rhd

Modelled distributions satisfying identity (
6.19
) are called ‘
coherent
’ in
[
16
]
.

Recall
{\sf E}\simeq\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, and define the family of differential operators

G\triangleright_{\sf e}H\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=G\,\partial_{u_{\sf e}}H\qquad({\sf e}\in{\sf E}),

acting on smooth functions of
(u_{0},u_{1})
, with
u_{1}=(u_{X_{i}})_{i=1}^{d}
. The family
\{\triangleright_{\sf e}\}_{{\sf e}\in{\sf E}}
defines an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra structure.

Proposition 36
.

The map
F
is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra morphism: For any
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
and any decorated trees
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}
in
\mathcal{B}
, one has

(6.20)

F\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)=F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}).

Proof –

Assume
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is of the form

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}).

Then by definition,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}\binom{n}{\ell}\mathfrak{b}^{n-\ell}\star\mathcal{I}_{{\sf e}-\ell}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{a}\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\big)\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j;j\neq i}\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}).

Hence

\displaystyle F\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}\binom{n}{\ell}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}D^{n-\ell}\partial_{{\sf e}-\ell}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\left\{\prod_{j;j\neq i}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}D^{n}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*}).

On the other hand, by Leibniz rule,

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})

\displaystyle=F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}\partial_{{\sf e}}D^{n}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})

\displaystyle\quad+F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\sum_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{\sf e}}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\left\{\prod_{j;j\neq i}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}D^{n}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*}).

The first terms in the expansions of
F\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)
and
F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
coincide, because of the identity (
6.15
). The second terms turn out to coincide if (
6.20
) holds for
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*}
, which leads an induction on the number of edges contained in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
.

\rhd

Assumption
(D
1
)
is a necessary condition for the basis
\mathcal{B}
. The next assumption means that
\mathcal{B}
is sufficiently large to describe all terms in the right hand side of (
6.2
).

Assumption (D2) –

One has
F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=0
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{B}
.

In particular, Assumption
(D
2
)
holds if
\mathcal{B}
contains all trees strongly conforming to the rule as in Section
9.1
. Indeed, if
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is not strongly conforming and does not have any node with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
decoration, then
\boldsymbol{\tau}
have an edge
\mathcal{I}_{p}
with
|p|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq 2
or have a node with at least three leaving edges
\mathcal{I}_{p}
with
|p|_{\mathfrak{s}}=1
.
Since
F(\bullet^{*})
is at most quadratic with respect to
u_{1}
, we have
F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=0
.
We define

\Upsilon\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=F|_{U^{*}}.

By Assumption
(D
2
)
, we can conclude that
\Upsilon
is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra morphism on the
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra
(U^{*},\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\}_{{\sf e}\in{\sf E}})
.

Proposition 37
.

Under Assumption
(D
2
)
, the map
\Upsilon
is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra morphism: For any
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
and any decorated trees
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}
in
\mathcal{B}
, one has

(6.21)

\Upsilon\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)=\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}).

Proof –

Since
\Upsilon\circ\pi_{U^{*}}=F\circ\pi_{U^{*}}=F
,

\Upsilon\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)=F\big(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\big)=F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})=\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\triangleright_{\sf e}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}).

\rhd

The next proposition is proved by an induction similar to the induction used in the proof of Proposition
36
, noting that
D_{i}
satisfies Leibniz rule.

Proposition 38
.

Under Assumption
(D
2
)
, for any
i\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
, one has

(6.22)

F(\,\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=D_{i}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}).

and for
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
,

(6.23)

\Upsilon(\,\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=D_{i}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}).

Proof –

Assume
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is of the form

\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{k=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{k}).

Then by definition,

\displaystyle\uparrow_{i}\tau

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}^{n+e_{i}}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{k=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{k})+\sum_{k=1}^{a}\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathcal{I}_{p_{k}}(\,\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{k})\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j;j\neq k}\mathcal{I}_{p_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{j}).Z

Hence

\displaystyle F(\,\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})

\displaystyle=\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{k}^{*})\right\}D^{n+e_{i}}\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{k}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})+\sum_{k=1}^{a}F(\,\uparrow_{i}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{k}^{*})\left\{\prod_{j;j\neq k}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{j}^{*})\right\}D^{n}\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{k}}}\right\}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*}).

If (
6.22
) holds for
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}
, then the above quantity is equal to
D_{i}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
by Leibniz rule. Thus the proof is reduced to an induction on the number of edges contained in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
.
One also obtains (
6.23
) by Assumption
(D
2
)
.

\rhd

6
.
3   Renormalization structure over a multi-pre-Lie algebra

We now come to the main result of
[
16
]
giving a dynamical meaning to the renormalization operations on models associated with elements
k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
of the renormalization group and more generally to elements
k\in G^{-}
. We keep working on the example of the generalized (KPZ) equation.

In Theorem
47
in Section
9
, we show that one can choose
U
stable under all
the splitting maps
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
, that is

(6.24)

\updownharpoons_{\sf e}(U)\subset U\otimes U

for any
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
. The restricted map

{\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}:U\to U\otimes U

is then the dual of the map
\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}
for any
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
. The following assumption is thus to be understood as a constraint on which renormalization schemes
\delta
can be used.

Assumption (D3) –

(a)

For any
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
, the space
U
is stable under
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
,
and one has

(6.25)

\big(\textrm{\emph{Id}}\,\otimes({\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U})\big)\delta=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta\otimes\delta\big){\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}.

and

(6.26)

\delta\,\circ\downarrow_{i}\,=\big(\textrm{\emph{Id}}\,\otimes\downarrow_{i}\big)\delta.

(b)

When
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is an element of
\mathcal{B}
without
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
decorations, then for any subforest
\varphi
of
\tau
,
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}:N_{\varphi}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\leq\mathfrak{n}
, and
\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime}:\partial\varphi\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, the tree

(6.27)

(\tau/^{\text{\rm red}}\varphi)_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime}}^{[\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}]_{\varphi},\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}+\pi\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime},\mathfrak{e})}

is also contained in
\mathcal{B}
– see Section
9.2
for the notation.

Assumptions
(D
1
)
,
(D
2
)
, and
(D
3
)
are jointly called Assumption
(D)
.
Identity (
6.25
) is the
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie version of the compatibility condition (
5.7
) between the splitting map
\Delta
of a regularity structure and
a renormalization splitting
\delta
.
Recall that any character
k
of
U^{-}
defines a linear map
\widetilde{k}=(k\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta:U\to U
. Denote by
\widetilde{k}^{*}:U^{*}\rightarrow U^{*}
the dual map of
\widetilde{k}
under the pairing (
6.5
). Anticipating over Section
9
, say here that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha}
is used to denote the result of extracting from a decorated tree
\boldsymbol{\tau}
the entire tree, but keeping track of the homogeneity
\alpha=|\boldsymbol{\tau}|
of the tree that was removed. Using the duality relation defining
\widetilde{k}^{*}
and the definition of
\widetilde{k}
we see that

\widetilde{k}^{*}(\circ^{*})=\circ^{*},\quad\widetilde{k}^{*}(\bullet^{*})=\bullet^{*},

and

\widetilde{k}^{*}\big(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha})^{*}\big)=0

for
\alpha>0
, and

\widetilde{k}^{*}\big(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha})^{*}\big)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B},|\boldsymbol{\tau}|=\alpha}\frac{k(\boldsymbol{\tau})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*},

for
\alpha<0
. The following result is part of Proposition 4.18 in Bruned, Chandra, Chevyrev and Hairer’s work
[
16
]
. It is the reason why we insisted on making a difference between
U
and
U^{*}
, to emphasize the dual action of
\widetilde{k}
.

Proposition 39
.

Under the compatibility Assumption
(D
3
)
, given any character
k
on
U^{-}
, the map
\widetilde{k}^{*}
is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie morphism: For any edge type
{\sf e}\in{\sf E}
, and any
\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{B}
, one has

\widetilde{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\widetilde{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})=\widetilde{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}),

and

(6.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}^{*}\circ\uparrow_{i}\,=\,\uparrow_{i}\circ\,\widetilde{k}^{*}\qquad(1\leq i\leq d).

Proof –

We prove the dual identities writing

\begin{split}\updownharpoons_{\sf e}\circ\,\widetilde{k}&=(k\,\otimes\updownharpoons_{\sf e})\delta\overset{\eqref{EqCompatibilityConditionPreLiRenorm}}{=}\Big(k\otimes\textrm{Id}\otimes\textrm{Id}\Big)\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta\otimes\delta\big)\updownharpoons_{\sf e}=\Big(\big(k\otimes\textrm{Id}\big)\delta\otimes\big(k\otimes\textrm{Id}\big)\delta\Big)\updownharpoons_{\sf e}=\big(\,\widetilde{k}\otimes\widetilde{k}\,\big)\updownharpoons_{\sf e},\end{split}

and

\downarrow_{i}\circ\,\widetilde{k}=(k\,\otimes\downarrow_{i})\delta\overset{\eqref{EqCompatibilityConditionPreLiRenorm2}}{=}(k\otimes\text{\rm Id})\delta\,\circ\downarrow_{i}=\widetilde{k}\,\circ\downarrow_{i}.

\rhd

Pick a character
k
on
U^{-}
. For primitive trees
\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}

define

(6.29)

\displaystyle F^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=F\Big(\widetilde{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\Big),

so we have

\displaystyle F^{(k)}(\circ^{*})=f({\sf u}_{0}),\quad F^{(k)}(\bullet^{*})=g({\sf u}_{0},{\sf u}_{1}),\quad F^{(k)}\big(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha})^{*}\big)={\bf 1}_{\alpha<0}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}\cap U_{\alpha}}\frac{k(\boldsymbol{\tau})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}).

By Lemma
35
,
F^{(k)}\big(({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha})^{*}\big)
is also a function of only
{\sf u}_{0}
and
{\sf u}_{1}
. For a tree

\boldsymbol{\tau}=\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}),

define inductively the functions of
(u_{0},u_{1})

(6.30)

\displaystyle F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})({\sf u})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}^{*})(u_{0},u_{1})\right\}\left\{D^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})({\sf u}),

similarly to (
6.13
).
Note that
F^{(k)}
can be defined for all elements
\boldsymbol{\tau}
in
\mathcal{V}
. The map
F^{(k)}
is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie morphism with respect to
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\}_{{\sf e}\in{\sf E}}
by the same proof as the proof of Proposition
36
. We prove the following proposition to ensure that
F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=0
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{B}
. The notations are all defined in Section
9.2
; the reader can skip it now and come back to it later.

Proposition 40
.

Let
k
be a character of
U^{-}
.
For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
, the function
F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
is represented as a linear combination of the functions
F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
,
where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
runs over all elements of

\mathcal{V}

without
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
decorations and such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is obtained by contracting
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
by its subforest
\varphi
with maps
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime}
as the form (
6.27
).

Proof –

The proof is an induction on the number of edges contained in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
. It is sufficient to consider
\boldsymbol{\tau}
of the form

\boldsymbol{\tau}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{n,\alpha}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}).

Assume that the result holds for each
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}
. Then by definition,
F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
is a linear combination of the functions of the form

\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}\left\{D^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{{p_{i}}}\right\}F(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}),

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}
is an element of
\mathcal{V}
without
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
decorations such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}
is obtained as a contraction of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}
, and
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{B}\cap U_{\alpha}
. By the formula
D^{n}\partial_{k}F=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}(-1)^{\ell}\binom{n}{\ell}\partial_{k-\ell}D^{n-\ell}F
obtained similarly to (
6.15
) and by Proposition
38
, the above function is a linear combination of the functions

\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}F(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*})\right\}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{a}\partial_{q_{i}}\right\}F(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}).

where
q_{i}\leq p_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{B}
is a tree appearing in the expansion of
\uparrow^{m}\boldsymbol{\eta}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\prod_{i=0}^{d}\uparrow_{i}^{m_{i}})\boldsymbol{\eta}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}
. By an argument similar to the proof of Proposition
36
, we can show that the above function is equal to

F\Big(\sum_{v_{1},\dots,v_{a}\in N_{\mu}}\big(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}\overset{q_{1}}{\curvearrowright}_{(v_{1})}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}\overset{q_{2}}{\curvearrowright}_{(v_{2})}\cdots(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{a}\overset{q_{a}}{\curvearrowright}_{(v_{a})}\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdots)\big)^{*}\Big)

The trees inside
F
produce
\boldsymbol{\tau}
when we contract
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{a},\boldsymbol{\mu}
as in the explicit formula of
{\sf D}^{-}
in Section
9.2
.

\rhd

As a result of Proposition
40
, under Assumptions
(D
2
)
and
(D
3
)
, we have that
F^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})=0
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{B}
,
since
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is an element of
\mathcal{B}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is a contraction of a tree in
\mathcal{B}
without
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}
decoration. This ensures that the map

\Upsilon^{(k)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=F^{(k)}|_{U^{*}},

is an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism on
U^{*}
with respect to
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\}_{\sf e\in E}
.
Moreover, denoting by
S
the subspace of
T
spanned by
\{\bullet^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}\cup\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{B})
,
we have that for any
\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}
and any function
\boldsymbol{u}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to S
, the function

\big(F^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\big)^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u})\star\mathfrak{b}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to V

is actually
T
-valued.

Corollary 41
.

Under Assumption
(D)
, one has
\Upsilon\circ\widetilde{k}^{*}=\Upsilon^{(k)}
, for all
k\in G^{-}
.

Proof –

It follows from Propositions
36
and
39
that the map
\Upsilon\circ\widetilde{k}^{*}
is an (
\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}
vs
\triangleright_{\sf e}
)
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism.
Because of Proposition
40
, the map
\Upsilon^{(k)}
is also an (
\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}
vs
\triangleright_{\sf e}
)
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism. Hence it is sufficient to show that they are equal on the generators of
U^{*}
, that is,

F\big(\widetilde{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{b}^{n})^{*}\big)=F^{(k)}\big((\mathfrak{b}^{n})^{*}\big)

for any
(\mathfrak{b},n)\in\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. The case
n=0
is given by definition (
6.29
). For
n=(n_{i})_{i=0}^{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, by writing
\uparrow^{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\prod_{i=0}^{d}\uparrow_{i}^{n_{i}}
, we
have

\displaystyle F\big(\widetilde{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{b}^{n})^{*}\big)

\displaystyle=F\big(\widetilde{k}^{*}(\uparrow^{n}\mathfrak{b}^{*})\big)\overset{\eqref{PropRenormMultiPreLieEq2}}{=}F\big(\uparrow^{n}(\widetilde{k}^{*}\mathfrak{b}^{*})\big)\overset{\eqref{EqCharacterizationGrafting2}}{=}D^{n}F\big(\widetilde{k}^{*}\mathfrak{b}^{*}\big)\overset{\eqref{EqDefnFk}}{=}D^{n}F^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})

\displaystyle=F^{(k)}\big((\mathfrak{b}^{n})^{*}\big),

using (
6.23
) and (
6.28
) in the last equality.

\rhd

Similarly to the definition of
\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})
, given a modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,\sf g)
with
\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
such that
(0,\gamma+\beta_{0})\cap A=\emptyset
, set

(6.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{u})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{\leq 0}\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}}\big(F^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\big)^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u})\star\mathfrak{b}\right).

Note the appearance in (
6.31
) of a number of symbols
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha}
, with
\alpha<0
, that have no counterpart in
\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})
.

Lemma 42
.

If
\boldsymbol{u}
is a solution of equation (
4.11
) then

(6.32)

\widetilde{k}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)=\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)

on the domain
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Proof –

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be evaluated at the fixed
x
in the domain
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
Lemma
35
implies that

\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}}\frac{\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\big((u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\big)}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\boldsymbol{\tau},

or equivalently,
\langle\!\langle\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
, for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}
. Noting that
\widetilde{k}
and
\widetilde{k}^{*}
preserve the grading of
T
, as a consequence of the compatibility condition (
5.4
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\widetilde{k}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\big),\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u}),\widetilde{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\rangle\!\rangle=\Upsilon\big(\widetilde{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\big)=\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})

for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}
, or equivalently,

\widetilde{k}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)=\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}}\frac{\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\big((u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}}\big)}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\boldsymbol{\tau}.

Next we consider the
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-component of
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)
by an argument similar to Lemma
35
. Note that Assumption
(C)
yields
\widetilde{k}(X^{n})=X^{n}
, and
\widetilde{k}\big(\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)=\mathcal{I}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)
. Hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})=\widetilde{k}\left\{\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{u_{k}}{k!}X^{k}+\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\mathcal{I}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)\right\}=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma}\frac{u_{k}}{k!}X^{k}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\frac{\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\tau}).

Thus by an argument similar to the former half part of Lemma
35
, we see that the
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-component of
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)
is equal to (
6.18
), where
u_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})}
is replaced by
\frac{\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}^{*})}{S(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j})}
, and
F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})
is replaced by
\Upsilon^{(k)}(\mathfrak{b}^{*})
.
By definition of
\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
, we see that the
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-component of
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)
is equal to
\frac{\Upsilon^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}
.
Therefore

\langle\!\langle\widetilde{k}\big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})\big),\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\big(\widetilde{k}(\boldsymbol{u})\big),\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\rangle\!\rangle

for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B}
with
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<\gamma+\beta_{0}
.

\rhd

The next statement provides a dynamical picture of the renormalization operation on models. As its proof will make it clear, it is a consequence of identity (
6.32
) and Theorem
28
, giving in particular the reconstruction operator of a
renormalized smooth model in terms of the unrenormalized smooth model, together with the multiplicativity property of the canonical model associated with a smooth noise.

Theorem 43
.

Assume
(D)
. Let
\zeta
be a smooth noise with canonical model
\sf M^{\zeta}=(\Pi^{\zeta},g^{\zeta})
. Given a character
k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{\emph{ad}}}
, denote
by
{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}=\big({\sf g}^{\zeta}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+},{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\circ\widetilde{k}\big)
its associated renormalized
\mathbf{K}
-admissible model – see Theorem
28
. Pick
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2]
and
\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
. Given an initial condition
v\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, let
\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{D}_{(0,t_{0})}^{\gamma,\eta}\big(T,{\sf g}^{\zeta}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)
stand for the solution on
(0,t_{0})
to the equation

\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}=P_{\gamma}v+\mathcal{P}_{t_{0}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}\Big(f^{\star}\big({\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}}\big)\Xi+g^{\star}\big({\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}},D{\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}}\big)\Big).

Then

u^{(k)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(C_{t_{0}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}})

is the solution on
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})
to the well-posed equation

\big(\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1\big)u^{(k)}=f\big({u^{(k)}}\big)\zeta+g\big({u^{(k)}},\partial_{x^{\prime}}{u^{(k)}}\big)+\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{B},\,|\tau|<0}\frac{k(\boldsymbol{\tau})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}\,F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\big({u^{(k)}},\partial_{x^{\prime}}{u^{(k)}}\big)

started from
v
.

Proof –

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
25
. The function
u^{(k)}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle u^{(k)}(x)=Pv(x)+\int_{(0,x_{0})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}P(x,y)\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}C_{t_{0}}\Big(f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)})\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)},D\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}\big)\Big)(y)dy.

Since
{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}
is a smooth model one has

\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(\boldsymbol{w})(x)={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\Big(\widetilde{k}\big(\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)\Big)(x).

for any modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{D}^{\alpha}\big(T,{\sf g}^{\zeta}\circ\widetilde{k}^{+}\big)
with
\alpha>0
. Applying Lemma
42

to
\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}
one has

\displaystyle\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}C_{t_{0}}\Big[f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)})\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)},D\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}\big)\Big](x)

\displaystyle={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\left[\widetilde{k}\Big(\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}(x)\Big)\right](x)={\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\left[\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\Big(\widetilde{k}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}(x)\big)\Big)\right](x).

for any
x\in(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
We see from the definition of the
F^{(k)}
that the term
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{w})
is a sum of functions of the form

H^{\star}(\boldsymbol{w})R^{\star}(D\boldsymbol{w}),

for smooth functions
H:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and polynomials
R
that are at most
quadratic. We now use the fact that since the map
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
is
multiplicative
so is its associated reconstruction operator.
The latter has value
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}_{x}(\cdot)(x)
at point
x
, so we have

{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\Big[H^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)R^{\star}\big(D\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)\Big](x)=H\Big[\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)(x)\Big]R\Big[\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}D\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)(x)\Big],

with
\boldsymbol{w}(x)=\widetilde{k}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}(x)\big)
.
Since
\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)(x)=\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(C_{t_{0}}\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)})(x)=u^{(k)}(x)

and
\big({\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}D\boldsymbol{w}(x)\big)(x)=\textbf{{R}}^{{}^{k}{\sf M}^{\zeta}}(C_{t_{0}}D\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)})(x)=\partial_{x^{\prime}}u^{(k)}(x)
on the domain
(0,\frac{t_{0}}{2})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we have in the end

{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\zeta}\Big[\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\Big(\widetilde{k}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{(k)}(x)\big)\Big)\Big](x)=\mathcal{F}^{(k)}\Big(u^{(k)},\partial_{x^{\prime}}u^{(k)}\Big)(x).

\rhd

Remark

–

The preceding proof underlines the fundamental role played by the multiplicative property of the centered naive interpretation operators
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}_{x}
. The canonical smooth model
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
is not the only multiplicative model that one can associate with a smooth noise
\zeta
. The class of models associated with ‘preparation maps’ introduced by Bruned in
[
15
]
provides a general setting where to obtain the renormalized equation for a class of renormalization procedures including the procedure implemented here
[
6
]
.

7  – The BHZ character

Among all the characters
k
on
U^{-}
that can be used to build a renormalization map
\widetilde{k}
, Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti proved in
[
19
]
that there is a unique character
k
whose associated random model is centered and translation invariant, in a probabilistic sense, when the smooth noise
\zeta
in the preceding section is random, centered and translation invariant. We describe it in this section and name it ‘BHZ character’, after the initials of Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti. We also call the associated renormalized model the BHZ model.

Arrived at that stage, the only piece of the story that will be missing to have a complete proof of the meta-theorems from Section
1
will be a proof of the fact that one can indeed construct some regularity structures satisfying the different assumptions that we put forward in the course of obtaining the above results, and a proof of convergence of the BHZ smooth renormalized models. We will tackle the first point in Section
9
. The second point is the object of Chandra & Hairer’s work
[
25
]
; we do not treat it here. We refer the reader to the work
[
71
,
60
,
10
]
for some alternative proofs of the convergence of BHZ renormalized models in situations where the law of the noise satisfies a spectral gap inequality.

We assume throughout this section that we work with regularity and renormalization structures satisfying Assumptions
(A-C)
.
To have a picture in mind, think of the structures associated with the generalized (KPZ) equation (
6.1
). Elements of
T=U
are thus given by node and edge decorated trees. Denote by
\mathbb{R}[U]
the commutative algebra generated by
U
. Recall from Assumption
(C
1
)
that
U^{-}
is an algebra generated by
\mathcal{U}_{<0}
and a unit
\textbf{{1}}_{-}
, and if one extends first the splitting map
\delta:U\to U^{-}\otimes U
into an algebra morphism
\hat{\delta}:\mathbb{R}[U]\to U^{-}\otimes\mathbb{R}[U]
, then the splitting map
\delta^{-}
satisfies

\delta^{-}=(\textrm{Id}\otimes P_{-}){\widehat{\delta}}|_{U^{-}},

for an algebra morphism projection map
P_{-}:\mathbb{R}[U]\rightarrow U^{-}
. (See Section
9.3
for the details; this projection map sends in particular
\bullet
and all the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha}
to
\textbf{{1}}_{-}
). Denote by
\widehat{\bf 1}_{-}
the unit of
\mathbb{R}[U]
, seen as the empty graph. (We use a distinct notation for
\widehat{\bf 1}_{-}
and
{\bf 1}_{-}
to emphasize that they do not live in the same space.) For basis elements
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}^{\mathfrak{m}}
of
\mathbb{R}[U]
, we write

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\prec\boldsymbol{\tau}

if
\sigma
is a strict
subgraph
of
\tau
, or
\sigma=\tau
and
\mathfrak{m}\leq\mathfrak{n}
with
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathfrak{n}
. (Do not get misled by the notation,
\sigma
may be a product of disjoint subtrees of
\tau
.) Recall from Section
5.2
the notation
\mathcal{F}=\{\mathcal{I}_{p}\}_{|p|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1}\cup\{X^{n}\star\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{1+d}\setminus\{0\}}
for the family of integral operators and multiplication by a non-null monomial. The following assumption describes the properties from the splitting map
\delta
that are relevant here. The renormalization structure built in Section
9
for the generalized (KPZ) equation satisfies it.

Assumption (E) –

(a)

For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
, one has the splitting formula

\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}\in P_{-}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\otimes{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,|\tau|}+U_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{-}\otimes U,

where
