U_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}\Big\{P_{-}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\in U^{-}\,;\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\prec\boldsymbol{\tau}\Big\}.

(b)

For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}

such that
F(\boldsymbol{\tau})\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
, one has

\delta\big(F(\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)\in(\text{\rm Id}\otimes F)\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}+P_{-}\big(F(\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)\otimes{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,|F(\boldsymbol{\tau})|}+\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{-}\otimes U,

where
\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{-}
is the ideal of
U^{-}
generated by
\Big\{P_{-}\big(F(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\big)\,;\,F\in\mathcal{F},\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0},\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\prec\boldsymbol{\tau}\Big\}
.

Property
(b)
is a refinement of the property (
5.13
) in Assumption
(C1)
.
Note that, under the definitions of gradings of
T
and
U
in Section
9
later,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\beta}\in\mathcal{B}_{\beta}
but
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\beta}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
, for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. (Basis elements of
U
with
0
-homogeneity are not necessarily unique, unlike in Assumption
(A
1
)
on concrete regularity structures.) Hence the above properties are consistent with the definitions of compatible renormalization and regularity structures. Property
(b)
also ensures the existence of the following map, defined by induction on the order relation
\prec
. Each element
\boldsymbol{\tau}
of
U^{-}
has by definition a unique representative
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{U}
in
\mathbb{R}[U]
. Denote by
\mathcal{M}
the multiplication operator on
\mathbb{R}[U]
and extend it naturally on
U^{-}\otimes\mathbb{R}[U]
setting
\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{\tau}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\mathcal{M}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma})
; it takes values in
\mathbb{R}[U]
.

0
Definition –

Under Assumption
(E)
, the
negative twisted antipode
is an algebra morphism

S^{\prime}_{-}:U^{-}\hskip-2.84544pt\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[U]

given recursively by
S_{-}^{\prime}{\bf 1}_{-}=\widehat{\bf 1}_{-}
and, for every basis element
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
by

(7.1)

S^{\prime}_{-}\big(P_{-}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)=-\mathcal{M}_{-}\big(S^{\prime}_{-}\otimes\textrm{\emph{Id}}\big)\Big(\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}-P_{-}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\otimes{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|}\Big).

The
P_{-}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
generating
U^{-}
as an algebra for
\boldsymbol{\tau}
ranging in
\mathcal{U}_{<0}
, identity (
7.1
) characterizes indeed uniquely an algebra morphism. The intuitive meaning of this recursive definition should be clear. One extracts from
\boldsymbol{\tau}
all possible subdiverging quantities
\varphi_{1}
, but also extracts from
\varphi_{1}
all its subdiverging quantities, and so on. This formula is close to
the Dyson-Salam renormalization formula for the antipode in Hopf algebras

[
38
]
; like the latter, it can be rewritten as a sum over forests of diverging sub-forests, as in Zimmermann forest formula. This will
not be useful here, and the only thing that matters here is property (
7.1
). The forest representation is however useful for the analysis of the convergence of renormalized models
[
25
]
.

Do not be mislead by the name of
S^{\prime}_{-}
: This is
not
the antipode of a Hopf algebra structure. Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti named it like that because its defining relation (
7.1
) looks like the defining relation (
B.1
) for the
antipode in a Hopf algebra.

Recall from Section
6.1
the definition of the naive interpretation operator
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
corresponding to a smooth noise
\zeta
in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We consider a random smooth noise
\zeta
, invariant by translation and centered.
Define the character
h^{\zeta}
on
\mathbb{R}[U]
by setting

h^{\zeta}(\widehat{\bf 1}_{-})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=1
and

(7.2)

h^{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbb{E}\big[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big](0)

for
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}
, and define a character on
U^{-}
setting

k^{\zeta}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=h^{\zeta}\circ S_{-}^{\prime}.

(Keep in mind that
S_{-}^{\prime}
gives back elements of
\mathbb{R}[U]
and that
h^{\zeta}
is multiplicative, so
k^{\zeta}
is multiplicative on
U^{-}
.) The associated ‘
BPHZ
renormalized’ interpretation operator
{}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
is defined on
U
by

{}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}=\big(k^{\zeta}\otimes{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\big)\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}=\Big((h^{\zeta}\circ S^{\prime}_{-})\otimes{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\Big)\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}.

The acronym BPHZ stands for Bogoliubov, Parasiuk, Hepp and Zimmermann, who made deep contributions to the renormalization problem in quantum field theory. We call
BHZ character
, after Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti, the character
k^{\zeta}
on
U^{-}
. The reason for introducing the negative twisted antipode operator lies entirely in the following simple computations used in the proof of the next statement claiming that the BPHZ renormalization associated with the BHZ character recenters probabilistically the
\sf\Pi
map at all points in spacetime. Its proof is taken from the proof of Theorem 6.17 in Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti’s work
[
19
]
on the
algebraic renormalization of regularity structures.

Theorem 44
.

Let
\zeta
stand for a smooth noise that is centered and translation invariant in law, and such that
\partial^{n}\zeta(0)
has finite moments of any order for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. We work with compatible regularity and renormalization structures under Assumptions
(A-C)
and Assumption
(E)
. The character
k^{\zeta}
belongs to
G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
and one has

(7.3)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big({}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)(x)\Big]=0

for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof –

First we show that
k^{\zeta}\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
. Let
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
be such that
F\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
. If
F=X^{n}\star
then
h^{\zeta}(F\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathbb{E}\big[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}X^{n}\star\boldsymbol{\tau}\big](0)=x^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}(x)\big]|_{x=0}=0
. Next consider
F=\mathcal{I}_{p}
. Recall from Section
3.1
that we defined the operator
\bf K
so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}y^{p}K(x,y)\,dy=0

for all
p\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
|p|_{\mathfrak{s}}<N
for fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Now pick
N=2
. Note that
\mathbb{E}[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}](x)=\mathbb{E}[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}](x)
is a polynomial of
x
with degree
\sum_{v\in N_{\tau}}|\mathfrak{n}(v)|_{\mathfrak{s}}
, since
K(x,y)
depends on
x-y
only. We use it here to have

h^{\zeta}\big(\mathcal{I}_{p}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}^{p}K(x,y)\mathbb{E}\big[{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}(y)\big]=0,

since
\sum_{v\in N_{\tau}}|\mathfrak{n}(v)|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq 1
(otherwise
|\boldsymbol{\tau}|\geq\beta_{0}+2>0
). Hence
h^{\zeta}(F\boldsymbol{\tau})=0
for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
. Since Assumption
(E)
guarantees that we have

\displaystyle S_{-}^{\prime}(F\boldsymbol{\tau})\in-\mathcal{M}_{-}\big(S_{-}^{\prime}\otimes F\big)\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}+\mathcal{M}_{-}\big(S_{-}^{\prime}\mathfrak{J}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\otimes U\big),

we can conclude that
h^{\zeta}\big(S_{-}^{\prime}(F\boldsymbol{\tau})\big)=0
, by an induction on the size of the graph
\tau
. Hence
k^{\zeta}\in G_{\text{\rm ad}}^{-}
.

– The negative twisteed antipode
S^{\prime}_{-}
is defined so as to have identity (
7.3
) for
x=0
. Indeed, since
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\beta})\equiv 1
for all
\beta
, one has from
the defining relation (
7.1
) for the twisted antipode, for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{U}_{<0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big({}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)(0)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\boldsymbol{\tau}}h^{\zeta}\big(S_{-}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\big)\,\mathbb{E}\big[\big({\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\tau}/^{-}\boldsymbol{\varphi})\big)(0)\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\boldsymbol{\tau}}h^{\zeta}\big(S^{\prime}_{-}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\big)\,h^{\zeta}(\boldsymbol{\tau}/^{-}\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle=h^{\zeta}\Big(\mathcal{M}\big(S^{\prime}_{-}\otimes\textrm{Id}\big)\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}\Big)=h^{\zeta}\big(S_{-}^{\prime}\tau\big)\Big(h^{\zeta}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|})-1\Big)=0.

Recall the homogeneity and grading notions on
U
and
T
are different. It is the homogeneity of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
, seen as an element of
T
, that appears in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|}
. It is elementary to
go from
\mathbb{E}\big[\big({}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\tau\big)(0)\big]=0
,
to
\mathbb{E}\big[\big({}^{k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\tau\big)(x)\big]=0
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, using the probabilistic translation invariance property of
{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}
.

\rhd

Remark

–

Note that the cointeraction identity between
\delta
and
\delta^{-}
implies that we have

(7.4)

^{k_{1}\star k_{2}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}={{}^{k_{1}}({{}^{k_{2}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}})},

for any two characters
k_{1},k_{2}
on
U^{-}
. There is no other character
k
on
U^{-}
than
h^{\zeta}\circ S^{\prime}_{-}
such that the renormalized naive interpretation operator
{}^{k}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\cdot$}}=(k\otimes{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta})\delta
, has property (
7.3
)
of Theorem
44
. The uniqueness claim amounts to proving that for any non-null character
k\neq 1
there exists an
element
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in U
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[\big(^{k\star k^{\zeta}}{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)(0)\big]\neq 0
. See the second part of the proof of Theorem 6.18 in
[
19
]
.

Assume now that
\zeta=\xi_{\varepsilon}
is the regularized version of a random irregular noise
\xi
, centered and translation invariant, and write

{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
for
{\sf\Pi}^{\xi_{\varepsilon}}
. The BHZ character
h
from (
7.2
) becomes
\varepsilon
-dependent as well. Set

(7.5)

k_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=h^{\xi_{\varepsilon}}\circ S^{\prime}_{-}.

Identity (
7.4
) tells us that if the maps
{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
converge to a limit when
\varepsilon
goes to zero, then for any character
k
on
U^{-}
, the renormalized interpretation map
{}^{k\star k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf\Pi}^{\varepsilon}
is also converging. There is thus a whole class of converging renormalization schemes indexed by the group of characters of
U^{-}
, if there is a single converging renormalization scheme. If we insist on building
\bf K
-admissible models, this provides a family of convergent models indexed by the renormalization group
G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
.

Recall the arguments in Section
6
. We say that the family of smooth cylindrical functions
F=\{F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\}_{\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}}
is a nonlinearity. Denote by
u=\textsc{Sol}(\xi;F)
for the solution to the PDE

(\partial_{x_{0}}-\Delta_{x^{\prime}}+1)u=\sum_{\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}}F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})(u,\partial_{x^{\prime}}u){\sf\Pi}^{\xi}\mathfrak{b}

driven by the smooth noise
\xi_{\varepsilon}
, associated with a given initial condition.
The arguments in Section
6.3
implies that, the nonlinearity
F=\{F(\mathfrak{b}^{*})\}_{\mathfrak{b}\in{\sf N}^{0}}
can be extended to smooth cylindrical functions
F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{V}
by (
6.30
), and the group
G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
acts on the set
\mathfrak{F}
of nonlinearities by
F^{(k)}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=F\circ\widetilde{k}^{*}
as in Corollary
41
. The renormalization group acquires a dynamical meaning from Theorem
43
if one notices that

\Big\{\textsc{Sol}\Big(\xi_{\varepsilon};F^{(k)}\Big)\Big\}_{k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}}=\Big\{\textsc{Sol}\Big(\xi_{\varepsilon};F^{(k_{\varepsilon}*k)}\Big)\Big\}_{k\in G^{-}}=\Big\{\textsc{Sol}\Big(\xi_{\varepsilon};\big(F^{(k_{\varepsilon})}\big){}^{(k)}\Big)\Big\}_{k\in G^{-}}

for any fixed positive
\varepsilon
. This remark tells us that the
family
of solutions of the singular stochastic PDE (
1.4
) is parametrized by the subset
\big(F^{(k)}\big)_{k\in G^{-}}
of the space
\mathfrak{F}
of nonlinearities. This remains true at the limit when
\varepsilon>0
goes to
0
. We will see in the Section
8
that this subset is actually a finite dimensional immersed manifold.

8  – The manifold of solutions

We take for granted in this section the convergence result of Chandra & Hairer from
[
25
]
, and work with the limit random admissible model
{\sf M}=({\sf g},{\sf\Pi})
, obtained as a limit in probability of the renormalized naive models
{}^{k_{\varepsilon}}{\sf M}^{\varepsilon}
when
\varepsilon>0
goes to
0
. Recall from equality (
6.31
) the expression of
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{u})
, for
k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
. Pick
\eta\in(0,\beta_{0}+2],\gamma\in(-\beta_{0},2)
and an initial condition
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Write
\boldsymbol{u}(k)\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
for the solution to the equation

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}(k)&=P_{\gamma}u_{0}+\mathcal{P}_{t_{0}}^{{\sf M}}\Big(\mathcal{F}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{u}(k))\Big)=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\Psi_{k}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(k)\big),\end{split}

and set

u(k)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}C_{t_{0}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(k)\big).

By continuity of the solution map the family of functions
\big\{u(k)\big\}_{k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}}
coincides with the limit of the family

\Big\{\textsc{Sol}\big(\xi_{\varepsilon};F^{(k*k_{\varepsilon})}\big)\Big\}_{k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}}.

Note that
\Psi_{k}
depends linearly, hence smoothly, on
k
. We saw in Theorem
23
in Section
4
that given a bounded set of nonlinearities in
C^{\infty}
, there exists a positive time
horizon
t_{0}
such that the ‘integral’ map
\Psi_{k}
is a contraction from
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
, uniformly with respect to the nonlinearities in the given bounded set. So the continuous linear map
\big(\textrm{Id}-\partial_{\boldsymbol{u}}\Psi_{k}\big)
, from the Banach space
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
into itself has a continuous inverse, given under the form of the classical Neumann series. The map
\big(\textrm{Id}-\partial_{\boldsymbol{u}}\Psi_{k}\big)
is thus a continuous isomorphism of
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
by the open mapping theorem. It is then
a direct consequence of the implicit function theorem that the unique fixed point
\boldsymbol{u}(k)
of the equation

\boldsymbol{u}(k)=\Psi_{k}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(k)\big)

is a smooth function of
k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
.

Proposition 45
.

The family
\big\{u(k)\big\}_{k\in G^{-}_{\textrm{\emph{ad}}}}
forms a finite dimensional immersed submanifold of
\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\eta
is the parameter chosen in Theorem
43
.

Proof –

It suffices from the implicit function theorem to see that
D_{k}\boldsymbol{u}(k)
, the derivative of
\boldsymbol{u}(k)
with respect to
k
, has constant rank; this follows from the linearity of the reconstruction map if we can see that
D_{k}\boldsymbol{u}(k)
is injective. (The reconstruction map is not injective without further assumptions.) The linear map
D_{k}\boldsymbol{u}(k)
sends the tangent space to
G^{-}_{\textrm{ad}}
at the identity into
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
. But picking
h
in that tangent space and setting
\boldsymbol{v}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(D_{k}\boldsymbol{u}(k))(h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{(0,t_{0})}(T,\sf g)
, the modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{v}
cannot be null unless
h=0
, since
\boldsymbol{v}
is the solution to the affine equation

\boldsymbol{v}=\mathcal{P}_{t_{0}}^{\sf M}\bigg(D_{k}(\mathcal{F}^{(k)})(\boldsymbol{u}(k))\boldsymbol{v}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{B},\,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|<0}\frac{h(\boldsymbol{\tau})}{S(\boldsymbol{\tau})}F(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})(\boldsymbol{u}(k),D\boldsymbol{u}(k))\bigg).

\rhd

Remark

–

The use of the implicit function theorem actually shows that the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of the equation

(8.1)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}&=P_{\gamma}u_{0}+\mathcal{P}_{t_{0}}^{\sf M}\Big(f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{u})\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u},D\boldsymbol{u}\big)\Big),\end{split}

a smooth function of
f,g\in C^{n}
, for
n
large enough. This gives a direct access to Taylor expansions in small noise, where
f
is replaced by
af
, for a small positive parameter
a
, or if
f
is the value at
a=0
of a smooth family
f(a,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}
, as the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
happens then to be a smooth function of the expansion parameter
a
. Elementary classical calculus is used to see that the derivatives of
\boldsymbol{u}
with respect to the parameter
a
are solutions of affine equations obtained by formal differentiation of equation (
8.1
) with respect to the parameter. This kind of questions has a long history, under the name ‘stochastic Taylor expansion’ in a stochastic calculus setting – after seminal works by Azencott
[
1
]
and Ben Arous
[
13
]
, where it was used together with the stationary phase method on Wiener space to get heat kernel estimates for elliptic and sub-elliptic diffusions. Inahama & Kawabi extended the approach to a rough paths setting in
[
65
]
, and Friz, Gassiat and Pigato made a first use of this type of ideas in a regularity structures setting in
[
41
]
. The result of Proposition
45
holds for all subcritical singular stochastic PDEs, with the above straightforward proof.

9  – Building regularity and renormalization structures

In the end, for the above results to hold, we require from the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
and the renormalization structure
\mathscr{U}
that they satisfy the different assumptions introduced along the way for different purposes. We summarize them here, with a quick description of what they are useful for.

Assumption

Section

What it is useful for

(A1-2)

2.3

Inclusion of the polynomial structure in our regularity structures.

(A3)

2.4

Product between
T_{X}
and
T
.

(B1)

3.2

Actions of
\Delta^{(+)}
on
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{(+)}
.

(B2)

3.5

Induction structure on
\Delta
for building admissible models.

(C)

5.2

Compatibility between the maps
\delta^{(+)}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}^{(+)}
.

(D1-2)

6.1
,
6.2

Largeness of the basis
\mathcal{B}
of
T
and
U
.

(D3)

6.3

Compatibility between multi-pre-Lie and renormalization structures.

(E)

7

Structure assumption on
U^{-}
, and induction structure on
\delta
.

Following Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti
[
19
]
, we describe in this section a setting tailor made for the study of the generalized (KPZ) equation (
6.1
) where all these conditions hold true. We introduce a homogeneity map on
the decorated trees from Section
6.1
.

0
Definition –

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm n}}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm e}}
be abstract finite sets, equipped with homogeneity maps
|\cdot|:\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm n}},\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm e}}\to\mathbb{R}
.

•

On the sets
{\sf N}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm n}}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
{\sf E}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\text{\rm e}}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, the homogeneity maps are extended by

\begin{cases}|k|_{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|n|+|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},&(n,k)\in{\sf N},\\
|\ell|_{e}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=|e|-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}},&(e,\ell)\in{\sf E}.\end{cases}

•

The
naive homogeneity
of a decorated tree
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is defined by

|\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}|^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{e\in E_{\tau}}|\mathfrak{e}(e)|_{\mathfrak{t}_{\text{\rm e}}(e)}+\sum_{n\in N_{\tau}}|\mathfrak{n}(n)|_{\mathfrak{t}_{\text{\rm n}}(n)}.

We start from the sets

\mathfrak{T}_{\text{n}}=\{\bullet,\circ\},\quad\mathfrak{T}_{\text{e}}=\{\mathcal{I}\},

for an abstract symbol
\mathcal{I}
– we use on purpose the same symbol as the
abstract integration map from Section
3.2
. The node type set
\mathfrak{T}_{\text{n}}
is enlarged later. The two elements
\bullet
and
\circ
of
\mathfrak{T}_{\text{n}}
represent the monomial
{\bf 1}=X^{0}
and the noise
\Xi
, respectively. The set
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
consists of only one integration operator
\mathcal{I}
. Each element has homogeneity

|\bullet|=0,\qquad|\circ|=\beta_{0},\qquad|\mathcal{I}|=2,

where
\beta_{0}\in(-2,0)
is the regularity of the noise
\zeta
in the equation. (Would the equation under study involve several noises with different regularities we would introduce several
\circ
symbols with the corresponding homogeneities.) Given that the polynomial structure is needed to encode at a regularity structure level the term
P_{\gamma}u_{0}
describing the propagation of the initial condition, and the piece of
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\sf M}
taking values in the polynomial regularity structure, the use of trees with a node decoration encoding multiplication by polynomials appears as natural. On the other hand, the use of edge decorations for equations that do not involve derivatives of the solution in their formulation, like the generalized (PAM) equation

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})u=f(u)\zeta,

may look strange. The necessity to use edge decorations to encode derivatives of quantities of the form
\mathcal{I}(\cdot)
, even in such a case, comes from the renormalization process implemented in this setting, as the latter involves Taylor expansions.

As said in Section
6
, the final form of a generic element of our regularity structures will be the datum of a decorated tree together with a coloring and an additional decoration

\mathfrak{o}:N_{\tau}\to\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}]
, which plays an important role in the compatibility condition between regularity and renormalization structures from Definition
27
. In a nutshell, this additional decoration will keep track of the naive homogeneity of the ‘diverging’ trees that will be extracted by the renormalization map
\delta
. This is what will allow to have maps
\delta^{(+)}
satisfying the fundamental conditions

\delta^{(+)}T^{(+)}_{\beta}\subset U^{-}\otimes T^{(+)}_{\beta}\qquad(\beta\in A^{(+)})

involved in the definition of compatible regularity and renormalization structures. So one should not be surprised that we will use the naive homogeneity to define the gradings in
U
and
U^{-}
and a different notion of
homogeneity in
T
and
T^{+}
, taking into account the
\mathfrak{o}
-decorations. The discussion will be general enough for the reader to see what needs to be added to deal with the general case.

9
.
1   Rules and extended decoration

Working with the set of all decorated trees as a candidate for a regularity structure is not reasonable and we first identify a few notions that help clarifying the matter. Recall the abstract self-explaining formulation

(9.1)

\boldsymbol{v}=\mathcal{I}\Big(f^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Xi+g^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{v},\partial\boldsymbol{v}\big)\Big)+(T_{X})

of the generalized (KPZ) equation. In the present tree setting the
\star
product is given by the ‘
joining
’ operator
\mathscr{J}
on trees. If one wants to make sense of Picard iteration within the concrete regularity structure, one needs to make sense of a number of recursive relations – recall the subcomodules introduced in Section
4.4
and see the pictures in Section
6.1
. General constraints of this type come under the name of
rule
, that is the definition for each node type
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
, of constraints on which kind of tuples of edges
\{e^{i}=(e^{i}_{-},e^{i}_{+})\}_{i}
can have
\mathfrak{t}_{\text{n}}(e^{i}_{-})=\mathfrak{b}
, for all
i
, in a tree allowed by the rule. The choice of a rule is determined by the equation under consideration. Consider the right hand side of equation (
9.1
). Making sense of the nonlinear term
f^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\Xi+g_{0}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})
requires that one can find
\mathscr{J}\big(\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\dots,\mathcal{I}(\cdot)\big)X^{n}
or
\mathscr{J}\big(\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\dots,\mathcal{I}(\cdot)\big)X^{n}\Xi
, within the trees allowed by the rule, that is the corresponding nodes are of the form

Making sense of the other terms
g_{2}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D\boldsymbol{v})^{\star 2}+g_{1}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{v})\star(D\boldsymbol{v})
requires that one can find

\mathscr{J}\big(\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\dots,\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}(\cdot)\big)X^{n}
, or
\mathscr{J}\big(\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\dots,\mathcal{I}(\cdot),\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}(\cdot),\mathcal{I}_{e_{j}}(\cdot)\big)X^{n}
for some
i,j=1,\dots,d
within the trees allowed by the rule, so each node of the corresponding elements of
C
has the
form

The operators
\mathcal{I}_{e_{i}}
are represented by the double line in the above picture. Given a rule, a decorated
conforming tree
is a tree such that all nodes of the tree, except perhaps the root, satisfy the rule. Denote by

C

the set of conforming trees. If all node of the tree satisfy the rule, the tree is called
strongly conforming
. We denote by

SC

the set of strongly conforming trees. A rule is said to be
normal
if any subtree of a strongly conforming tree is also strongly conforming.

To construct regularity and renormalization structures, the rooted decorated trees obtained from the above iterations are not sufficient. Another important operation is the
contraction
of rooted trees,
involved in the definition of the splitting maps
\Delta
and
\delta
. Given a typed rooted tree
\tau
and a family
\varphi
of disjoint typed subtrees of
\tau
, we use the notation

\tau/^{\text{red}}\varphi

to denote the typed rooted tree obtained by identifying each subtree
\tau_{i}
with a single node
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
with red color in the quotient tree. Here is an example, with
\varphi
in
green
,

\tau=\hbox to89.76pt{\vbox to47.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.87914pt\lower-2.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

We allow such an operation for the set
SC
of strongly conforming trees. Precisely, if each connected component of
\varphi
belongs to
SC
, then we assume that
\tau/^{\text{red}}\varphi\in SC
. Hence each element of
SC
is a rooted decorated tree with a node type set

\mathfrak{T}_{\text{n}}^{SC}=\{\bullet,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet},\circ\}.

The analytic role of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
is the same as that of
\bullet
. In particular, the homogeneity of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
is
0
. This is an example of the coloring of the tree. Only decorated trees
without red color appear in the analysis of the well-posedness problem (
9.1
), but colors are used in the definition of the splitting maps in the renormalization structure.

Recall from Assumption
(B1)
and Section
3.5
that the algebra
T^{+}
is spanned by elements of the form

(9.2)

\displaystyle X^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{I}_{k_{i}}^{+}(\tau_{i}),

where
n\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d},\,k_{1},\dots,k_{N}\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, and
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}\in SC
. It is convenient to consider an element like (
9.2
) as a tree by interpreting
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{+}
as the planting operator like
\mathcal{I}_{k}
and the product
\prod
as the tree product

\mathscr{J}
. To distinguish such trees from elements of
SC
, we give a
blue color to their roots, encoding in this way the
+
sign in
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

(9.3)

k_{1}
k_{N}
n

The set
C
consists of such trees, where we see that the rule is broken at the root. This is because
C
is only conforming, not strongly conforming. Each element of
C
is thus a rooted decorated tree with a node type set

\mathfrak{T}_{\text{n}}^{C}=\{\bullet,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet},\circ\}.

A node of a conforming tree has the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}
if and only if it is a root. The homogeneity of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}
is
0
. The trees with a blue root will only be involved in the description of the space
T^{+}
.

A rule is said to be
subcritical
if for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, only finitely many elements of
SC
have naive homogeneity less than
\gamma
. A
complete
rule will guarantee that a rooted decorated tree obtained from the contraction of a strongly conforming tree by extracting ‘diverging’ pieces, and changing the decorations accordingly, will still be strongly conforming. Proposition 5.21 in
[
19
]
ensures that
any normal subcritical rule can be extended into a normal subcritical complete rule
. We take this result for granted and do not reprove it here. The above rule on the set of decorated trees is normal, subcritical and complete.

To construct compatible regularity and a renormalization structures we introduce an additional decoration. Denote by
N_{\tau}^{\text{\rm red}}
the
subset of
N_{\tau}
consisting of the nodes with type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}
.

0
Definition –

A tree with
extended decoration
is a rooted decorated tree
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
with a map

\mathfrak{o}:N^{{\rm red}}_{\tau}\to\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}].

We write
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}
for a generic tree with extended decoration.
The
extended homogeneity
of such a tree is defined by

\big|\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\big|\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{e\in E_{\tau}}\big|\mathfrak{e}(e)\big|_{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm e}(e)}+\sum_{n\in N_{\tau}}\big|\mathfrak{n}(n)\big|_{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm n}(n)}+\sum_{n\in N_{\tau}^{\rm red}}\mathfrak{o}(n).

We extend the naive homogeneity to the set of decorated trees with an extended decoration setting

\big|\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\big|^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big|\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\big|^{\prime}.

Note here that only trees without
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration, that is
\mathfrak{o}=0
, appear in the analysis of the fixed point problem (
9.1
). Indeed, the trees without
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration are stable under the coproducts
(\Delta^{+},\Delta)
defined below.
The
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration is only involved in the analysis of the renormalization procedure and the associated convergence problem – see the second equality of (
9.6
). Without it, the condition (
5.4
) for the compatibility of
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{U}
does not hold. We define the set

\mathcal{SC}

of strongly conforming trees with
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration as the minimal set which contains
SC
and
such that the vector space spanned by
\mathcal{SC}

is stable under all the coproducts defined below. (One could also consider
\mathcal{SC}
as a set of rooted decorated trees with node type set

\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}^{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}}=\{\bullet,\circ\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{\cdot,\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}]}.

We used such an identification in Section
6
. In the present section we treat
\mathfrak{o}
as a decoration, rather than as part of a node type.) Similarly, we define

\mathcal{C}

as the set of decorated trees with extended decorations of the form (
9.2
), where
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{N}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}
. We use the bold symbol
\boldsymbol{\tau}
to denote a generic element of
\mathcal{SC}
or
\mathcal{C}
. The above rule on the set of extended decorated trees is normal, subcritical and complete. (The subcriticality of the rule on
this set of trees with extended decorations comes from the fact that, for
any fixed
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the decoration
\alpha
of trees with extended
homogeneity less than
\gamma
, will only range in the set of homogeneities of subtrees of strongly conforming trees
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
with homogeneity less than
\gamma
.)

9
.
2   Coproducts

We define coproducts in the spaces of rooted decorated trees. This requires first that we define what we mean by ‘subtrees’ and ‘subforests’. Recall that the type sets

\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}^{SC}=\{\bullet,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet},\circ\},\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}^{C}=\big\{\bullet,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet},\circ\big\},\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}=\{\mathcal{I}\}

are fixed. Given a typed rooted tree
\tau
, a nonempty connected subgraph of
\tau
is called a
subtree
if it inherits from
\tau
its type map. Any possibly empty family of disjoint subtrees of
\tau
is called a
subforest
. Given a rooted tree
\tau
and
a subforest
\varphi=\big\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m}\big\}
, we use the notation

\tau/\varphi

to denote the rooted tree obtained by identifying each subtree
\tau_{i}
with a single node with node type
\bullet
in the quotient tree. Precisely, writing
y\sim_{\varphi}z
if
y
and
z
are in the same connected component of
\varphi
, we define
\tau/\varphi
as the tree consisting of the node set
N_{\tau}/\sim_{\varphi}
and the edge set
E_{\tau}\setminus E_{\varphi}
.
Moreover, we write

\tau/^{\text{red}}\varphi,\quad\text{or}\quad\tau/^{\text{blue}}\varphi

if we give a corresponding color to the nodes of
\varphi
in the quotient tree.

•

For any function
f:N_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, define the function

[f]_{\varphi}
on
N_{\tau/\varphi}
by

[f]_{\varphi}\big([x]\big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{y\sim_{\varphi}x}f(y),

where
[x]
denotes the equivalence class of
x\in N_{\tau}
.

•

Denote by
\partial\varphi
the leaves of
\varphi
, that is, the set of edges
(x,y)\in E_{\tau}
such that
x\in N_{\varphi}
and
y\in N_{\tau}\setminus N_{\varphi}
.
For any function
g:\partial\varphi\to\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, define the function
\pi g
on
N_{\tau}
by setting

(\pi g)(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{e=(x,y)\in\partial\varphi}g(e).

•

For any decorations
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\varphi}
on
\varphi
, define the function
\mathfrak{o}(\varphi,\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{e}_{\varphi}):N_{\tau/\varphi}\to\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}]
by

\mathfrak{o}\big(\varphi,\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{e}_{\varphi}\big)\big([\tau_{j}]\big)=\left|(\tau_{j})_{\mathfrak{e}_{\varphi}|_{\tau_{j}}}^{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}|_{\tau_{j}}}\right|^{\prime}

for each
1\leq j\leq m
, and
\mathfrak{o}(\varphi,\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{e}_{\varphi})=0
outside
[\varphi]
.

Define

T\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}(\mathcal{SC}),\qquad{\sf T}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}(\mathcal{C}),\qquad{\sf U}^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbb{R}[\mathcal{SC}].

Note that
{\sf T}^{+}
is an algebra with the tree product and unit
{\bf 1}_{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}^{0}
, and
{\sf U}^{-}
is an algebra with
the forest product and unit
{\bf 1}_{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset
. The space
T^{+}
will
be built from the side space
{\sf T}^{+}
and the space
U^{-}
from the side space
{\sf U}^{-}
. Similarly, the different splitting maps defining a regularity structure and a renormalization structure are built from splitting maps taking values in, or defined on, the spaces
T,{\sf T}^{+},{\sf U}^{-}
.

0
Definition –

We introduce three splitting operators.

1.

The linear map

{\sf D}:T\to T\otimes{\sf T}^{+}

is defined for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}
by

(9.4)

\begin{split}{\sf D}\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=&\sum_{\mu}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}!}{{\mathfrak{n}}\choose{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}}}\mu^{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}+\pi\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu},\mathfrak{o}|_{\mu}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{\rm blue}}\mu\big)^{[\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}]_{\mu},\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\mu}}_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}},\end{split}

where the first sum is over all subtrees
\mu
of
\tau
which contains the root of
\tau
,
and the second sum is over functions
\mathfrak{n}:N_{\mu}\to\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
,
with
\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}\leq\mathfrak{n}
and functions
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}:\partial\mu\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
.
The algebra morphism

{\sf D}^{\,+}:{\sf T}^{+}\to{\sf T}^{+}\otimes{\sf T}^{+},

is defined by the same formula (
9.4
) for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

2.

The algebra morphism

{\sf D}^{-}:{\sf U}^{-}\to{\sf U}^{-}\otimes{\sf U}^{-}

is defined by
{\sf D}^{-}({\bf 1}_{-})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}={\bf 1}_{-}\otimes{\bf 1}_{-}
, and for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}

\begin{split}{\sf D}^{-}\big(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}\big)&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\varphi}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}!}{{\mathfrak{n}}\choose{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}}}\varphi^{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}+\pi\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi},\mathfrak{o}|_{\varphi}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{\rm red}}\varphi\big)^{[\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n_{\varphi}}]_{\varphi},[\mathfrak{o}]_{\varphi}+\mathfrak{o}(\varphi,\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}+\pi\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime},\mathfrak{e})}_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}},\end{split}

where the first sum is over all subforests
\varphi
of
\tau
which contains all red nodes of
\tau
, and the sum over
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime}
is taken as in item
1
of the present definition.

3.

The algebra morphism

\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}:{\sf T}^{+}\to{\sf U}^{-}\otimes{\sf T}^{+}

is defined by the same formula as
{\sf D}^{-}
, with the first sum restricted to subforests
\varphi
which are disjoint from the root of
\tau
.

Remark

–

Since the right hand side of (
9.4
) may become an infinite series, we have to consider the ‘bigraded spaces’ of rooted decorated trees defined in Section 2.3 of
[
19
]
. For any collection of vector spaces
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
, we denote by
V=\bigboxplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}V_{n}
the space of all sequences
(v_{n})_{n=(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
with
v_{n}\in V_{n}
such that there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{n}=0
unless
n_{2}\leq k
. The tensor product of two bigraded spaces
V=\bigboxplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}V_{n}
and
W=\bigboxplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}W_{n}
is defined by

V\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}W\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigboxplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\bigg(\bigoplus_{k+\ell=n}V_{k}\otimes W_{\ell}\bigg).

For example, the bigraded space
{\sf V}=\bigboxplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}{\sf V}_{n}
of rooted decorated trees is given by setting
{\sf V}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
as the vector space spanned by all decorated trees
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}
such that
\sum_{e\in E_{\tau}}|\mathfrak{e}(e)|_{\mathfrak{s}}=n_{1}
and
|N_{\tau}\setminus\mathfrak{t}^{-1}\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\}|=n_{2}
. The spaces
T
,
{\sf T}^{+}
, and
{\sf U}^{-}
above are defined as sub-bigraded spaces of
{\sf V}
, and their tensor products are also defined as bigraded spaces in the above sense. As in Lemma 2.14 of
[
19
]
, triangular maps between bigraded spaces are well-defined. For any bigraded spaces
V
and
W
, the family of linear maps
A_{mn}:V_{n}\to W_{m}
is called
triangular
if
A_{mn}=0
unless
m_{1}\geq n_{1}
and
m_{2}\leq n_{2}
. The the linear map from
V
to
W

A\big((v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}A_{mn}v_{n}\bigg)_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}

is well-defined. The maps
{\sf D}
,
{\sf D}^{+}
,
{\sf D}^{-}
, and
\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}
above are well-defined as triangular maps. In the following, when dealing with infinite series, we use these facts implicitly. In Section
9.3
, we introduce some truncation maps which reduce infinite series to finite sums.

As suggested by the target spaces of the preceding maps, the splitting map
\Delta
will be constructed from
\sf D
and the map
\Delta^{+}
from
{\sf D}^{+}
, the maps
\delta
and
\delta^{-}
from
{\sf D}^{-}
, and the map
\delta^{+}
from
\overline{\sf D}^{-}
. Only trees with blue roots appear
in the right hand side of the tensor products defining
\sf D
. This is consistent with the fact that the trees with blue roots will represent later elements of
T^{+}
. The restriction on the choice of
\varphi
to subforests which are disjoint from the root in the definition of
\overline{\sf D}^{-}
ensures that it takes values in
{\sf U}^{-}\otimes{\sf T}^{+}
and that the multiplicative property

\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}(\boldsymbol{\tau}\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\big(\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)\big(\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\big)

holds. This reflects the fact that the product of two functions

{\sf g}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\,{\sf g}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\quad\boldsymbol{\tau},\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{C}

does not cause any new renormalization.

Remark

–

Keep in mind that the elements of
U^{-}
are meant to be evaluated by characters of
U^{-}
, and turned to numbers, while elements of
U
are meant to be turned to distributions. This is done jointly in a renormalized naive model
(k\otimes{\sf\Pi}^{\zeta})\delta
. Recall that the problem of renormalization comes from the fact that the kernel of the operator
\bf K
explodes on the diagonal. The building block of the renormalization operations
\delta
and
\delta^{-}
is best understood in the light of the following archetype problem. Let
g:([0,1]^{d})^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function that is smooth outside the deep diagonal
\textsf{\emph{diag}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big\{{\bf z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in([0,1]^{d})^{n};z_{1}=\cdots=z_{n}\big\}
, near which it behaves as
|{\bf z}-(z_{1},\dots,z_{1})|^{-a}
, for an exponent
a>d
. The function
g
is not integrable in any neighbourhood
of the deep diagonal, so it only makes sense as a distribution on
([0,1]^{d})^{n}\backslash\textsf{\emph{diag}}

\int_{([0,1]^{d})^{n}}g({\bf z})f({\bf z})d{\bf z},

for
f
smooth, with support with empty intersection with the deep diagonal. Can we define a distribution
\Lambda
on
([0,1]^{d})^{n}
that extends this distribution? This can be done defining
\Lambda
on
([0,1]^{d})^{n}

(\Lambda,\psi)=\int_{([0,1]^{d})^{n}}g({\bf z})\left(\psi({\bf z})-\psi({\bf z}_{1})-\cdots-\frac{({\bf z}-{\bf z}_{1})^{[a-d]}}{[a-d]!}\,\psi^{([a-d])}({\bf z}_{1})\right)\,d{\bf z},

for any smooth function
\psi
on
([0,1]^{d})^{n}
. This formula defines indeed a distribution, which coincides with the distribution associated with

g
outside the deep diagonal, since the
\psi^{[\ell]}({\bf z}_{1})
are null for functions with compact support with null intersection with
diag
. Taylor expansion appears as the building block of this extension procedure. In this parallel,
\tau
has two pieces,
g
and
f
, so the role of
\varphi
in
{\sf D}^{-}
would be played by either of them, and the role of the projector
p_{-}
in
\delta^{-}
, defined below, would select only the diverging term. The term
\varphi\otimes(\tau/^{-}\varphi)
in
\delta^{-}\tau
would precisely correspond to a term
g({\bf z})\,\frac{({\bf z}-{\bf z}_{1})^{n}}{n!}\,f^{(n)}({\bf z}_{1})
in the integral defining
\Lambda
. A formula like the above defining relation for
{\sf D}^{-}
appears if one deals with a multiple integral where several subintegrals define functions of their external variables of the same kind as
g
,
and one uses a similar kind of extension procedure as above.

The following lemma is proved in Appendix
C.3
.

Lemma 46
.

One has the coassociativity formulas

\displaystyle({\sf D}\otimes\text{\rm Id}){\sf D}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{+}){\sf D},

\displaystyle({\sf D}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}){\sf D}^{+}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{+}){\sf D}^{+},

\displaystyle({\sf D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}){\sf D}^{-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{-}\big){\sf D}^{-},\quad

\displaystyle({\sf D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big)\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}.

Moreover, one has the cointeraction formulas

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({\sf D}^{-}\otimes\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){\sf D}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}){\sf D}^{-},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\otimes\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){\sf D}^{+}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{+})\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}.

9
.
3   Regularity and renormalization structures

We define the Hopf algebra parts of regularity and renormalization structures, from the side spaces
{\sf T}^{+}
and
{\sf U}^{-}
. We use the shorthand notation
\mathcal{I}^{+}_{n}(\tau)
to denote the tree
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tau)
with a blue root, with
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tau)
standing for
\mathcal{I}(\tau)
with decoration
n
on the edge outgoing from the root. We define subsets
\mathcal{C}^{+}\subset\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{SC}^{-}\subset\mathcal{SC}
, by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\{X^{n}\mathscr{J}\big(\mathcal{I}_{m_{1}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{I}_{m_{b}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{b})\big)\in\mathcal{C}\,;\,\text{$\big|\mathcal{I}_{m_{j}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{j})\big|>0$, for any $j=1,\dots,b$}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{SC}^{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big\{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{SC}\,;\,|\boldsymbol{\tau}|^{\prime}<0\Big\}

and set

T=U=\;\textrm{span}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}),\qquad T^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\;\text{\rm span}(\mathcal{C}^{+}),\qquad U^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\;\mathbb{R}[\mathcal{SC}^{-}].

Note the use of the two notions of homogeneity in these definitions, the extended homogeneity
|\cdot|
for
T
and
T^{+}
, and the naive homogeneity
|\cdot|^{\prime}
for
U
and
U^{-}
. Denote by

p_{+}:{\sf T}^{+}\to T^{+}

the canonical projection, and define an algebra morphism

p_{-}:{\sf U}^{-}\to U^{-}

setting

\displaystyle p_{-}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\begin{cases}{\bf 1}_{-},&\textrm{for}\;\boldsymbol{\tau}={\bf 1}_{-},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha},\\
\boldsymbol{\tau},&\textrm{for}\;\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{SC}^{-},\\
0,&\textrm{for}\;\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{C}\setminus\Big\{\mathcal{SC}^{-}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}]}\Big\}.\end{cases}

0
Definition –

Define the linear maps

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\big){\sf D}:T\to T\otimes T^{+},

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(p_{+}\otimes p_{+}\big){\sf D}^{+}|_{T^{+}}:T^{+}\to T^{+}\otimes T^{+},

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(p_{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){\sf D}^{-}|_{U}:U\to U^{-}\otimes U,

\displaystyle\delta^{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big(p_{-}\otimes p_{-}\big){\sf D}^{-}|_{U^{-}}:U^{-}\to U^{-}\otimes U^{-},

\displaystyle\delta^{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(p_{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}|_{T^{+}}:T^{+}\to U^{-}\otimes T^{+}.

It follows from the multiplicativity of
p_{\pm}
that
\Delta^{+}
and
\delta^{\pm}

are algebra morphisms
. The assumption
p_{-}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{0,\mathfrak{o}})={\bf 1}_{-}
, is needed to ensure the formulas

\displaystyle\delta\boldsymbol{\tau}

\displaystyle={\bf 1}_{-}\otimes\boldsymbol{\tau}+\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|<0}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\tau}/\boldsymbol{\varphi}),

\displaystyle\delta^{-}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle={\bf 1}_{-}\otimes\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\otimes{\bf 1}_{-}+\sum_{|\boldsymbol{\sigma}|<|\boldsymbol{\psi}|<0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\sigma}/\boldsymbol{\psi})

for
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{SC}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\mathcal{SC}^{-}
.

Theorem 47
.

Set

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big((T^{+},\Delta^{+}),(T,\Delta)\big),

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\big((U^{-},\delta^{-}),(U,\delta)\big).

(a)

\mathscr{T}
is a regularity structure satisfying Assumptions
(A)
and
(B)
, with the grading
|\cdot|
.

(b)

\mathscr{U}
is a renormalization structure satisfying Assumption
(E)
, with the grading
|\cdot|^{\prime}
.

(c)

\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{U}
are compatible and satisfy Assumption
(C)
.

(d)

Assumption
(D)
, the compatibility between the splittings
{\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}
and
\delta
holds true.

Proof –

Write as shorthand

\displaystyle{\sf D}\boldsymbol{\tau}\;\textrm{ or }\;{\sf D}^{+}\boldsymbol{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i},

\displaystyle{\sf D}^{-}\boldsymbol{\tau}\;\textrm{ or }\;\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\boldsymbol{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{j}\otimes\boldsymbol{\psi}_{j},

and note that the following stability formulas of the naive and extended homogeneities. One has

(9.5)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\tau}|=|\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}|+|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}|,

(9.6)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\tau}|^{\prime}=|\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{j}|^{\prime}+|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{j}|^{\prime},\quad|\boldsymbol{\tau}|=|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{j}|

for each
i
and
j
. Here we define
|{\bf 1}_{-}|^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=0
.

(a)

By the first identity of (
9.5
),

(p_{+}\otimes p_{+}){\sf D}^{+}p_{+}=(p_{+}\otimes p_{+}){\sf D}^{+}

holds on
{\sf T}^{+}
. Then one has the comodule property of
\Delta
as follows.

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\text{\rm Id})\Delta

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\otimes p_{+}\big)({\sf D}\otimes\text{\rm Id}){\sf D}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\otimes p_{+}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{+}){\sf D}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\otimes p_{+}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}^{+})(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}){\sf D}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta^{+})\Delta.

The coassociativity of
\Delta^{+}
is obtained similarly. One gets for free the existence of an antipode on
T^{+}
from the fact that
T^{+}
is a connected graded bialgebra – see Proposition
48
in Appendix
B
.

(b)

The comodule properties of
\delta
and
\delta^{-}
are obtained by the similar way to
(a)
, since

(p_{-}\otimes p_{-}){\sf D}^{-}p_{-}=(p_{-}\otimes p_{-}){\sf D}^{-}

holds on
{\sf U}^{-}
, by identity (
9.6
).
By definition,
{\bf 1}_{-}
is the only element in
U^{-}
of
0
homogeneity, so
U^{-}
is a connected graded bialgebra.

(c)

We prove the cointeraction property

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta^{+})\Delta=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta)\delta;

the proofs of other properties are left to readers. See also Proposition
30
. The second identity of (
9.6
) yields

\delta^{+}\circ p_{+}=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+})\delta^{+}

on
{\sf T}^{+}
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta^{+})\Delta

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta\otimes(\delta^{+}\circ p_{+})\big){\sf D}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\Big(p_{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{-}\otimes p_{+}\Big)\big({\sf D}^{-}\otimes\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){\sf D}

\displaystyle=\big(p_{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\big)\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({\sf D}^{-}\otimes\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){\sf D}

\displaystyle=\big(p_{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\otimes p_{+}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{\sf D}){\sf D}^{-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\Delta\big)\delta.

(d)

Recall the explicit formula for the map
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
, from Lemma
33
. It is obvious that
U
is stable under
\updownharpoons_{\sf e}
. Define

\updownharpoons(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\sigma\in A(\tau)}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{\sigma},\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\sigma}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\sigma}^{\prime}!}\binom{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{n}_{\sigma}}(\tau/\sigma)_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\sigma}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{\sigma},\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}_{\sigma}+\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\sigma}^{\prime},\mathfrak{o}},

where
A(\tau)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{P_{e}\tau\}_{e\in E_{\tau}}
– where
P_{e}\tau
is a connected component of the graph
\tau/\{e\}
containing the root of
\tau
, see (
6.7
). Comparing this with the definition of
\Delta^{+}
, it is not difficult to show the equality

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\delta\otimes\delta\big)\updownharpoons=(\text{\rm Id}\,\otimes\updownharpoons)\delta

proceeding as in the proof of point
(c)
. Note that the contracted tree
\tau/\sigma
is always planted. Let
p_{\sf e}
be the canonical projection on the set of planted trees
\eta
with

\mathfrak{n}(\rho_{\eta})=0,\quad\mathfrak{e}(e_{\eta})={\sf e},

where
e_{\eta}
is the only one edge leaving the root
\rho_{\eta}
, and let

c
be the map sending the tree of the form
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tau)
to
\tau
. Then

{\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}=(c\circ p_{\sf e}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\updownharpoons.

on
U
. Since it is elementary to show

\begin{split}(\textrm{Id}\otimes c)\delta&=\delta\circ c,\\
(\textrm{Id}\otimes p_{\sf e})\delta&=\delta\circ p_{\sf e},\end{split}

the compatibility condition follows by writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta){\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\Big(\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes c\circ p_{\sf e}\big)\delta\otimes\delta\Big)\updownharpoons

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes c\circ p_{\sf e}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta)\updownharpoons

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes c\circ p_{\sf e}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\big(\text{\rm Id}\,\otimes\updownharpoons\big)\delta=(\text{\rm Id}\,\otimes({\updownharpoons_{\sf e}}|_{U}))\delta.

\rhd

9
.

represents the edge with

-decoration

-decoration

.

– Here are some examples of the actions of splitting map

. The dot
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}\text{\tiny$(\alpha)$}

.

\displaystyle{\sf D}^{-}\,\hbox to3.8pt{\vbox to14.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.9pt\lower-1.7pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

p_{-}
or
k\in G_{\rm ad}^{-}
. Omitting them by
(\cdots)
, one has for example

Appendix B
Basics from algebra

We recall some basics of bialgebras, Hopf algebras, and comodules without
proofs. See
[
78
,
73
,
39
]
for details. Note that, for any two algebras
A
and
B
with units
{\bf 1}_{A}
and
{\bf 1}_{B}
respectively, the tensor space
A\otimes B
is also an algebra with the product

(a_{1}\otimes b_{1})\cdot(a_{2}\otimes b_{2})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(a_{1}a_{2})\otimes(b_{1}b_{2}),\qquad(a_{1},a_{2}\in A,\ b_{1},b_{2}\in B)

and with unit
{\bf 1}_{A}\otimes{\bf 1}_{B}
.

0
Definition –

A
bialgebra

(B,\mathcal{M},{\bf 1},\triangle,\theta)
is a
5
-tuple of the following components.

•

An algebra
B
with product
\mathcal{M}:B\otimes B\to B
, and unit
{\bf 1}
.

•

An algebra morphism
\triangle:B\to B\otimes B
satisfying the
coassociativity

(\triangle\otimes\text{\rm Id})\triangle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\triangle)\triangle.

•

An algebra morphism
\theta:B\to\mathbb{R}
, satisfying

(\theta\otimes\text{\rm Id})\triangle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\theta)\triangle=\text{\rm Id},

where we identify
a\otimes\tau=\tau\otimes a=a\tau
, for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tau\in B
.

The map
\triangle
is called a
coproduct
, and the map
\theta
is called a
counit
. An algebra morphism
S:B\to B
, such that

(B.1)

\mathcal{M}(\text{\rm Id}\otimes S)\triangle=\mathcal{M}(S\otimes\text{\rm Id})\triangle=\theta(\cdot){\bf 1}

is called an
antipode
. A bialgebra equipped with an antipode
S
is called a
Hopf algebra
.

The counit
\theta(\cdot)
is traditionally denoted
\varepsilon(\cdot)
.
We use a different letter as
\varepsilon
already stands for a regularization parameter in this work. The following result gives a sufficient condition for a bialgebra to be a Hopf algebra.
A bialgebra
B
is called
graded
if it is a direct sum
\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}B_{\lambda}
of vector spaces such that

•

\Lambda
be a locally finite subset of
[0,\infty)
such that
0\in\Lambda
and
\Lambda+\Lambda\subset\Lambda
.

•

{\bf 1}\in B_{0}
and
B_{\lambda}\cdot B_{\mu}\subset B_{\lambda+\mu}
,
for any
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
.

•

\triangle B_{\lambda}\subset\bigoplus_{\mu,\nu\in\Lambda,\,\mu+\nu=\lambda}B_{\mu}\otimes B_{\nu}
.

We call
\Lambda
a
grading
in this paper. A graded bialgebra with
B_{0}=\langle{\bf 1}\rangle
is said to be
connected
.

Proposition 48
.

[
78
, Exercises pages 228 and 238]
,

[
73
, Proposition II.1.1 and Corollary II.3.2]

Any connected graded bialgebra is a Hopf algebra. Moreover, one has the following properties.

•

\theta({\bf 1})=1
and
\theta(\tau)=0
for any
\tau\in\bigoplus_{\lambda>0}B_{\lambda}
.

•

\triangle{\bf 1}={\bf 1}\otimes{\bf 1}
, and for any
\tau\in B_{\lambda}
with
\lambda>0
,

\triangle\tau\in\bigg\{\tau\otimes{\bf 1}+{\bf 1}\otimes\tau+\underset{\mu+\nu=\lambda,\,0<\mu<\lambda}{\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\Lambda}}B_{\mu}\otimes B_{\nu}\bigg\}.

Based on the first assertion, we denote by
{\bf 1}^{\prime}
the counit

\theta
of a connected graded bialgebra. The preceding formula for
\triangle\tau
gives an inductive formula for the antipode. For
\tau\neq{\bf 1}
and
\triangle\tau=\tau\otimes{\bf 1}+{\bf 1}\otimes\tau+\sum\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}
, one has

S(\tau)=-\tau-\sum S(\tau_{1})\tau_{2}.

On the dual space
B^{\prime}
of the bialgebra
B
, the
convolution product
is defined by

(f*g)\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(f\otimes g)\triangle\tau,

for all
f,g\in B^{\prime},\ \tau\in B
, where we identify
a\otimes b=ab
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The coassociativity of
\triangle
implies the associativity of the convolution

(f*g)*h=f*(g*h),

for all
f,g\in B^{\prime}
, and the counit
\theta
is indeed a unit of the convolution product

f*\theta=\theta*f=f,

for all
f\in B^{\prime}
. Hence the triplet
(B^{\prime},*,\theta)
is a unital ring. Moreover, the subset
G\subset B^{\prime}
of algebra morphisms
g:B\to\mathbb{R}
is stable under the convolution product. The existence of an antipode
S
implies that
G
is a group. Indeed, the inverse of
g\in G
is given by
g^{-1}=g\circ S
. Each element of
G
is called a
character
, and when
B
is a Hopf algebra, the set
G
is called the
character group
.

We recall comodules and comodule bialgebras.
Given an algebra
A
and two spaces
E,F
, we define on the algebraic tensor product
A\otimes E\otimes A\otimes F
the
A\otimes E\otimes F
-valued map

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(a_{1}\otimes e\otimes a_{2}\otimes f\big)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(a_{1}a_{2})\otimes e\otimes f.

0
Definition –

Let
(B,\mathcal{M},{\bf 1},\triangle,\theta)
be a bialgebra.

•

A linear space
M
equipped with a linear map
\delta:M\to B\otimes M
, with the properties

(\text{\rm Id}_{B}\otimes\delta)\delta=(\triangle\otimes\text{\rm Id}_{M})\delta,\quad\textrm{and}\quad(\theta\otimes\text{\rm Id}_{M})\delta=\text{\rm Id}_{M},

is called a left
B
-
comodule
.
Similarly, a linear space
N
is called a right
B
-comodule if a linear map
\rho:N\to N\otimes B
, exists and satisfies

(\rho\otimes\text{\rm Id}_{B})\rho=(\text{\rm Id}_{N}\otimes\triangle)\rho,\quad\textrm{and}\quad(\text{\rm Id}_{N}\otimes\theta)\rho=\text{\rm Id}_{N}.

•

A bialgebra
M
is called a left
B
-
comodule bialgebra
if
M
is a left
B
-comodule by an algebra morphism
\delta:M\to B\otimes M
, such that

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}(\delta\otimes\delta)\triangle_{M}=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\triangle_{M})\delta,\quad\textrm{and}\quad(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\theta_{M})\delta=\theta_{M}(\cdot){\bf 1},

where
\triangle_{M}
is a coproduct of
M
, and
\theta_{M}
is a counit of
M
.

Proposition 49
.

[
39
, Proposition 2]

Let
M
be a
B
-comodule bialgebra. If
M
has an antipode
S_{M}
, then

\delta\circ S_{M}=(\text{\rm Id}_{B}\otimes S_{M})\delta.

Appendix C
Technical proofs

This section is dedicated to proving Theorem
20
and Lemma
46
.

C
.
1   Proof of Theorem
20

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda\in(0,1]
, denote by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}
the transformation of functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\lambda^{-d-2}\varphi\Big(\lambda^{-2}(y_{0}-x_{0}),\lambda^{-1}(y^{\prime}-x^{\prime})\Big).

The following bound appears in the Hairer’s original paper
[
53
]
. Recall
\beta_{0}=\min A
and that
p_{t}
denotes the heat kernel of the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=\partial_{x_{0}}^{2}-(\Delta_{x^{\prime}}-1)^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 50
.

Let
\sf M=(\Pi,g)
be a model over the regularity structure
\mathscr{T}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
Assume
\beta_{0}>-4
. Then for any Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda\in(0,1]
, one has the bound

\displaystyle\left|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle\right|\leq C_{\varphi}\|{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}\|\left\|\boldsymbol{f}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\lambda^{\gamma},

where the constant
C_{\varphi}
depends on the size
\sup_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq N}\|x^{k}\partial^{\ell}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for
N>0
large enough.

Proof –

Write
\Lambda_{x}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)
, to shorten notations. Using
p_{0}=\int_{0}^{\lambda^{4}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}p_{t}dt+p_{\lambda^{4}}
and the symmetry of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
,

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda_{x},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{\lambda^{4}}\big\langle\Lambda_{x},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}p_{t}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\,dtdy+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\langle\Lambda_{x},p_{\lambda^{4}}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{\lambda^{4}}\big\langle\Lambda_{x},p_{t}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\,dtdy+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\langle\Lambda_{x},p_{\lambda^{4}}(y,\cdot)\big\rangle\,\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\,dy=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}(A)+(B).

Using the properties of models as in Proposition
2
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Lambda_{x},p_{t}(y,\cdot)\rangle\right|\lesssim t^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}+\sum_{\beta\in[\beta_{0},\gamma)}t^{\frac{\beta}{4}}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}.

This implies
|(B)|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}
. Moreover, since
\beta_{0}>-4
, one has

\displaystyle|(A)|\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in[\beta_{0},\gamma]}\int_{0}^{\lambda^{4}}t^{\frac{\beta}{4}}dt\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)|\,dy\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in[\beta_{0},\gamma]}\lambda^{\beta+4}\lambda^{\gamma-\beta-4}\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}.

\rhd

We prove a fundamental fact on the connection of modelled distributions. For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, denote by
\mathcal{D}_{I}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
the set of functions
\boldsymbol{f}:I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to T_{<\gamma}
which satisfies the bounds of
[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}_{I}^{\gamma}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{I}^{\gamma}}
as in Definition
3
with
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
replaced by
I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 51
.

Let
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
and let
a<b<c
.
If
\boldsymbol{f}:[a,c]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to T_{<\gamma}
satisfies the bounds of
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,b]}^{\gamma}}
and
|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[b,c]}^{\gamma}}
, then
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{[a,c]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
and one has

|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,c]}^{\gamma}}\lesssim(1+\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma})\big(|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,b]}^{\gamma}}+|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[b,c]}^{\gamma}}\big).

Proof –

It is sufficient to show the bound of
\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\|_{\beta}
for
x\in[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
y\in[b,c]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Setting
z=(b,y^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yz}}\boldsymbol{f}(z)\|_{\beta}+\big\|\widehat{{\sf g}_{yz}}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(z)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{zx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)\big\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{[b,c]}^{\gamma}}d(y,z)^{\gamma-\beta}+\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,b]}^{\gamma}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\beta,\gamma)}d(y,z)^{\alpha-\beta}d(z,x)^{\gamma-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma})\big(|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,b]}^{\gamma}}+|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[b,c]}^{\gamma}}\big)d(y,x)^{\gamma-\beta}.

\rhd

We recall now from J. Martin’s work
[
74
, Theorem 5.3.16]
the existence of a ‘Whitney extension’ map on locally defined modelled distributions.

Theorem 52
.

Let
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
with a regular product
\star
satisfying Assumption
(A)
. Then there exists a continuous linear operator
E:\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,t]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
such that
(E\boldsymbol{f})|_{(-\infty,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\boldsymbol{f}
, and the bound

|\!|\!|E\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\leq C|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,t]}^{\gamma}}

holds for a positive constant
C=C(\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma})
depending polynomially on
\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}
, and independent of
t>0
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,t]}^{\gamma}(T,\sf g)
. A similar result holds for the modelled distributions defined on
[t,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof –

For simplicity, we consider
t=0
. It is sufficient to construct the continuous linear extension operator
\widetilde{E}:\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,0]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,1]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
. Indeed, once we pick
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
which depends only on
x_{0}
such that
\chi(x_{0},x^{\prime})=1
when
x_{0}\leq 0
and
\chi(x_{0},x^{\prime})=0
when
x_{0}\geq 1
, and set

\boldsymbol{\chi}(x)=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\frac{\partial^{k}\chi(x)}{k!}X^{k},

then by Proposition
8
, we can define the modelled distribution on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

E\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\big(\boldsymbol{\chi}\star(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})\big).

Then the operator
E:\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,0]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
satisfies the desired properties.

We construct the extension operator
\widetilde{E}:\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,0]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})\to\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,1]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
. Let
h(x_{0},x^{\prime})=h_{x_{0}}(x^{\prime})
be the kernel of the operator
e^{x_{0}\Delta_{x^{\prime}}}
with
x_{0}>0
, and define the function

\boldsymbol{h}(x)=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\frac{\partial^{k}h(x)}{k!}X^{k}

on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we have the properties

(C.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\boldsymbol{h}(x)dx^{\prime}={\bf 1},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{k}h(x_{0},x^{\prime})||x^{\prime}|^{\alpha}\lesssim x_{0}^{\frac{\alpha-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{2}}

for any
\alpha\geq 0
.
For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}_{(-\infty,0]}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
, we set
(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\boldsymbol{f}(x)
if
(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\boldsymbol{h}\big(x-(0,y^{\prime})\big)\star\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,y^{\prime})}}f(0,y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}

if
x\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We first prove

(C.2)

\displaystyle\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})\|_{\beta}\lesssim\|\widehat{\sf g}\|\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\,x_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}}

for any
\beta<\gamma
and
x=(x_{0},x^{\prime})\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since

(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\boldsymbol{h}(x_{1},x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})\star\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\big\{\widehat{{\sf g}_{(0,x^{\prime})(0,y^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,y^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})\big\}dy^{\prime}

by the first property of (
C.1
), we have (
C.2
) as follows.

\displaystyle\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{k}h(x_{0},x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})|\big\|\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\big\{\widehat{{\sf g}_{(0,x^{\prime})(0,y^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,y^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})\big\}\big\|_{\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\sf g}\|\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},\gamma)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial^{k}h(x_{0},x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})|\,x_{0}^{\frac{\alpha-\beta+|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{2}}|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{\gamma-\alpha}dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\widehat{\sf g}\|\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},\gamma)}x_{0}^{\frac{\alpha-\beta+|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{2}}x_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\alpha-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{2}}\lesssim\|\widehat{\sf g}\|\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\,x_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}}.

The bound of
\big\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\big\|_{\beta}
on
x\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
follows from (
C.2
) and the bound of
\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})
.
For the bound of
\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\|_{\beta}
, it is sufficient to consider the case that
x,y\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
by Lemma
51
. If
x_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge y_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq d(x,y)
, we can bound above
\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\|_{\beta}
by

\displaystyle\|(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,y^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,y^{\prime})\|_{\beta}+\big\|\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,y^{\prime})}}\big\{\boldsymbol{f}(0,y^{\prime})-\widehat{{\sf g}_{(0,y^{\prime})(0,x^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})\big\}\big\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\big\{\widehat{{\sf g}_{x(0,x^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,x^{\prime})-(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\big\}\big\|_{\beta},

and using (
C.2
), we obtain the desired bound. If
d(x,y)<x_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\wedge y_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, by using the formula

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{h}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}\bigg(\partial^{k}h(y)-\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(y-x)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\partial^{k+\ell}h(x)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}\sum_{|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\gamma-\beta_{0}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{(y-x)^{n}}{n!}\int_{[0,1]^{d+1}}\partial^{k+n}h(x_{t})m_{n}(dt)

obtained from Lemma
18
, where
n
runs over a finite set, we decompose

\displaystyle(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}(\widetilde{E}\boldsymbol{f})(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\{\boldsymbol{h}\big(y-(0,z^{\prime})\big)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{h}\big(x-(0,z^{\prime})\big)\Big\}\star\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,z^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{<\gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma-\beta_{0}\\
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\gamma-\beta_{0}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}\end{subarray}}\frac{(y-x)^{n}}{k!n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{[0,1]^{d+1}}\partial^{k+n}h\big(x_{t}-(0,z^{\prime})\big)m_{n}(dt)X^{k}\star\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,z^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}.

Thus it is sufficient to show that

(C.3)

\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{k+n}h\big(x_{t}-(0,z^{\prime})\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,z^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}\bigg\|_{\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\lesssim d(x,y)^{\gamma-\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

For this purpose, we decompose

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{k+n}h\big(x_{t}-(0,z^{\prime})\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,z^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}\bigg\|_{\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{k+n}h\big(x_{t}-(0,z^{\prime})\big)\widehat{{\sf g}_{y(0,x_{t}^{\prime})}}\big\{\widehat{{\sf g}_{(0,x_{t}^{\prime})(0,z^{\prime})}}\boldsymbol{f}(0,z^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{f}(0,x_{t}^{\prime})\big\}dz^{\prime}\bigg\|_{\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\gamma}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},\gamma)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\partial^{k+n}h\big(x_{t}-(0,z^{\prime})\big)\big|d(x,y)^{\alpha-\beta+|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}|x_{t}^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|^{\gamma-\alpha}dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\gamma}}\sum_{\alpha\in[\beta-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}},\gamma)}(x_{0}\wedge y_{0})^{\frac{\gamma-\alpha-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}{2}}d(x,y)^{\alpha-\beta+|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}}.

Since
\gamma-\alpha-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\leq-\alpha+\beta_{0}\leq 0
under the condition that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\gamma-\beta_{0}-|k|_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\alpha\in A
, we obtain the desired bound (
C.3
).

\rhd

Corollary 53
.

Let
\sf M=(g,\Pi)
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
with a regular product
\star
satisfying Assumption
(A)
.
For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}(T,{\sf g})
and
a>0
, the restriction
\boldsymbol{f}|_{[a,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}
satisfies the bound

|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|_{[a,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}_{[a,\infty)}^{\gamma}}\leq Ca^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

for a positive constant
C=C(\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma})
depending polynomially on
\|\widehat{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}
, independent of
a
and
\boldsymbol{f}
.
Therefore by writing
\textbf{{R}}_{a}^{\sf M}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\circ E_{a}
for the extension map
E_{a}
from
\mathcal{D}_{[a,\infty)}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
to
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(T,{\sf g})
, for any Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda\in(0,1]
, one has the bounds

(C.4)

\left|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}_{a}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}E_{a}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle\right|\leq C^{\prime}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}a^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}\lambda^{\gamma}

and

(C.5)

\left|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}_{a}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle\right|\leq C^{\prime}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}a^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}\lambda^{\gamma}.

for a positive constant
C^{\prime}
depending polynomially on
\|{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}+\|{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}\|_{\gamma}
, independent of
a
and
\boldsymbol{f}
.

Proof –

It is sufficient to show the bound of
\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}
for any
\beta<\gamma
and
x,y\in[a,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
d(x,y)\geq\omega(x,y)
. We decompose

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)\big\|_{\beta}+\big\|\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\bigg(a^{\frac{(\eta-\beta)\wedge 0}{2}}+\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha<\gamma}d(x,y)^{\alpha-\beta}a^{\frac{(\eta-\alpha)\wedge 0}{2}}\bigg).

Since
