d(x,y)\geq\omega(x,y)\geq a
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{f}(y)-\widehat{{\sf g}_{yx}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big\|_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}d(x,y)^{\gamma-\beta}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha<\gamma}d(x,y)^{\alpha-\gamma}a^{\frac{(\eta-\alpha)\wedge 0}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim[\hskip-1.42262pt]\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}d(x,y)^{\gamma-\beta}a^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}.

Thus the modelled distribution
\boldsymbol{f}
restricted to
[a,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
has the norm of size
a^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}
.

\rhd

We turn to the proof of the reconstruction theorem for singular modelled distributions.

Proof of Theorem
20
–

The proof is just an analogue of the proof of Proposition 6.9 in Hairer’ seminal work
[
53
]
, so we omit a number of details. The only difference is that
p_{t}(x,\cdot)
is not compactly supported. For the sake of generality, for any Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda\in(0,1]
, we prove the bound

(C.6)

\left|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle\right|\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}\lambda^{\gamma}

for any
\lambda\in(0,\omega(x)]
, and the bound

(C.7)

\left|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle\right|\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}}\lambda^{\eta}

for any
\lambda\in(0,1]
. To lighten notation, we omit the proportional constants depending on the model
\sf M
.
By linearity, we can assume that
\boldsymbol{f}=0
on
(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For any
a>0
, we consider the distributions
\textbf{{R}}_{a}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}(E_{a}\boldsymbol{f})
defined in Corollary
53
.
Because of the local property of the reconstruction operator, Corollary
5
, these distributions are compatible over all
a>0
in the sense that
\langle\textbf{{R}}_{a}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\rangle=\langle\textbf{{R}}_{b}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\rangle
for any
0<a<b
and Schwartz functions
\varphi
supported on
[b,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so the quantity
\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle
is defined for any
\varphi
supported on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
\boldsymbol{f}
vanishes on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{0}
, one defines
\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle=0
if
\varphi
is supported on
(-\infty,0)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
To consider arbitrary
\varphi
, fix a family
\{\phi_{n,k}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of functions of the forms

\phi_{n,k}=2^{-n(d+2)}\phi_{x_{n,k}}^{2^{-n}},\quad x_{n,k}=\big(2^{-2n},2^{-n}k\big)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

where
\phi
is a smooth function supported on
\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};d(0,x)<1\big\}
, and such that
\sum_{n,k}\phi_{n,k}(x)=1
, if
0<x_{0}<1/2
.
To show the bound (
C.7
), fixing an integer
n_{0}
such that
2^{-n_{0}}\simeq\lambda
, and setting
\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}=1-\sum_{n\geq n_{0},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\phi_{n,k}
, we decompose

\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\big\rangle=\sum_{n\geq n_{0},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\phi_{n,k}\big\rangle+\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}\big\rangle.

For the second term, since
\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}
is supported in
\{y\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};y_{0}\geq a_{n_{0}}\simeq 2^{-2n_{0}}\}
and
\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}=\psi_{x}^{\lambda}
for some Schwartz function
\psi
which is uniform over
\lambda,x,n_{0}
, by (
C.5
) one has

\displaystyle\left|\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}\big\rangle\right|\lesssim 2^{-n_{0}(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma)}\lambda^{\gamma}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\simeq 2^{-n_{0}(\eta\wedge\beta_{0})}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}.

For the first term, since
\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\phi_{n,k}
is supported in
[2^{-2n},\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and roughly equal to

2^{-n(d+2)}\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})\phi_{x_{n,k}}^{2^{-n}},

one has

\displaystyle\left|\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\phi_{n,k}\big\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-n(d+2)}|\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})|2^{-n(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma)}(2^{-n})^{\gamma}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-n(d+2)}|\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})|2^{-n(\eta\wedge\beta_{0})}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

The sum
2^{-nd}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})|
is roughly bounded by
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi^{\lambda}(x)|dx^{\prime}\lesssim\lambda^{-2}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq n_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left|\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\phi_{n,k}\big\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\lambda^{-2}\sum_{n\geq n_{0}}2^{-n(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\lambda^{-2}2^{-n_{0}(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2)}\lesssim|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}\lambda^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}.

Here we use the assumption
\eta\wedge\beta_{0}>-2
.

The proof of (
C.6
) is almost the same with some modifications.
We fix the same
n_{0}
as above and decompose
\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}
into
\sum_{n\geq n_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\phi_{n,k}+\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}
.
For the dual with the second term, by taking
a\simeq x_{0}
in (
C.6
), one has

\displaystyle\left|\big\langle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\phi}_{n_{0}}\big\rangle\right|\lesssim\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}\lambda^{\gamma}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}.

For the dual with the remaining terms, one decomposes

\displaystyle\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\textbf{{R}}}{}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-{\sf\Pi}_{x_{n,k}}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x_{n,k})\right)+{\sf\Pi}_{x_{n,k}}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x_{n,k})-{\sf\Pi}_{x_{n,k}}^{\sf g}\Big(\widehat{{\sf g}_{x_{n,k}x}}\boldsymbol{f}(x)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}(a)+(b)+(c).

For
(a)
, one has

\displaystyle\big|\langle(a),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\phi_{n,k}\rangle\big|\lesssim 2^{-n(d+2)}|\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})|2^{-n(\eta\wedge\beta_{0})}\|{\sf\Pi}^{\sf g}\||\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

as before. The sum
2^{-nd}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}(x_{n,k})|
is roughly bounded by
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi^{\lambda}(x)|dx^{\prime}\lesssim\lambda^{-2}|\lambda^{-2}x_{0}|^{-N}
for any
N>0
. By picking
N=\frac{\gamma-\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}{2}
, one has

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq n_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big|\langle(a),\varphi_{x}^{\lambda}\phi_{n,k}\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-2}|\lambda^{-2}x_{0}|^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}\sum_{n\geq n_{0}}2^{-n(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2)}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-2}|\lambda^{-2}x_{0}|^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}{2}}2^{-n_{0}(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2)}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}\lambda^{\gamma}|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{f}|\!|\!|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}.

Using the bound
\|f(x)\|_{\beta}\lesssim|x_{0}|^{(\eta-\beta)/2\wedge 0}
, one gets the same bounds as above for
(b)
and
(c)
.

It remains to show the uniqueness of
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}
satisfying (
4.6
).
We start from the identity

\big|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}t^{\frac{\gamma}{4}}

satisfied uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\omega(x)^{4}\geq t
by any other reconstruction
operator
(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}
. Since
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}
, we also have

\big|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim t^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}{4}}

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0<t\leq 1
. From these bounds, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\gamma)
we can show that

\big|\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\lesssim\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}t^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
0<t\leq 1
. If one chooses small
\varepsilon>0
such that
\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\varepsilon>-2
, since
\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\varepsilon}\varphi(x)dx<\infty
for any Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, one has

\big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-\big(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\big)^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},\varphi\big\rangle=\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\int\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}-(\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f},p_{t}(x,\cdot)\Big\rangle\,\varphi(x)dx=\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\,O\big(t^{\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\big)=0.

\rhd

In the end of this section, we provide a sketch of the proof of Theorem
17
’.

Proof of Theorem
17
’ –

The proof is carried out by a method similar to the proof of Theorem
17
with modifications on the bounds of
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}
terms.
Similarly to the proof of Theorem
17
, we use the decomposition
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}_{r}dr
and
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}_{r}dr
and write
(\tau)_{X^{n}}
for the
X^{n}
-component of
\tau\in T
. As for the bound of
[\hskip-1.42262pt]\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}[\hskip-1.42262pt]_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
, we focus on the bound of

[\circleddash]_{r}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Big(\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}_{r}(x)\boldsymbol{f}(x)+(\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}_{r}\boldsymbol{f})(x)\Big)_{X^{n}}

for any
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}<\gamma+2
.
If
r\leq\omega(x)^{4}
, we estimate the
\mathcal{J}^{\sf M}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}
terms separately. By using the bound (
4.6
) for
\mathcal{N}^{\sf M}
, we have

\displaystyle[\circleddash]_{r}\lesssim\sum_{\beta\in(|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2,\gamma)}\omega(x)^{(\eta-\beta)\wedge 0}r^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}+\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}r^{\frac{\gamma-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}.

Its integration over
r\in[0,\omega(x)^{4}]
has an upper bound
\omega(x)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}
.
If
r>\omega(x)^{4}
, we decompose

\displaystyle[\circleddash]_{r}=\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},\partial_{x}^{n}q_{r}(x,\cdot)\Big\rangle-\sum_{\beta<\gamma,\,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}\geq\beta+2}\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\big(\boldsymbol{f}(x)\big)_{\beta},\partial_{x}^{n}q_{r}(x,\cdot)\Big\rangle.

Note that, in the sum over
\beta
, the term associated with
\beta
such that
|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}=\beta+2
vanishes because of (
3.3
).
Since
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle[\circleddash]_{r}\lesssim r^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}+\sum_{\beta<\gamma,\,|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}>\beta+2}\omega(x)^{(\eta-\beta)\wedge 0}r^{\frac{\beta-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}.

Its integration over
r\in[0,\omega(x)^{4}]
has an upper bound
\omega(x)^{(\eta\wedge\beta_{0}+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}})\wedge 0}
.

As for the bound of
\|\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathcal{D}^{\gamma,\eta}}
, we focus on the bound of
(\circleddash)_{r}
similarly to the proof of Theorem
17
.
For the integrations over
r\leq d(x,y)^{4}
and
d(x,y)^{4}\leq r\leq\omega(x,y)^{4}
, we use the same
(\Asterisk)
-decomposition and the
(\bigstar)
-decomposition respectively. Then we have the same bounds except the existence of the factor
\omega(x,y)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}
. For the integration over
r\geq\omega(x,y)^{4}
, we further decompose
(\bigstar)_{r}^{1}
into

(\bigstar)_{r}^{1}=\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f},(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\Big\rangle-\frac{1}{n!}\Big\langle{\sf\Pi}_{x}^{\sf g}\boldsymbol{f}(x),(\partial^{n}q_{r})_{y,x}^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\Big\rangle.

For the first term, since
\textbf{{R}}^{\sf M}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}}
, by using the same remainder formula (
3.20
), we have the upper bound
\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}>\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(x,y)^{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}r^{\frac{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}-2}{4}}
.
Its integration over
r\in[\omega(x,y)^{4},1]
has an upper bound

\sum_{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}>\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}d(x,y)^{|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\omega(x,y)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}-|\ell|_{\mathfrak{s}}+2}\lesssim d(x,y)^{\gamma+2-|n|_{\mathfrak{s}}}\omega(x,y)^{\eta\wedge\beta_{0}-\gamma}

since
d(x,y)\leq\omega(x,y)
. We also have a similar bound for the remaining terms.

\rhd

C
.
2   Proof of Proposition
31
and Proposition
32

We provide a sketch of the proof Proposition
31
and Proposition
32
, following
[
40
]
and
[
20
]
. Recall that, the basis
\mathcal{V}
of
V
is the set of all rooted trees with node types
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}
and edge types
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}
, and with
decorations
\mathfrak{n}:N_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
and
\mathfrak{e}:E_{\tau}\to\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
.
Write
{\sf N}=\mathfrak{T}_{\rm n}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
and
{\sf E}=\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
.
Moreover, the basis
\mathcal{B}
of
U
is a subset satisfying Assumption
(D)
.
For simplicity, we prove the following proposition for
V
and
U
, instead of
V^{*}
and
U^{*}
.

0
Proposition –

(a)

The space
V
(or
U
) with the operators
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\}_{\sf e\in E}
(or
\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\}_{\sf e\in E}
) is an
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie algebra.

(b)

Let
(W,\{\rightslice_{\sf{e}}\}_{\sf{e}\in\sf{E}})
be an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra, and let
\{\varphi_{\mathfrak{b}^{n}}\}_{(\mathfrak{t},n)\in{\sf N}}
(or
\{\varphi_{\mathfrak{b}^{n}}\}_{(\mathfrak{t},n)\in{\sf N}\cap\mathcal{B}}
)
\subset W
.
Then there exists a unique
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism
\varphi:V
(or

U
)
\to W
such that
\varphi(\mathfrak{b}^{n})=\varphi_{\mathfrak{b}^{n}}
for any
\mathfrak{b}^{n}\in{\sf N}
(or
{\sf N}\cap\mathcal{B}
).

The
{\sf E}
-multi-pre-Lie property of
(V,\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\}_{\sf e\in E})

(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta})=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\tau})\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta})

is proved in a similar way to Proposition 2.2 of
[
40
]
and Corollary 2.9 of
[
20
]
, so we omit the proof. The same property for
(U,\{\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\}_{\sf e\in E})
follows from it. Indeed, by Assumption
(D1)
for the canonical projection
\pi_{U}:V\to U
, we have

(\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}_{\flat}\boldsymbol{\eta})=\pi_{U}\big((\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\sf e}{\curvearrowright}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\overset{{\sf e}^{\prime}}{\curvearrowright}\boldsymbol{\eta})\big).

In order to prove
(b)
, we introduce the Guin-Oudom extension of the multi-pre-Lie structure. The following is the content of Section 2.2 of
[
40
]
and Section 3.2 of
[
20
]
.

0
Definition –

Let
(W,\{\rightslice_{\sf{e}}\}_{\sf{e}\in\sf{E}})
be an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie algebra.
Let
(W,{\sf e})=\{(a,{\sf e})\}_{a\in W}
be a copy of the linear space
W
, and denote

W^{\oplus{\sf E}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bigoplus_{\sf e\in E}(W,{\sf e}).

Moreover, let
S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})
be the symmetric algebra of
W^{\oplus{\sf E}}
, with unit
\bf 1
. Then one can define the following linear maps.

•

Define the linear map
\rightslice_{\sf e}:W\otimes S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})\to S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})
inductively as follows.

\displaystyle c\rightslice_{\sf e}{\bf 1}=0,

\displaystyle c\rightslice_{\sf e}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(b_{i},{\sf e}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(c\rightslice_{\sf e}b_{i},{\sf e}_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}(b_{j},{\sf e}_{j}),

where
c,b_{1},\dots,b_{N}\in W
and
{\sf e}_{1},\dots,{\sf e}_{N}\in{\sf E}
.

•

Define the linear map
\rightslice:S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})\otimes W\to W
inductively as follows.

\displaystyle{\bf 1}\rightslice b=b,

\displaystyle(c,{\sf e})\rightslice b=c\rightslice_{\sf e}b,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}(c_{i},{\sf e}_{i})\rightslice b=c_{1}\rightslice_{{\sf e}_{1}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{N}(c_{i},{\sf e}_{i})\rightslice b\right)-\left(c_{1}\rightslice_{{\sf e}_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{N}(c_{i},{\sf e}_{i})\right)\rightslice b,

where
c,c_{1},\dots,c_{N},b\in W
and
{\sf e},{\sf e}_{1},\dots,{\sf e}_{N}\in{\sf E}
. (The last quantity is invariant under the permutations of
(c_{1},{\sf e}_{1}),\dots,(c_{N},{\sf e}_{N})
because of the multi-pre-Lie property of
W
, so the extension
\rightslice
is well-defined.)

The above extensions keep the multi-pre-Lie morphism property.
Indeed, if
\varphi:V\to W
is an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism, then defining the extension
\varphi:S(V^{\oplus{\sf E}})\to S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})
by
\varphi((c,{\sf e}))\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\varphi(c),{\sf e})
for any
(c,{\sf e})\in(V,{\sf E})
, and denoting by
\curvearrowright
the extension
\curvearrowright:S(V^{\oplus{\sf E}})\otimes V\to V
, one has

\varphi(X\curvearrowright b)=\varphi(X)\rightslice\varphi(b)

for any
X\in S(V^{\oplus{\sf E}})
and
b\in V
.

Since
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm e}=\{\mathcal{I}\}
, we identify
\sf E
with
\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
. The following formula can be proved by a similar argument to Lemma 2.6 of
[
40
]
by taking the ‘Taylor deformation’ map
\Theta
introduced in
[
20
]
(see Theorem 2.7 and Proposition 3.8 of therein) into account.

Lemma 54
.

For any
(\mathfrak{t},n)\in{\sf N}\cap\mathcal{B}
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{a}\in\mathcal{B}
, and
p_{1},\dots,p_{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
, one has

\pi_{U}\left(\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leq p_{1},\dots,q_{a}\leq p_{a}\\
q_{1}+\cdots+q_{a}\leq n\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|q_{1}|+\cdots+|q_{a}|}\binom{n}{q_{1},\dots,q_{a}}\prod_{i=1}^{a}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i},p_{i}-q_{i})\curvearrowright_{\flat}\mathfrak{b}^{n-q_{1}-\cdots-q_{a}},

where
\curvearrowright_{\flat}
denotes the extension
\curvearrowright_{\flat}:S(U^{\oplus{\sf E}})\otimes U\to U
, and
\binom{n}{q_{1},\dots,q_{a}}

is the multinomial coefficient

\binom{n}{q_{1},\dots,q_{a}}=\frac{n!}{q_{1}!\cdots q_{a}!(n-q_{1}-\cdots-q_{a})!}.

Then we can prove the uniqueness part of
(b)
immediately. Indeed, if
\varphi:U\to W
is an
\sf E
-multi-pre-Lie morphism, then it extends to a multi-pre-Lie morphism from
S(U^{\oplus{\sf E}})
to
S(W^{\oplus{\sf E}})
and satisfies

\varphi\left(\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leq p_{1},\dots,q_{a}\leq p_{a}\\
q_{1}+\cdots+q_{a}\leq n\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|q_{1}|+\cdots+|q_{a}|}\binom{n}{q_{1},\dots,q_{a}}\prod_{i=1}^{a}(\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}),p_{i}-q_{i})\rightslice\varphi(\mathfrak{b}^{n-q_{1}-\cdots-q_{a}})

for any
\mathfrak{b}^{n}\star\mathop{\vphantom{\sum}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{589827}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{655368}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{458758}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{327684}{}{$\bigstar$}}}}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{I}_{p_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i})\in\mathcal{B}
.
The right hand side provides the recursive definition of the map
\varphi
, so we can conclude that
\varphi
is determined by the values
\varphi(\mathfrak{b}^{n})
for any
\mathfrak{b}^{n}\in{\sf N}
. On the other hand, given
\{\varphi(\mathfrak{b}^{n})\}_{(\mathfrak{t},n)\in{\sf N}}
, we can prove that the map
\varphi
defined by the above formula satisfies indeed the multi-pre-Lie property. See Proposition 2.5 and Corollary 2.10 of
[
20
]
for details.
We do not provide the details here because only the uniqueness part of
(b)
is used in this paper, especially in Corollary
41
.

C
.
3   Proof of Lemma
46

C
.
3
.
1 Reduced coproducts

First we consider trees with
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{e}
-decorations, without
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration. Recall that
SC
is a set of strongly conforming trees and
C
is a set of conforming trees. Set

\displaystyle{{}^{\circ}}T\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}(SC),\qquad{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{\rm span}(C),\qquad{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{U}}^{-}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbb{R}[SC].

0
Definition –

We define the following splitting maps.

1.

The linear map
{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}:{{}^{\circ}}T\to{{}^{\circ}}T\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}
, is defined for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in SC
by

\begin{split}{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}})\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=&\sum_{\mu\in ST(\tau)}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}!}{{\mathfrak{n}}\choose{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}}}\,\mu^{\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}+\pi\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{\rm blue}}\mu\big)^{[\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}]_{\mu}}_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}},\end{split}

where
ST(\tau)
is the set of all
subtrees

\mu
of
\tau
which contain the root of
\tau
, and the second sum is over functions
\mathfrak{n}:N_{\mu}\to\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{\mu}\leq\mathfrak{n}
and functions
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\mu}:\partial\mu\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
. The algebra morphism

{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}^{+}:{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}\to{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}

is defined by the same formula for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in C
.

2.

The algebra morphism

{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}^{-}:{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{U}}^{-}\to{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{U}}^{-}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{U}}^{-}

is defined by
{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}{\bf 1}_{-}={\bf 1}_{-}\otimes{\bf 1}_{-}
, and for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}\in SC

\begin{split}{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}^{-}(\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}})&\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\varphi\in SF(\tau)}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}!}{{\mathfrak{n}}\choose{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}}}\varphi^{\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}+\pi\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{\rm red}}\varphi\big)^{[\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n_{\varphi}}]_{\varphi}}_{\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{\partial\varphi}},\end{split}

where
SF(\tau)
is the set of all
subforests

\varphi
of
\tau

which contain all red nodes of
\tau
, and the sum over
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\partial\varphi}^{\prime}
is taken as in item
\sf 1
.

3.

The algebra morphism

{{}^{\circ}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}:{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}\to{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{U}}^{\,-}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{T}}^{+}

is defined by the same formula as
{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}^{-}
, but the first sum is restricted to the set
\overline{SF}(\tau)
of all subforests
\varphi\in SF(\tau)

which is disjoint with the root of
\tau
.

Our aim is to show the coassociativities of
{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{\pm}
and
{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}
and
{{}^{\circ}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}
. To avoid a confusing calculation, we separate the coproducts into graph part and decoration part. Define simpler coproducts acting on undecorated trees by

\displaystyle{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\sigma\in ST(\tau)}\sigma\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma\big),\quad{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\varphi\in SF(\tau)}\varphi\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{red}}\varphi\big),\quad{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\tau\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\overline{SF}(\tau)}\varphi\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{red}}\varphi\big).

Given an undecorated tree
\tau
, denote by
\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
the map
adding

to the node
n\in N_{\tau}
the
\mathfrak{n}
-decoration
k\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
, and denote by
\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}
the map giving to the edge
e\in E_{\tau}
the
\mathfrak{e}
-decoration
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Then any decorated tree
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
is of the form

(C.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\tau=\mathbb{F}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{F}_{N}\tau,

where
\tau
is an undecorated tree, and
\mathbb{F}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{F}_{N}
are family of
\mathbb{X}
-type or
\mathbb{I}
-type operators, applying to pairwise different nodes or edges. Moreover, we define the coproducts of such operators by

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k}\binom{k}{k^{\prime}}\,\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k-k^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(e_{-},0)}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\ell^{\prime}!}\,\mathbb{X}_{(e_{-},\ell^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell+\ell^{\prime})},

where
e_{-}
denotes the node from where the edge
e
leaves. For the products of pairwise disjoint such operators, define

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{1})\dots(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{N}).

There are two remarks about this identity. First, the right hand side does not depend on the order of
\mathbb{F}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{F}_{N}
. To prove it, we have only to show that
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(e_{-},k)}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}=\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(e_{-},k)}
and
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,k)}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(f,\ell)}=\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(f,\ell)}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,k)}
for
e,f
such that
e_{-}=f_{-}
, because for other pairs of operators
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{G}
, the operators
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{G}
apply to different nodes or edges. These identities can be checked easily by definitions. Second, (
C.9
) holds even if there are distinct
i
and
j
such that
\mathbb{F}_{i}
and
\mathbb{F}_{j}
apply to the same
node
. Let
\mathbb{F}_{i}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
and
\mathbb{F}_{j}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)}
. Since
\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,k+\ell)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)})=\sum_{m\leq k+\ell}\binom{k+\ell}{m}\mathbb{X}_{(n,m)}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k+\ell-m)}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)})=\sum_{a\leq k,\,b\leq\ell}\binom{k}{a}\binom{\ell}{b}\mathbb{X}_{(n,a+b)}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k+\ell-a-b)}.

We can see that the right hand sides coincide by using Chu-Vandermonde identity
\sum_{a+b=m}\binom{k}{a}\binom{\ell}{b}=\binom{k+\ell}{m}
.
Therefore we have
(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)})=\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k+\ell)}=(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,\ell)})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)})
.

At this stage, we see that the coproducts
{{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}^{(\cdot,+,-)}

apply to the decorated tree (
C.8
) by the forms

(C.10)

\displaystyle{{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}^{(\cdot,+)}(\mathbb{F}\tau)=(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F})({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau),\quad{{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}^{-}(\mathbb{F}\tau)=(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F})({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\tau).

In the right hand side of (
C.10
), be careful that
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}
acts on subtrees and contracted trees.
For an
\mathbb{X}
-type operator, if
n\notin N_{\sigma}
then set
\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}\sigma={\bf 1}_{k=0}\sigma
. On a contracted tree
\tau/\sigma
, the
\mathbb{X}
-type operator acts of the form
\mathbb{X}_{([n],k)}
, where
[n]
denotes the equivalence class in the contraction
\tau\to\tau/\sigma
. Hence

\displaystyle(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)})(\sigma\otimes(\tau/\sigma))=\begin{cases}\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k}\binom{k}{k^{\prime}}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime})}\sigma\otimes\mathbb{X}_{([n],k-k^{\prime})}(\tau/\sigma),&n\in N_{\sigma},\\
\sigma\otimes\mathbb{X}_{([n],k)}(\tau/\sigma),&n\notin N_{\sigma}.\end{cases}

For an
\mathbb{I}
-type operator, if
e\notin E_{\sigma}
(resp.
e\notin E_{\tau/\sigma}
) then set
\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}\sigma=0
(resp.
\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}(\tau/\sigma)=0
). Combing with the definition of
\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)})(\sigma\otimes(\tau/\sigma))=\begin{cases}\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}\sigma\otimes(\tau/\sigma),&\textrm{for }e\in E_{\sigma},\\
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\ell^{\prime}!}\mathbb{X}_{(e_{-},\ell^{\prime})}\sigma\otimes\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell+\ell^{\prime})}(\tau/\sigma),&\textrm{for }e\in\partial\sigma,\\
\sigma\otimes\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}(\tau/\sigma),&\textrm{for }e\in E_{\tau}\setminus(E_{\sigma}\cup\partial\sigma).\end{cases}

These conventions show that the identities (
C.10
) hold.

C
.
3
.
2 Coassociativity

Lemma 55
.

One has the coassociativity formulas

\displaystyle\big({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D},

\displaystyle\big({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+},

\displaystyle\big({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-},\quad

\displaystyle\big({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){{}^{\circ}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\big){{}^{\circ}}\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{-}.

Proof –

We prove the identity

(C.11)

\displaystyle({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id}){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D};

the other identities are proved similarly. By the commutation relation (
C.10
), we have

\displaystyle\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}^{+}\big){{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}\,\mathbb{F}\tau=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}{\sf D}^{+}\big)(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F})({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau)=\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\big)\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau.

Hence it is sufficient for proving (
C.11
) to show
the two identities

(C.12)

\displaystyle\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+},

(C.13)

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}=(\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}.

It is not difficult to show (
C.12
) by the definition of
ST(\tau)
, by noting that

ST(\tau/\sigma)=\big\{\eta/\sigma\,;\,\sigma\subset\eta\subset\tau\big\}

and
(\tau/\sigma)/(\eta/\sigma)=\tau/\eta
. Next we show (
C.13
). By the multiplicativity (
C.9
), for any family
\mathbb{F}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{F}_{N}
of
\mathbb{X}
-type or
\mathbb{I}
-type operators, applying to pairwise different nodes or edges, one has

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\big((\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{1})\cdots(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{N})\big)

\displaystyle=\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{1})\big)\cdots\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{N})\big).

In the last equality, we use the fact that (
C.9
) holds even if multiple operators act on the same node.
If (
C.13
) holds for
\mathbb{F}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{F}_{N}
, then

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle=\big((\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{1})\big)\cdots\big((\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{N})\big)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\big((\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{1})\cdots(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}_{N})\big)=(\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}.

Therefore it is sufficient to show (
C.13
) for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
and
\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}
. For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
, we have

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k}\binom{k}{k^{\prime}}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k-k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k,\,k^{\prime\prime}\leq k-k^{\prime}}\binom{k}{k^{\prime}}\binom{k-k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime\prime}}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime};k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}\leq n}\binom{k}{k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k^{\prime\prime})}\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime})}.

We have the same expansion from
(\mathbb{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
, so (
C.13
) holds for
\mathbb{X}
-type operators.
We can prove the same result for
\mathbb{I}
-type operators by similar computations.

\rhd

Now we consider the extended decoration.

Lemma 56
.

One has the coassociativity formulas

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathsf{D}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{+}\big)\mathsf{D},

\displaystyle\big(\mathsf{D}^{+}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\mathsf{D}^{+}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{+}\big)\mathsf{D}^{+},

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathsf{D}^{-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{-}\big)\mathsf{D}^{-},\quad

\displaystyle\big(\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id}\big)\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}

\displaystyle=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\big)\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}.

Proof –

We consider the first and third identities; the other identities are proved similarly. In this proof, denote by
\bar{\tau}=\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
a generic decorated tree without
\mathfrak{o}
-decoration, and write
\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}
for
\tau_{\mathfrak{e}}^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}
. As in Section
2.2

and Section
5.1
, we use a shorthand notation

{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}\bar{\tau}=\sum_{\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\tau}}\bar{\sigma}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma}),\quad{{}^{\circ}}{\mathsf{D}}^{-}\bar{\tau}=\sum_{\bar{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau}}\bar{\varphi}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\varphi}).

Then we can write

{\mathsf{D}}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}=\sum_{\bar{\sigma}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma})^{\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}},\quad{\mathsf{D}}^{-}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}=\sum_{\bar{\varphi}}\bar{\varphi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi})}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi}):N_{\tau/\varphi}\to\mathbb{Z}[\beta_{0}]
, is a function giving the value
|\bar{\tau}_{j}|
, where
\bar{\tau}_{j}
is
a connected component if
\varphi
, to the node
[\tau_{j}]\in N_{\tau/\varphi}
. We obtain the coassociativity of
\mathsf{D}
from the coassociativity of
{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}
, noting that

(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma})/(\bar{\eta}/\bar{\sigma})=\bar{\tau}/\bar{\eta},\quad\mathfrak{o}|_{(\tau\setminus\sigma)\setminus(\eta\setminus\sigma)}=\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\eta}

for any
\bar{\eta}\leq\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\tau}
. To prove the coassociativity of
\mathsf{D}^{-}
, noting that

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\text{\rm Id})\mathsf{D}^{-}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}=\sum_{\bar{\psi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau}}\bar{\psi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes(\bar{\varphi}/\bar{\psi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi})}

and

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{-})\mathsf{D}^{-}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\psi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau}}\bar{\psi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{-}(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\psi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\psi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau}}\bar{\psi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes(\bar{\varphi}/\bar{\psi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})}\otimes(\bar{\tau}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi}/\bar{\psi})},

it is sufficient to show that
\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi})=\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi}/\bar{\psi})
as a function on
N_{\tau/\varphi}
. This holds true because
|\bar{\varphi}|^{\prime}=|\bar{\psi}|^{\prime}+|\bar{\varphi}/\bar{\psi}|^{\prime}
.

\rhd

C
.
3
.
3 Co-interaction

Lemma 57
.

One has the co-interaction formulas

(C.14)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}&=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-},\\
\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({{}^{\circ}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}&=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{+}\big){{}^{\circ}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-},\end{split}

and

(C.15)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\big)\mathsf{D}&=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D})\mathsf{D}^{-},\\
\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\big)\mathsf{D}^{+}&=(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D}^{+})\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}.\end{split}

Proof –

Consider the first identity of (
C.14
) and the first identity of (
C.15
); the two other identities are proved similarly. By the commutation relations (
C.10
), identity (
C.14
) rewrites

(C.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big((\mathbb{D}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\big)\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau=\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\big)(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}.

By the multiplicativity (
C.9
) of
\mathbb{D}
, it is sufficient to show (
C.16
) for the operators
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}
and
\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}
. By definition,

\displaystyle\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau=\sum_{\sigma\in ST(\tau)}\sum_{\varphi\in SF(\sigma),\psi\in\overline{SF}(\tau/\sigma)}\varphi\otimes(\sigma/^{\text{red}}\varphi)\otimes\psi\otimes(\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma)/^{\text{red}}\psi.

Note that
\varphi
and
\psi
are disjoint subforests of
\tau
because of the definition of
\overline{SF}
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big((\mathbb{D}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}\big)(\varphi\otimes(\sigma/^{\text{red}}\varphi)\otimes\psi\otimes(\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma)/^{\text{red}}\psi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\sum_{k=a+b+c+d}\frac{k!}{a!b!c!d!}\,\mathbb{X}_{(n,a)}\varphi\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,b)}(\sigma/^{\text{red}}\varphi)\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,c)}\psi\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,d)}\big((\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma)/^{\text{red}}\psi\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=a+b+d}\frac{k!}{a!b!d!}\,\mathbb{X}_{(n,a)}(\varphi\psi)\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,b)}(\sigma/^{\text{red}}\varphi)\otimes\mathbb{X}_{(n,d)}\big((\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma)/^{\text{red}}\psi\big)

\displaystyle=\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{X}_{(n,k)}\big)(\varphi\psi\otimes(\sigma/^{\text{red}}\varphi)\otimes\big(\tau/^{\text{blue}}\sigma)/^{\text{red}}\psi\big),

since either of
a
and
c
has to be
0
in the second line.
It is not difficult to show a similar equality for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{I}_{(e,\ell)}
.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big((\mathbb{D}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\big)\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau=\big((\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathbb{D})\mathbb{D}\mathbb{F}\big)\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau.

Since it is not difficult to show the co-interaction formula

\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big({{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-}\otimes{{}^{*}}\overline{\sf D}^{\,-}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\tau=\big(\text{\rm Id}\otimes{{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{+}\big){{}^{*}}{\sf D}^{-},

identity (
C.16
) follows as a consequence.

Next we consider (
C.15
). By definition,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(13)}\big(\mathsf{D}^{-}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{D}}^{\,-}\big)\mathsf{D}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}=\sum_{\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\tau},\bar{\varphi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\sigma},\bar{\psi}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma}}\bar{\varphi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\bar{\psi}^{\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}}\otimes(\bar{\sigma}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi})}\otimes\big((\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma})/\bar{\psi}\big)^{\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})}

and

\displaystyle(\text{\rm Id}\otimes\mathsf{D})\mathsf{D}^{-}\bar{\tau}^{\mathfrak{o}}=\sum_{\bar{\zeta}\trianglelefteq\bar{\tau},\bar{\eta}\leq\bar{\tau}/\bar{\zeta}}\bar{\zeta}^{\mathfrak{o}}\otimes\bar{\eta}^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\zeta})}\otimes\big((\bar{\tau}/\bar{\zeta})/\bar{\eta}\big)^{(\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\zeta}))|_{(\tau/\zeta)\setminus\eta}}.

The cointeraction between
{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}
and
{{}^{\circ}}\mathsf{D}^{-}
implies that the
change of variables

\bar{\zeta}\leftrightarrow\bar{\varphi}\,\bar{\psi},\quad\bar{\eta}\leftrightarrow\bar{\sigma}/\bar{\varphi}

is possible. Since
\sigma
and
\psi
are disjoint,

\displaystyle(\bar{\varphi}\bar{\psi})^{\mathfrak{o}}=\bar{\varphi}^{\mathfrak{o}}\,\bar{\psi}^{\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}},\quad(\bar{\sigma}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi}\bar{\psi})}=(\bar{\sigma}/\bar{\varphi})^{\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi})},

\displaystyle\big((\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma})/\bar{\psi}\big)^{(\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\varphi}\bar{\psi}))|_{(\tau/(\varphi\psi))\setminus(\sigma/\varphi)}}=\big((\bar{\tau}/\bar{\sigma})/\bar{\psi}\big)^{\mathfrak{o}|_{\tau\setminus\sigma}+\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\psi})}.

Thus (
C.15
) follows.

\rhd

Appendix D
Comments

Section
1
–
Regularity structures theory has its roots in T. Lyons’ theory of rough paths and rough differential equations
[
72
]
. This theory deals with controlled ordinary differential equations

dx_{t}=V(x_{t})dh_{t},

with controls
h
of low regularity, say
\alpha
-Hölder. For
\alpha>1/2
, Young integration theory allows to make sense of the equation as a fixed point problem for an integral equation. As one expects a solution path to be
\alpha
-Hölder, the product
V(x_{t})dh_{t}
makes sense as a distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
iff
\alpha+(\alpha-1)>0
, that is
\alpha>1/2
. One of Lyons’ deep insights was to realize that what really governs the dynamics is not the
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
-valued control
h
, say, but rather a finite
collection of its iterated integrals. The latter are ill-defined when
\alpha\leq 1/2
, and a rough path is the a priori datum of quantities playing their role. Natural algebraic and size constraints on these objects are then sufficient to set the entire theory. These constraints are similar
to the constraints that define the
\sf g
-part of a model. Several reformulations of rough paths theory were given after Lyons’ seminal work: Davie’s numerical scheme approach
[
32
]
, Gubinelli’s controlled paths
approach
[
47
,
48
]
, Friz & Victoir’s
limit ODE picture
[
43
]
, and Bailleul’s approximate flow-to-flow approach
[
2
]
, amongst others. Gubinelli’s versatile notion of controlled paths was a direct source of inspiration for the construction of regularity structures.

Other tools than regularity structures have been developed for the study of singular stochastic PDEs. None of them offers presently a complete alternative to regularity structures.

•

Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski laid in
[
51
]
the foundations of paracontrolled calculus, that was developed by Bailleul & Bernicot
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
. While the fundamental notions of regularity structures involve pointwise expansions, paracontrolled calculus uses paraproducts as a mean for making sense of what it means to look like a reference quantity. See
[
49
]
for lecture notes on the subject and
[
50
]
for an overview on the subject, both by Gubinelli & Perkowski.

In a nutshell, the starting point of the paracontrolled approach to the study of singular stochastic PDEs is the decomposition of a product of two distributions
f,g
into

fg=\big({\sf P}_{f}g+{\sf P}_{g}f\big)+{\sf\Pi}(f,g).

This decomposition is obtained in a Fourier picture of the product by splitting the convolution into what happens far from the diagonal
\big({\sf P}_{f}g+{\sf P}_{g}f\big)
from what happens near the diagonal
{\sf\Pi}(f,g)
. This decomposition isolates in the resonant term
{\sf\Pi}(f,g)

what does not make sense in a general product, the paraproduct terms
{\sf P}_{f}g,{\sf P}_{g}f
being always well-defined. The definition of
{\sf P}_{f}g
allows to think of it as a modulation of
g
by
f
and give meaning to what it means for a distribution/function
u
to look like another distribution/function
g

u={\sf P}_{u^{\prime}}g+u^{\sharp},

for a function
u^{\prime}
and a distribution/function
u^{\sharp}
that is more
regular than
g
. The role of modelled distributions is played in a paracontrolled setting by systems
(u_{a})_{a\in\mathscr{A}}
of paracontrolled distributions/functions

(D.1)

u_{a}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathscr{T};|a\tau|\leq n\alpha}{\sf P}_{u_{a\tau}}[\tau]+u_{a}^{\sharp}

indexed by the set
\mathscr{A}
of words over an alphabet
\mathscr{T}
, with remainders
u_{a}^{\sharp}
sufficiently regular. The reference distributions/functions
[\tau]
somehow play the role of
{\sf\Pi}\tau
and
the
(u_{a})_{a\in\mathscr{A}}
the role of the
(u_{\tau})_{\tau\in T,|\tau|<\gamma}
. (Bailleul & Hoshino’s work
[
8
,
9
]
on the relations between paracontrolled calculus and regularity structures make that link clear.) Identity (
D.1
) is an analogue of the notion of modelled distribution. While the definition of the latter involves pointwise comparisons, here the comparison is somehow done in ‘momentum space’, although not in a pointwise sense. The core point of the paracontrolled analysis of a (system of) singular stochastic PDE(s) is that we end up dealing with ill-defined terms of the form

(D.2)

{\sf\Pi}^{\prime}\big(u,\zeta^{(i)}\big)

for operators
{\sf\Pi}^{\prime}
that have similar properties as the resonant operator
\sf\Pi
, and possibly multi-dimensional functionals
\zeta^{(i)}
of the noise
\zeta
. It turns out that while an expression like (
D.2
) does not make sense for a generic
u
, it makes sense on a restricted class of
u
of the form (
D.1
)
provided
one can make sense of the terms
{\sf\Pi}^{\prime}\big(\tau,\zeta^{(i)}\big)
. The analysis of a given (system of) singular stochastic PDE(s) gives an inductive definition of the
\zeta^{(i)}
and the
\tau
’s. Compared to the regularity structures setting, the datum of all the
{\sf\Pi}^{\prime}\big(\tau,\zeta^{(i)}\big)
plays the role of the datum of a model. The inductive/tree structure of the elements of a regularity structure takes here the form of the inductive definition of the
\tau
’s and
\zeta^{(i)}
’s. A systematic treatment of renormalization operations within paracontrolled calculus has not been invented yet. The links between the regularity structure and paracontrolled settings detailed in Bailleul & Hoshino’s works
[
8
,
9
]
allow however to transport the renormalization machinery of regularity structures into the setting paracontrolled calculus. What is missing presently is an independent, purely paracontrolled, approach of the renormalization problem.

•

Otto & Weber
[
75
]
developed jointly with Sauer and Smith
[
76
]
a variant of regularity structures that is more in the flavour of rough paths theory. See in also their most recent joint works
[
70
,
71
]
with Linares, Tempelmayr and Tsatsoulis to see how far they were able to go. Most concepts and objects from regularity structures have counterparts in their setting. It was specifically designed and used for the analysis of a number of quasilinear singular stochastic PDEs. Some of these equations can be approached using the original first order paracontrolled calculus as in Bailleul, Debussche and Hofmanová’s work
[
7
]
or a variant of it using paracomposition operators, as in Furlan & Gubinelli’s work
[
45
]
. See also
[
12
,
46
]
for extensions of paracontrolled calculus and regularity structures designed for the study of a whole class of quasilinear singular stochastic PDEs.

•

Kupiainen & Marcozzi managed in
[
67
,
68
]
to implement a renormalization group approach to the (KPZ) and
\Phi^{4}_{3}
equations. The starting point of their strategy consists in decomposing the resolution operator
{\bf K}^{-1}
involved in the Picard formulation of the equation as a sum of operators
{\bf K}^{-1}_{n}
turning distributions into smooth functions that vary essentially only up to scale
2^{-n}
. The approximate renormalized dynamics will take the form

u_{N}=\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{N}{\bf K}^{-1}_{n}\bigg)\Big(F_{N}(u_{N},\partial u_{N}\,;\,\zeta)\Big)

in a simplified problem where the initial condition was taken to be null. The noise is left untouched, with no problem for defining the nonlinearity in the right hand side since
u_{N}
is smooth. So one has

u_{N}=u_{N}^{0}+\cdots+u_{N}^{N},

where each term
u_{N}^{n}
is morally varying only up to scale
2^{-n}
. The point is now to see that one can choose the nonlinearity
F_{N}
in such a way that each
u_{N}^{n}
is the solution of an equation of the form

u_{N}^{n}=\Big(\sum_{m=n}^{N}{\bf K}^{-1}_{n}\Big)\Big(F_{N}^{n}(u_{N}^{n},\partial u_{N}^{n}\,;\,\zeta)\Big)

for a nonlinearity
F_{N}^{n}
, and is converging in a proper space as
N
goes to infinity. This is done via the use of rescaling operators, taking profit from the exact scaling property of the heat kernel, by turning the problem of convergence of each
u_{N}^{n}
into the problem of the convergence of the family of rescaled versions of the functions
F_{N}^{n}
– taking profit of the fact that the former is a continuous function of the later. The overall convergence of
u_{N}
as
N
goes to
\infty
is somehow similar to the well-known fact that a sum of functions
\sum_{n}u^{n}
converges in an
\alpha
-Hölder space if
u^{n}
is localized in Fourier space on a
ball of size
2^{n}
and has uniform norm of order
2^{-\alpha n}
.

This approach was improved a lot by some recent works of P. Duch
[
33
,
34
,
35
]
, who traded the above discrete scale decomposition for a continuous scale decomposition and uncovered a certain structure on the cumulants of some functionals of the noise that pave the way to the development of a robust approach to some classes of singular equations. One can look at Chandra & Ferdinand’s work
[
24
]
for an application of this approach to the generalized (KPZ) equation.

Section
2
–
The functional setting adopted here draws inspiration from
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
and
[
75
]
. The main result of this section is the reconstruction theorem.

Several proofs of the reconstruction theorem are available now, in addition to Hairer’s original proof. Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski gave in
[
51
]
an alternative construction of the reconstruction map using a paraproduct-like operator. Singh & Teichmann showed in
[
77
]
how it can be understood as the continuous extension of an elementary reconstruction operator defined on a set of smooth modelled distributions. Otto and Weber have an analogue of the reconstruction map in their rough paths-like setting
[
75
,
76
]
. Caravenna & Zambotti’s recent work
[
22
]
provide a robust version of the reconstruction theorem in a setting free of any reference to regularity structures. The notion of coherent germ turns it into a particularly versatile tool. See
[
57
,
62
,
69
]
for versions of the reconstruction theorem in functional settings different from Hölder spaces. See also our previous work
[
8
]
for a paracontrolled representation of the reconstruction operator that refines over a similar flavoured representation given in Theorem 6.10 of
[
51
]
.

The reconstruction theorem takes its place in the history of a family of statements producing ‘transcendantal’ objects, i.e. objects constructed by limiting procedures, from families of objects satisfying constraints involving no limiting procedures. The one-step Euler scheme for solving ordinary differential equations characterizes for instance uniquely their flows under sufficient regularity conditions on the vector fields. In its simplest form, for the equation
\dot{y}=-y
, in
\mathbb{R}
, it yields the elementary identity
(1-t/n)^{n}\rightarrow e^{-t}
, as
n
goes to
\infty
.
It takes a more elaborate form in Hille’s approximation
\big((\textrm{Id}+\frac{t}{n}\,G)^{-1}\big)^{n}
of the semigroup
(e^{-tG})_{t>0}
generated by an unbounded operator
G
under well-known conditions. Chernov’s theorem
[
28
]
on families of strongly continuous perturbations of the identity used for constructing
(e^{-tG})_{t>0}
has a similar flavour. So is the
C^{1}
-approximate flow-to-flow machinery of
[
2
]
, that provides a far reaching generalization of Lyons’ extension theorem in rough paths theory and Gubinelli and Feyel & de la Pradelle’ sewing lemma
[
47
,
36
,
37
]
. All these statements characterize uniquely a transcendantal object as the unique object close to a family of objects satisfying a ‘
O(1)
condition’, involving no limiting procedure. The characterizing identity (
2.33
) for the reconstruction is of that form when the reconstruction operator
is unique. This kind of situation allows to build a calculus for the transcendantal objects from an elementary calculus on their generators.

The space of models over a given regularity structure is nonlinear. Bailleul & Hoshino showed in
[
8
,
9
]
how to parametrize this space by
a linear space using the tools of paracontrolled calculus. The set of
\bf K
-admissible models on a given regularity structure turns out in particular to be parametrized by the data for each
\tau
of negative homogeneity of a
|\tau|
-Hölder distribution, describing somehow the most regular part of the distribution
{\sf\Pi}\tau
. This has a number of consequences, such as an extension theorem similar to Lyons’ extension theorem in rough paths theory.

Proposition
9
, giving a definition of the image of a modelled distribution by a nonlinear map, has a counterpart in paracontrolled calculus, generalizing Bony’s paralinearisation formula to an arbitrary order – see Section 2 of Bailleul & Bernicot’s work
[
5
]
.

Section
3
–
The proof of the continuity result for
\mathcal{K}^{\sf M}
is an adaptation of the material from
Hairer’s groundbreaking work
[
53
]
to the functional setting adopted here. It is called by Hairer the multilevel Schauder estimates. The construction of
\bf K
-admissible models from Section
3.5
is adapted from Bailleul & Hoshino’s work
[
8
]
, which gives amongst others a parametrization of the set of all admissible models on any reasonable concrete regularity structure. See
[
9
]
for more results on the structure of the space of models and modelled distributions. Note that in the different components
u_{\tau}
of a modelled distribution also appear in the paracontrolled approach, in which they are involved in the global description of a possible solution, as opposed to their local meaning in the regularity structure setting.

Section
4
–
This section essentially follows the line of the corresponding results in
[
53
]
, Section 7 therein. Note that the setting presented here does not allow to take as initial condition a Dirac mass for instance. One needs for that purpose to set a Besov counterpart of the theory, as opposed to the Hölder flavoured version presented here. See Hairer & Labbé’s work
[
57
]
, Hensel & Rosati’s work
[
62
]
or Singh & Teichmann’s work
[
77
]
.

In a different direction, an number of works have been done on quasilinear singular stochastic PDEs
[
7
,
12
,
45
,
46
,
70
,
71
,
75
,
76
,
OSSW2
]
.

Section
5
–
The notion of renormalization structures and compatible regularity and renormalization structures introduced in this section is new. It encodes in a simple way the mechanics at work in Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti’s work
[
19
]
.

Section
6
–
This section contains the core insights of Bruned, Chandra, Chevyrev and Hairer’s work
[
16
]
, implemented here on the example of the generalized (KPZ) equation. The relevance of the notion of pre-Lie algebra was
first noticed in the work
[
17
]
of Bruned, Chevyrev and Friz on rough paths. The article
[
23
]
would provide a pre-history of the pre-Lie algebra. The comodule-bialgebra structure of the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra was first investigated in the work
[
21
]
of Calaque, Ebrahimi-Fard and Manchon; it played a key motivating role in the work of Bruned, Chevyrev and Friz. The description of the free pre-Lie algebra in this setting is due to Chapoton and Livernet
[
27
]
. The notion of multi-pre-Lie algebra was introduced in the work
[
16
]
of Bruned, Chandra, Chevyrev and
Hairer, where the free multi-pre-Lie was first described.

The
\mathfrak{o}
-decorations introduced here under the form of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}^{n,\alpha}
is forced by our construction of compatible regularity and renormalization structures for the generalized (KPZ) equation, given in Section
9
. It has no dynamical meaning. Bailleul & Bruned showed in
[
6
]
how to obtain the renormalized equation without using extended decorations for a large class of renormalization procedures including the BPHZ scheme. This work is based on the recursive renormalization scheme introduced by Bruned
[
15
]
.

The setting described here is robust enough to deal with equations driven by multiple noises, or systems of equations driven by multiple noises. We
take Funaki’s example
[
44
]
of the random motion of a rubber on a manifold as an archetype – see also
[
55
,
18
]
. The unknown
u
is a spacetime function with values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, solution of the system

(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x}^{2})u=\Gamma(u)\big(\partial_{x}u,\partial_{x}u\big)+\Sigma(u)\xi,

where
\Gamma(z)
is a symmetric matrix on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\Sigma(z)
a linear map from
\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\xi=(\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{k})
is an
k
-dimensional tuple of identically distributed independent one-dimensional spacetime white noises. We still have only one operator
(\partial_{t}-\partial_{x}^{2})
in this example, so the edge type set is here the same as in the study of the generalised (KPZ) equation.
The node set is changed from
\{\circ,\bullet\}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
to
\{\circ^{1},\dots,\circ^{k},\bullet\}\times\mathbb{N}^{d+1}
to account for the fact that we have
k
noises
\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{k}
in the system. Things get a bit messier if the system involves different operators, with different regularising properties, and noises with different regularities. The fundamental ideas involved in the analysis remain the same, while the notations
needed to take care of this richer setting become heavier. All this is explained in full details in
[
19
]
.

Section
7
–
This section gives what seems to us to be one of the two core results of
[
19
]
, Theorem
44
here. More general renormalization schemes were introduced by Bruned in
[
15
]
and Bailleul & Bruned have shown in
[
6
]
how to get back the renormalized equation in a very simple way in a setting with no extended decorations.

Section
8
–
The fact that the family of solutions to the generalized (KPZ) equation forms a finite dimensional manifold of some function space had not been noticed so far. More generally, this is true for any singular stochastic PDE that can be treated by the methods of regularity structures This short section emphasizes that fact.

Section
9
–
This section builds
on the fundamental work
[
19
]
, with a number of simplifications. The
notion of subcritical equation is subtle to check in the general case of a system of equations, as one needs to keep track of how a given symbol of a regularity structure ‘flows’ in the different pieces of a system, involving possibly operators with different regularizing properties. The meaning of subcriticality remains, though.
